 \node[n,label={[tag]above right:$p_4$}] (P4) at (1.2,-1.2) {$c$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]left:$p_1$}]  (A) at (-2.0,-2.4) {$a$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]right:$p_2$}] (F) at (-0.4,-2.4) {$4$};
 \draw (P5)--(P3); \draw (P5)--(P4); \draw (P3)--(A); \draw (P3)--(F);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{minipage}\hfill
\begin{minipage}[c]{0.50\textwidth}
\centering\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{label} & \textbf{left} & \textbf{right} \\ \midrule
$p_1$ & \textbf{id}  & $\to$ entry of $a$ & \\
$p_2$ & \textbf{num} & 4 & \\
$p_3$ & $-$          & $p_1$ & $p_2$ \\
$p_4$ & \textbf{id}  & $\to$ entry of $c$ & \\
$p_5$ & $+$          & $p_3$ & $p_4$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{minipage}
\caption{The finished syntax tree (left) and the records that represent it (right). An interior record stores the \emph{pointers} of its two children.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Three things to check.}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item The parse tree has 11 grammar nodes, but only 5 nodes are created: $3$ operands $+\ 2$ operators. In general an expression with $n$ operands and $n-1$ binary operators gives $2n-1$ nodes, which is a quick self-check.
\item $\mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},p_1,p_2)$ was called \emph{before} $\mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},p_3,p_4)$, because the pointer $p_3$ is an input of the second call. This is why $a-4$ is grouped first: the SDD together with the left-recursive grammar gives left associativity.
\item The first argument of $\mathit{Node}$ is always the pointer of the \emph{left} operand $E_1$, the second is that of $T$. Swapping them would build the mirror image of the tree.
\end{enumerate}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example 2: parentheses leave no trace in the tree, \texttt{a - (4 + c)}}
The same SDD is applied to $\mathbf{id}\ \tok{-}\ \tok{(}\ \mathbf{num}\ \tok{+}\ \mathbf{id}\ \tok{)}$. The new step is the rule $T\to(E)$: it is a copy rule, so the parentheses do not create any node.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Node visited (production)} & \textbf{Semantic rule executed} & \textbf{Result} \\ \midrule
1 & $T\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (a)   & $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},\ \mathit{entry}_a)$ & $p_1$ \\
2 & $E\to T$                  & copy & $p_1$ \\
3 & $T\to\mathbf{num}$ \ (4)  & $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{num},\ 4)$ & $p_2$ \\
4 & $E\to T$ \ (inner $E$)    & copy & $p_2$ \\
5 & $T\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (c)   & $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},\ \mathit{entry}_c)$ & $p_3$ \\
6 & $E\to E_1+T$              & $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},\ p_2,\ p_3)$ & $p_4$ \\
7 & $T\to(E)$                 & copy: $\at{T}{node}=\at{E}{node}$ & $p_4$ \\
8 & $E\to E_1-T$              & $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},\ p_1,\ p_4)$ & $p_5$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Compare the two trees in Figure~\ref{fig:sddtree-paren}. The input symbols are the same; only the \emph{grouping} differs, and the grouping is visible in the shape of the tree. This is why later phases never need to look at parentheses.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering

 % (a)

 \node[n,label={[tag]left:$p_3$}]  (P3) at (-1.0,-1.2) {$-$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]right:$p_4$}] (P4) at (1.0,-1.2) {$c$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]left:$p_1$}]  (A) at (-1.7,-2.4) {$a$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]right:$p_2$}] (F) at (-0.3,-2.4) {$4$};
 \draw (P5)--(P3); \draw (P5)--(P4); \draw (P3)--(A); \draw (P3)--(F);
 \node[font=\small] at (0,-3.4) {(a) \texttt{a - 4 + c}: \ $(a-4)+c$};
 % (b)
 \begin{scope}[xshift=7cm]
  \node[n,label={[tag]above right:$p_5$}] (Q5) at (0,0) {$-$};
  \node[n,label={[tag]left:$p_1$}]  (Qa) at (-1.0,-1.2) {$a$};
  \node[n,label={[tag]right:$p_4$}] (Q4) at (1.0,-1.2) {$+$};
  \node[n,label={[tag]left:$p_2$}]  (Qf) at (0.3,-2.4) {$4$};
  \node[n,label={[tag]right:$p_3$}] (Qc) at (1.7,-2.4) {$c$};
  \draw (Q5)--(Qa); \draw (Q5)--(Q4); \draw (Q4)--(Qf); \draw (Q4)--(Qc);
  \node[font=\small] at (0,-3.4) {(b) \texttt{a - (4 + c)}: \ $a-(4+c)$};

\caption{Parentheses change the shape of the syntax tree but never appear in it.}
\label{fig:sddtree-paren}

\end{examplebox}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Common mistakes when building the tree}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Creating a node for a copy rule.} $E\to T$ and $T\to(E)$ only pass a pointer upward. If your tree contains a node labelled $E$ or $T$, you have drawn the parse tree, not the syntax tree.
\item \textbf{Mixing up the children.} In $E\to E_1-T$ the left child is $\at{E_1}{node}$ and the right child is $\at{T}{node}$; for $a-4$ the order matters.
\item \textbf{Forgetting what post-order implies.} The first node created is the leftmost leaf, not the root; the root is visited, and its node created, last.
\end{itemize}
\end{pitfallbox}

\subsection{SDD for constructing DAGs directly}
\label{sec:sdddag}

\paragraph{The problem with the two-step method.} In Section~\ref{sec:vn} we first built the complete syntax tree and then walked over it in post-order to obtain the DAG. That works, but it builds a structure (the tree) that is thrown away immediately. An SDD lets us skip the tree: the parser creates DAG nodes \emph{while it parses}, and the tree never exists in memory.

\paragraph{The key observation.} The SDD of the previous subsection already has the right shape. Its rules for $E \to E_1 + T$ and $T \to T_1 * F$ create a node \emph{after} the nodes of the children are known, so they run in post-order, which is also the order used by the value-number method. All we change is \emph{what the constructor functions do}:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item In the tree SDD, $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(op, l, r)$ \emph{always} allocates a fresh node.
\item In the DAG SDD, $\mathit{Node}(op, l, r)$ first \emph{searches} for an existing record with the same $(op, l, r)$ and allocates a new one only if there is none. $\mathit{Leaf}$ behaves in the same way.
\end{itemize}
Both functions return a \textbf{value number}, so the attribute $\at{E}{node}$ is now simply \emph{an integer: the index of the DAG node} (instead of a pointer to a tree node). The attribute rules themselves are unchanged.

\paragraph{The SDD.} To have a multiplication operator we use the usual three-level expression grammar ($E$ expression, $T$ term, $F$ factor), and a single synthesized attribute \textit{node}:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rule} \\ \midrule
$E \to E_1 + T$ & $\at{E}{node} = \mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},\ \at{E_1}{node},\ \at{T}{node})$ \\
$E \to E_1 - T$ & $\at{E}{node} = \mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},\ \at{E_1}{node},\ \at{T}{node})$ \\
$E \to T$ & $\at{E}{node} = \at{T}{node}$ \\
$T \to T_1 * F$ & $\at{T}{node} = \mathit{Node}(\tok{'*'},\ \at{T_1}{node},\ \at{F}{node})$ \\
$T \to F$ & $\at{T}{node} = \at{F}{node}$ \\
$F \to (\,E\,)$ & $\at{F}{node} = \at{E}{node}$ \\
$F \to \mathbf{id}$ & $\at{F}{node} = \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{entry})$ \\
$F \to \mathbf{num}$ & $\at{F}{node} = \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{num},\ \at{\mathbf{num}}{val})$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The rules that only \emph{copy} a value ($E\to T$, $T\to F$, $F\to(E)$) create nothing: parentheses affect only the \emph{shape} of the DAG, they do not produce a node of their own. Only the rules containing $\mathit{Node}$ or $\mathit{Leaf}$ touch the array.

\paragraph{The two constructor functions.} The array of records and a hash table keyed on the \emph{signature} of a record are \emph{global data}, shared by all semantic rules for the whole input:
\begin{lstlisting}
record table[];                 // table[v] = record of the node with value number v
hash   index;                   // signature -> value number

int Leaf(kind, x) {             // kind is id or num; x is the entry or the value
    sig = (kind, x);
    if (index has sig) return index[sig];     // already exists: share it
    v = append(table, sig);                   // new record at the end
    index[sig] = v;
    return v;
}

int Node(op, l, r) {            // l and r are value numbers of the children
    sig = (op, l, r);
    if (index has sig) return index[sig];     // common sub-expression found
    v = append(table, sig);
    index[sig] = v;
    return v;
}
\end{lstlisting}
Compare with the \texttt{findOrCreate} routines of Section~\ref{sec:vn}: they are the same functions. The only new idea is \emph{who calls them}: the semantic rules of the grammar, not a tree-walking procedure.


\begin{notebox}{How does the SDD say ``tree'' or ``DAG''?}
The \emph{rules} cannot tell the difference: the DAG SDD above and the syntax-tree SDD of the previous subsection have the same productions, the same attribute \textit{node}, and the same dependency graph. Both are S-attributed. What differs is the \textbf{meaning of the constructor functions} that the rules call, and that is part of the definition of the SDD, in the same way that the meaning of \textit{gen} or \textit{addType} is:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(op,l,r)$ \emph{always allocates} a fresh node. Equal sub-expressions get different nodes, so the result is a \textbf{syntax tree}.
\item $\mathit{Node}(op,l,r)$ \emph{looks for a record with the same $(op,l,r)$ first} and allocates only if none exists. Equal sub-expressions get the same node, so the result is a \textbf{DAG}.
\end{itemize}
So whoever writes the SDD must state which of the two is meant. In these notes we use the keyword $\mathbf{new}$ for ``always create'' and plain $\mathit{Node}$, $\mathit{Leaf}$ for ``find or create''. Nothing else changes: every later phase simply receives the attribute \textit{node} and does not care whether some nodes are shared (except that shared nodes need to be translated only once).
\end{notebox}

\begin{notebox}{Why this is a legitimate SDD}
$\mathit{Node}$ and $\mathit{Leaf}$ have a \emph{side effect} (they change the global table), which Section~\ref{sec:sddwhat} allowed under one condition: the order of the calls must not change the result. Here it is fixed by the dependencies: a parent's $\mathit{Node}$ call needs the value numbers of its children, so those calls are made first. The SDD is S-attributed, so it can be evaluated by a post-order traversal of the parse tree, each rule being executed when its node is completed.
\end{notebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example: input $(a+b)*(a+b)$}
The table lists the rules in the order in which they run (post-order), but only those that call $\mathit{Leaf}$ or $\mathit{Node}$. The omitted rules ($T\to F$, $E\to T$, $F\to(E)$) just copy the value number upward.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Node completed} & \textbf{Call} & \textbf{Result} & \textbf{Value no.} \\ \midrule
1 & $F \to \mathbf{id}$ \ (first $a$) & $\mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},a)$ & new record & 1 \\
2 & $F \to \mathbf{id}$ \ (first $b$) & $\mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},b)$ & new record & 2 \\
3 & $E \to E_1 + T$ & $\mathit{Node}(+,1,2)$ & new record & 3 \\
4 & $F \to \mathbf{id}$ \ (second $a$) & $\mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},a)$ & \textbf{found} & 1 \\
5 & $F \to \mathbf{id}$ \ (second $b$) & $\mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},b)$ & \textbf{found} & 2 \\
6 & $E \to E_1 + T$ & $\mathit{Node}(+,1,2)$ & \textbf{found: shared} & 3 \\
7 & $T \to T_1 * F$ & $\mathit{Node}(*,3,3)$ & new record & 4 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The attribute of the root $E$ is $\at{E}{node}=4$: the root of a DAG with four records, $1\!:\!a$, $2\!:\!b$, $3\!:\!(+,1,2)$, $4\!:\!(*,3,3)$. At step 6 the second copy of $a+b$ is recognised as the first and no new node is made; this is the sharing we wanted. The tree version would have created seven nodes.
\end{examplebox}

For the larger expression $a + a*(b-c) + (b-c)*d$ the sequence of $\mathit{Leaf}$ and $\mathit{Node}$ calls is exactly the sequence of requests in Table~\ref{tab:vntrace}. This is no accident: the value-number method visits the tree in post-order, and the rules of the SDD run in post-order too. Both methods therefore build the \emph{same DAG with the same value numbers}, and the final $\at{E}{node}=9$ identifies its root.

\paragraph{Tree-first versus direct construction.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.0cm} L{5.6cm} L{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{Tree first, then DAG} (Section~\ref{sec:vn}) & \textbf{Directly with an SDD} \\ \midrule
Needs a syntax tree in memory & yes & no \\
Who calls find-or-create & a tree-walking procedure & the semantic rules, when a node is completed \\
Order of calls & post-order of the tree & post-order of the tree (the same) \\
Resulting DAG & identical & identical \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Two details.}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Commutative operators.} If $\mathit{Node}$ stores the children of $+$ and $*$ in a canonical order (smaller value number first), then $a+b$ and $b+a$ are shared as well; without this, they are treated as different expressions (Section~\ref{sec:vn}).
\item \textbf{Generating code at the same time.} Whenever $\mathit{Node}$ \emph{creates} a record, it can also emit one three-address instruction for it, with a fresh temporary; when it \emph{finds} an existing record, it emits nothing. This is method~2 in the comparison of Section~\ref{sec:tacdag} (the paragraph ``Three ways to obtain three-address code''), and it produces code in which $b-c$ is computed only once.
\end{itemize}

%====================================================================
\section{Syntax-Directed Translation Schemes (SDT)}
\label{sec:sdt}
%====================================================================

\subsection{From specification to implementation}
An SDD tells us \emph{what} every attribute must equal, but it does not say \emph{when} each rule should run. A real compiler, however, runs the rules \emph{while it is parsing}, in one definite order, and often without ever building the whole parse tree. A \textbf{syntax-directed translation scheme (SDT)} solves this: it takes the program fragments of the rules and writes them \emph{inside the production body, at the exact position where they should run}.

The same rule written both ways:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{SDD} (specification) & \textbf{SDT} (implementation) \\ \midrule
\begin{tabular}[t]{@{}l@{}}$E \to E_1 + T$\\ \ \ rule: $\at{E}{val} = \at{E_1}{val} + \at{T}{val}$\end{tabular}
&
$E \to E_1 + T\ \ \{\ \at{E}{val} = \at{E_1}{val} + \at{T}{val}\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.2cm} L{5.6cm} L{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{SDD} & \textbf{SDT} \\ \midrule
Nature & High-level specification: rules attached to productions & Concrete implementation: actions embedded in productions \\
Order of evaluation & Only constrained by the dependencies: any topological order is legal (post-order, left-to-right DFS, \dots{} depending on the class) & One explicit order: actions run in the left-to-right order of the production body, at the moment the parser reaches them \\
Rules/actions & Written next to the production & Written in braces $\{\dots\}$ inside the body \\
Used for & Designing and reasoning about the translation & Building the actual translator \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Definition and the meaning of an action's position}
\begin{defbox}{Syntax-Directed Translation Scheme}
A \textbf{syntax-directed translation scheme} (SDT) is a context-free grammar with \textbf{program fragments} (\emph{semantic actions}) embedded within the production bodies, written in braces. The position of an action defines \emph{when} it is executed during parsing.
\[
B \to X\ \{a\}\ Y
\]
Here action $a$ is executed after $X$ has been recognised (and before $Y$).
\end{defbox}

\paragraph{How an SDT is executed.} Imagine the parse tree in which every action appears as an \emph{extra child}, exactly at the position where it is written in the production. Perform a \textbf{depth-first traversal from left to right}; whenever the traversal reaches an action, it is executed. Figure~\ref{fig:actiontree} shows this for a scheme that translates infix to postfix: \texttt{expr $\to$ expr + term \{print('+')\}\ |\ expr - term \{print('-')\}\ |\ term} and \texttt{term $\to$ digit \{print(digit)\}}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\resizebox{0.95\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt},
  act/.style={inner sep=2pt,draw,dashed,rounded corners=2pt,fill=orange!15,font=\footnotesize}]
 \node[nd] (e0) at (0,0) {expr};
 \node[nd] (e1) at (-3.4,-1.4) {expr};
 \node[nd] (p1) at (0.2,-1.4) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (t2) at (2.8,-1.4) {term};
 \node[act] (a2) at (5.2,-1.4) {$\{\mathit{print}(\texttt{'+'})\}$};
 \node[nd] (e2) at (-5.4,-2.8) {expr};
 \node[nd] (m1) at (-3.6,-2.8) {$-$};
 \node[nd] (t5) at (-2.0,-2.8) {term};
 \node[act] (am) at (-0.3,-2.8) {$\{\mathit{print}(\texttt{'-'})\}$};
 \node[nd] (d2) at (2.2,-2.8) {2};
 \node[act] (ad2) at (3.9,-2.8) {$\{\mathit{print}(2)\}$};
 \node[nd] (t9) at (-5.4,-4.2) {term};
 \node[nd] (d5) at (-2.8,-4.2) {5};
 \node[act] (ad5) at (-1.0,-4.2) {$\{\mathit{print}(5)\}$};
 \node[nd] (d9) at (-6.3,-5.6) {9};
 \node[act] (ad9) at (-4.5,-5.6) {$\{\mathit{print}(9)\}$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {e0/e1,e0/p1,e0/t2,e0/a2,e1/e2,e1/m1,e1/t5,e1/am,e2/t9,t5/d5,t5/ad5,t2/d2,t2/ad2,t9/d9,t9/ad9}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \node[anchor=west] at (-1.5,-5.6) {Output (actions visited left to right): \ \texttt{9\ 5\ -\ 2\ +}};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Parse tree of \texttt{9-5+2} with actions (dashed boxes) as extra children. Visiting the tree depth-first, left to right, executes the actions in the order $9,\,5,\,-,\,2,\,+$, producing the postfix form.}
\label{fig:actiontree}
\end{figure}

In practice the parser does not need to build this tree. A top-down parser executes an action at the corresponding point of its recursive-descent procedure. An action at the right end of a body (a postfix SDT, next subsection) simply runs when the procedure of that production is about to return.

\subsection{Postfix translation schemes}
If the SDD is \textbf{S-attributed}, we can build an SDT with every action at the \emph{right end} of its production (a \textbf{postfix SDT}). Each action then runs when the node of its production is \emph{completed} in a post-order traversal of the parse tree, so the values of all the children are already available.

\begin{examplebox}{Postfix SDT for the desk calculator}
\begin{align*}
L &\to E\ \mathbf{n} &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\at{E}{val})\ \}\\
E &\to E_1 + T &&\{\ \at{E}{val} = \at{E_1}{val} + \at{T}{val}\ \}\\
E &\to T &&\{\ \at{E}{val} = \at{T}{val}\ \}\\
T &\to T_1 * F &&\{\ \at{T}{val} = \at{T_1}{val} \times \at{F}{val}\ \}\\
T &\to F &&\{\ \at{T}{val} = \at{F}{val}\ \}\\
F &\to (\,E\,) &&\{\ \at{F}{val} = \at{E}{val}\ \}\\
F &\to \mathbf{digit} &&\{\ \at{F}{val} = \at{\mathbf{digit}}{lexval}\ \}
\end{align*}
\end{examplebox}

\paragraph{Evaluation in post-order.} Each action runs when the node of its production is completed, that is, after all its children (which already hold their values) have been completed. For the input \texttt{3 * 5 + 4 n} the nodes of the parse tree are completed in this order:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Node completed (production)} & \textbf{Action} & \textbf{Value} \\ \midrule
1 & $F\to\mathbf{digit}$ \ (3) & $F.val=3$ & 3 \\
2 & $T\to F$ & $T.val=F.val$ & 3 \\
3 & $F\to\mathbf{digit}$ \ (5) & $F.val=5$ & 5 \\
4 & $T\to T_1*F$ & $T.val=3\times5$ & 15 \\
5 & $E\to T$ & $E.val=T.val$ & 15 \\
6 & $F\to\mathbf{digit}$ \ (4) & $F.val=4$ & 4 \\
7 & $T\to F$ & $T.val=F.val$ & 4 \\
8 & $E\to E_1+T$ & $E.val=15+4$ & 19 \\
9 & $L\to E\,\mathbf{n}$ & $\mathit{print}(E.val)$ & prints 19 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsection{Worked example: infix to postfix, written out completely}
\label{sec:infix2postfix}

\paragraph{The task.} In \textbf{infix} notation the operator stands between its operands (\texttt{a + b}); in \textbf{postfix} notation it stands \emph{after} them (\texttt{a b +}). Postfix needs no parentheses and no precedence rules: \texttt{a*(b+c)-d} becomes \texttt{a b c + * d -}, and a stack machine can execute it by pushing operands and letting each operator pop its two arguments. We translate with the usual three-level expression grammar, which already encodes precedence ($*,/$ bind tighter than $+,-$) and left associativity.

\paragraph{Step 1: the SDD.} Let every nonterminal have a string attribute $t$, the postfix form of the part of the input it derives. The operator is appended \emph{after} the strings of its two operands ($\cc$ means concatenation):
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rule} \\ \midrule
$E \to E_1 + T$ & $\at{E}{t} = \at{E_1}{t}\cc\at{T}{t}\cc\tok{'+'}$ \\
$E \to E_1 - T$ & $\at{E}{t} = \at{E_1}{t}\cc\at{T}{t}\cc\tok{'-'}$ \\
$E \to T$       & $\at{E}{t} = \at{T}{t}$ \\
$T \to T_1 * F$ & $\at{T}{t} = \at{T_1}{t}\cc\at{F}{t}\cc\tok{'*'}$ \\
$T \to T_1 / F$ & $\at{T}{t} = \at{T_1}{t}\cc\at{F}{t}\cc\tok{'/'}$ \\
$T \to F$       & $\at{T}{t} = \at{F}{t}$ \\
$F \to (\,E\,)$ & $\at{F}{t} = \at{E}{t}$ \\
$F \to \mathbf{id}$  & $\at{F}{t} = \at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme}$ \\
$F \to \mathbf{num}$ & $\at{F}{t} = \at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Every rule is S-attributed, so the SDD can be evaluated by a post-order traversal of the parse tree. Notice the special form of the rules: the string of the head is made of the strings of the body nonterminals \emph{in the same order}, with extra characters (the operators) inserted in between or appended. For such ``simple'' SDDs we do not have to build the long strings at all: we may print each piece \emph{at the moment it would have been appended}. That gives the SDT.

\paragraph{Step 2: the SDT.} Each rule is replaced by a $\mathit{print}$ action at the right end of the body. Since all actions are at the right end, this is a \textbf{postfix SDT}: each action runs when the node of its production is completed (post-order). All productions are listed, including those that have no action.
\begin{align*}
E &\to E_1 + T &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\ \}\\
E &\to E_1 - T &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'-'})\ \}\\
E &\to T\\
T &\to T_1 * F &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'*'})\ \}\\
T &\to T_1 / F &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'/'})\ \}\\
T &\to F\\
F &\to (\,E\,)\\
F &\to \mathbf{id}  &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\ \}\\
F &\to \mathbf{num} &&\{\ \mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval})\ \}
\end{align*}
The rules $E\to T$, $T\to F$ and $F\to(E)$ print nothing: they only pass the work on. Operands are printed by the leaf rules, operators by the operator rules, and the position of the action explains the order: when the node of $E\to E_1+T$ is completed, $E_1$ and $T$ have already been completed, so their output has already appeared and the $+$ comes after both.

\begin{examplebox}{Trace 1: \texttt{9-5+2} in post-order}
The nodes of the parse tree are completed in the order below; read the last column to see the output grow. Only three kinds of rule print, and the copy rules ($T\to F$, $E\to T$) print nothing.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Node completed (production)} & \textbf{Action} & \textbf{Output so far} \\ \midrule
1 & $F\to\mathbf{num}$ \ (9) & \textbf{print 9} & \texttt{9} \\
2 & $T\to F$ & --- & \texttt{9} \\
3 & $E\to T$ & --- & \texttt{9} \\
4 & $F\to\mathbf{num}$ \ (5) & \textbf{print 5} & \texttt{95} \\
5 & $T\to F$ & --- & \texttt{95} \\
6 & $E\to E_1-T$ & \textbf{print -} & \texttt{95-} \\
7 & $F\to\mathbf{num}$ \ (2) & \textbf{print 2} & \texttt{95-2} \\
8 & $T\to F$ & --- & \texttt{95-2} \\
9 & $E\to E_1+T$ & \textbf{print +} & \texttt{95-2+} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Step 6 is the important one: the node $E\to E_1-T$ (the subtraction $9-5$) is completed \emph{before} the \texttt{+} is handled, so the minus is printed first. That is left associativity: $(9-5)+2$, not $9-(5+2)$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Trace 2: \texttt{a*(b+c)-d}, with parentheses and precedence}
Only the nodes that print something are listed; the copy rules ($T\to F$, $E\to T$, $F\to(E)$) print nothing.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Node completed that prints} & \textbf{Prints} & \textbf{Output so far} \\ \midrule
1 & $F\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (a)            & \texttt{a} & \texttt{a} \\
2 & $F\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (b)            & \texttt{b} & \texttt{ab} \\
3 & $F\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (c)            & \texttt{c} & \texttt{abc} \\
4 & $E\to E_1+T$ \ ($b+c$)             & \texttt{+} & \texttt{abc+} \\
5 & $T\to T_1*F$ \ ($a*(b+c)$)         & \texttt{*} & \texttt{abc+*} \\
6 & $F\to\mathbf{id}$ \ (d)            & \texttt{d} & \texttt{abc+*d} \\
7 & $E\to E_1-T$ \ (whole expression)  & \texttt{-} & \texttt{abc+*d-} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The node $F\to(E)$ is completed between rows 4 and 5, but it prints nothing: the parentheses have done their job once $b+c$ was completed as a unit, before the multiplication. The output is the \textbf{post-order traversal of the syntax tree}: Figure~\ref{fig:postfixtree} numbers the nodes in the order in which they are finished, and these are exactly the rows of the table.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  n/.style={circle,draw,minimum size=7mm,inner sep=0pt,font=\small},
  tag/.style={font=\scriptsize\bfseries,text=red!70!black}]
 \node[n,label={[tag]above right:7}] (r) at (0,0) {$-$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]above left:5}]  (m) at (-1.5,-1.2) {$*$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]above right:6}] (d) at (1.5,-1.2) {$d$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]left:1}]        (a) at (-2.5,-2.4) {$a$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]above right:4}] (p) at (-0.5,-2.4) {$+$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]left:2}]        (b) at (-1.3,-3.6) {$b$};
 \node[n,label={[tag]right:3}]       (c) at (0.3,-3.6) {$c$};
 \draw (r)--(m); \draw (r)--(d); \draw (m)--(a); \draw (m)--(p); \draw (p)--(b); \draw (p)--(c);
 \node[anchor=west,align=left,font=\small] at (2.6,-1.8) {Visit order (red): $a,\ b,\ c,\ +,\ *,\ d,\ -$\\[3pt] Output: \ \texttt{a\ b\ c\ +\ *\ d\ -}};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Syntax tree of $a*(b+c)-d$. A node prints itself when its subtree is finished; this is the post-order traversal of Section~\ref{sec:vn}, so the SDT output is the postfix form.}
\label{fig:postfixtree}
\end{figure}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{notebox}{The position of the action decides the output}
Take $E\to E_1 + T$. With the action at the \emph{end}, after $T$, the operator is printed after both operands: \textbf{postfix}. If the action is moved into the \emph{middle}, between $E_1$ and $T$ (written $E\to E_1\ \{\mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\}\ +\ T$), the operator is printed between its operands, and the same traversal gives back the \textbf{infix} order (without the parentheses). The grammar did not change; only the place of the action did. This is the whole point of an SDT: \emph{where} the action stands defines \emph{when} it runs.
\end{notebox}

\subsection{SDTs with actions inside productions}
When an action is in the middle of a production, it is performed \emph{immediately after all grammar symbols to its left have been matched}. A top-down parser does exactly that: the action is just a statement placed at that point of the recursive-descent procedure.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{4.2cm} L{9.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Where the action is} & \textbf{In a top-down parser (recursive descent)} \\ \midrule
At the right end & run after the last symbol of the body is matched, just before the procedure returns \\
In the middle & run at that point of the procedure, right after the symbols to its left \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}{Complete example: infix to postfix for a \emph{top-down} parser}
The postfix SDT of Section~\ref{sec:infix2postfix} uses left-recursive productions ($E\to E_1+T$), which a top-down parser cannot handle. To run the same translation in a recursive-descent parser, we remove the left recursion from the grammar \emph{and keep the actions where they belong}: each print action stays right after the operand it follows. (Why this rewriting works is explained step by step in Section~\ref{sec:leftrec}.)

\textbf{The simplest version: digits with $+$ and $-$.}
\begin{align*}
E &\to T\ R\\
R &\to +\ T\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\ \}\ R_1 \ \mid\ -\ T\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'-'})\ \}\ R_1 \ \mid\ \varepsilon\\
T &\to \mathbf{num}\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval})\ \}
\end{align*}
Here $R$ stands for ``the rest of the expression'': zero or more groups of an operator followed by an operand. The action of $R\to+\,T\,\{\dots\}\,R_1$ comes \emph{after $T$ and before $R_1$}: the operand is printed first, then the operator, and only then the rest of the expression is handled. The production $R\to\varepsilon$ has no action: there is nothing left to print.

\textbf{Execution on \texttt{9-5+2}.} Figure~\ref{fig:topdownpost} shows the parse tree with each action as an extra child (dashed box). A depth-first, left-to-right visit executes the actions in the order of the bold numbers.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center},
  act/.style={inner sep=2.5pt,draw,dashed,rounded corners=2pt,fill=orange!15,font=\footnotesize,align=left}]
 \node[nd] (E)  at (0,0) {$E$};
 \node[nd] (T9) at (-3.4,-1.5) {$T$};
 \node[nd] (R1) at (2.0,-1.5) {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n9) at (-4.2,-3.0) {\textbf{9}};
 \node[act] (a9) at (-2.3,-3.0) {\textbf{1:} $\mathit{print}(9)$};
 \node[nd] (m1) at (-0.4,-3.0) {$-$};
 \node[nd] (T5) at (1.3,-3.0) {$T$};
 \node[act] (am) at (3.7,-3.0) {\textbf{3:} $\mathit{print}(\tok{'-'})$};
 \node[nd] (R2) at (6.1,-3.0) {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n5) at (0.5,-4.5) {\textbf{5}};
 \node[act] (a5) at (2.1,-4.5) {\textbf{2:} $\mathit{print}(5)$};
 \node[nd] (p1) at (4.6,-4.5) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (T2) at (6.0,-4.5) {$T$};
 \node[act] (ap) at (8.4,-4.5) {\textbf{5:} $\mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})$};
 \node[nd] (R3) at (10.6,-4.5) {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n2) at (5.2,-6.0) {\textbf{2}};
 \node[act] (a2) at (6.9,-6.0) {\textbf{4:} $\mathit{print}(2)$};
 \node[nd] (ep) at (10.6,-6.0) {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {E/T9,E/R1,T9/n9,T9/a9,R1/m1,R1/T5,R1/am,R1/R2,T5/n5,T5/a5,R2/p1,R2/T2,R2/ap,R2/R3,T2/n2,T2/a2,R3/ep}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (-5.2,-6.0) {Output, in the order of the bold numbers:\\[2pt] \texttt{9}\ \ \texttt{5}\ \ \texttt{-}\ \ \texttt{2}\ \ \texttt{+} \ $\Rightarrow$ \ \texttt{95-2+}};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Parse tree of \texttt{9-5+2} for the right-recursive scheme, with the actions as extra children. The tree leans to the right, but the actions print the operands and operators in the correct postfix order.}
\label{fig:topdownpost}
\end{figure}

\textbf{As a recursive-descent parser.} A top-down parser has one procedure per nonterminal, and every action is simply a statement written at its position in the production. Here \texttt{look} is the current input symbol and \texttt{match(c)} consumes it.
\begin{lstlisting}
void E() { T(); R(); }

void R() {
    if (look == '+') { match('+'); T(); print('+'); R(); }
    else if (look == '-') { match('-'); T(); print('-'); R(); }
    else { /* R -> epsilon: do nothing */ }
}

void T() {
    if (isdigit(look)) { c = look; match(look); print(c); }
    else error();
}
\end{lstlisting}
Compare \texttt{R()} with the production: \texttt{match('+'); T(); print('+'); R();} is the body $+\ T\ \{\mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\}\ R_1$ read from left to right.

\textbf{The complete scheme with $*$, $/$, parentheses and identifiers.} The same trick is repeated for the second precedence level. $R$ is the rest of an expression ($+,-$) and $Q$ is the rest of a term ($*,/$).
\begin{align*}
E &\to T\ R\\
R &\to +\ T\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\ \}\ R_1 \ \mid\ -\ T\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'-'})\ \}\ R_1 \ \mid\ \varepsilon\\
T &\to F\ Q\\
Q &\to *\ F\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'*'})\ \}\ Q_1 \ \mid\ /\ F\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\tok{'/'})\ \}\ Q_1 \ \mid\ \varepsilon\\
F &\to (\,E\,) \ \mid\ \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\ \}\ \mid\ \mathbf{num}\ \{\ \mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval})\ \}
\end{align*}
For \texttt{a*(b+c)-d} this scheme prints \texttt{abc+*d-}, the same output as the left-recursive scheme of Section~\ref{sec:infix2postfix}: $F$ prints \texttt{a}; $Q$ matches $*$, calls $F$, which parses $(b+c)$ and prints \texttt{bc+} inside; only then does $Q$ print \texttt{*}; finally $R$ matches $-$, $T$ prints \texttt{d}, and $R$ prints \texttt{-}.
\end{examplebox}


\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: actions in the middle of productions (declarations)}
We want to give every identifier in a declaration such as \texttt{float x, y} its type. The type is known only after $T$ has been parsed, and the identifiers must receive it \emph{as they are read}. This is why the actions cannot all stand at the end of a production.
\begin{align*}
D &\to T\ \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \at{L}{inh}=\at{T}{type};\ \mathit{addType}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{entry},\ \at{T}{type})\ \}\ L\\
T &\to \mathbf{int}\ \{\ \at{T}{type}=integer\ \}\ \mid\ \mathbf{float}\ \{\ \at{T}{type}=float\ \}\\
L &\to \tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \mathit{addType}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{entry},\ \at{L}{inh});\ \at{L_1}{inh}=\at{L}{inh}\ \}\ L_1\ \mid\ \varepsilon
\end{align*}
The actions of $T$ are at the right end of their bodies, but the actions in $D$ and in $L$ are in the \emph{middle}: each is followed by the nonterminal ($L$ or $L_1$) that needs the inherited attribute it computes.

\textbf{Solution for \texttt{float x, y}.} Figure~\ref{fig:midaction} shows the parse tree with each action as an extra child. A depth-first traversal from left to right executes the actions in the order of the bold numbers.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.98\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center},
  act/.style={inner sep=2.5pt,draw,dashed,rounded corners=2pt,fill=orange!15,font=\footnotesize,align=left}]
 \node[nd] (D) at (0,0) {$D$};
 \node[nd] (T) at (-5.4,-1.7) {$T$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $type=float$}};
 \node[nd] (ix) at (-2.7,-1.7) {\textbf{id}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize (x)}};
 \node[act] (a2) at (0.4,-1.7) {\textbf{2:} $L.inh=T.type$;\\ \phantom{\textbf{2:}} $addType(x,float)$};
 \node[nd] (L) at (5.2,-1.7) {$L$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $inh=float$}};
 \node[nd] (fl) at (-6.5,-3.4) {\textbf{float}};
 \node[act] (a1) at (-4.2,-3.4) {\textbf{1:} $T.type=float$};
 \node[nd] (cm) at (2.9,-3.4) {$,$};
 \node[nd] (iy) at (4.1,-3.4) {\textbf{id}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize (y)}};
 \node[act] (a3) at (7.2,-3.4) {\textbf{3:} $addType(y,float)$;\\ \phantom{\textbf{3:}} $L_1.inh=L.inh$};
 \node[nd] (L1) at (10.6,-3.4) {$L_1$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $inh=float$}};
 \node[nd] (ep) at (10.6,-4.9) {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {D/T,D/ix,D/a2,D/L,T/fl,T/a1,L/cm,L/iy,L/a3,L/L1,L1/ep}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Parse tree of \texttt{float x, y} with the actions as extra children (dashed boxes). The bold numbers give the execution order in a left-to-right depth-first traversal.}
\label{fig:midaction}
\end{figure}

\begin{enumerate}[nosep,label=(\arabic*)]
\item Reaching the end of $T\to\mathbf{float}$: $\at{T}{type}=float$.
\item Back in $D$, after $T$ and \textbf{id} (x) have been visited: $\at{L}{inh}=float$ and \textit{addType}$(x, float)$.
\item In $L$, after \texttt{,} and \textbf{id} (y): \textit{addType}$(y, float)$ and $\at{L_1}{inh}=float$. The remaining $L_1\to\varepsilon$ has no action.
\end{enumerate}
Result: the symbol table says $x:float$ and $y:float$.

\textbf{Why the position matters.} If the action of $D$ were written at the \emph{end}, after $L$, then $L$ would be processed before $\at{L}{inh}$ has a value, and \textit{addType}$(y,\dots)$ would use an undefined type. A mid-production action is therefore the way an SDT guarantees that an inherited attribute is computed \emph{before} the symbol that uses it. A top-down parser executes these actions as statements of its recursive-descent procedures.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example: actions at the left end of a production body}
An action may be the very first item of a body. (An action cannot be attached to the head of a production; ``left'' here means the left end of the body.) Such an action runs \emph{before any symbol of the body is matched}, so it can use only the \emph{inherited} attributes of the head. The scheme below prints the nesting depth of every opening parenthesis of a balanced string; $P.d$ is an inherited attribute holding the depth of the current position.
\begin{align*}
S &\to \{\ \at{P}{d}=0\ \}\ P\\
P &\to \{\ \mathit{print}(\at{P}{d})\ \}\ \tok{'('}\ \{\ \at{P_1}{d}=\at{P}{d}+1\ \}\ P_1\ \tok{')'}\ \{\ \at{P_2}{d}=\at{P}{d}\ \}\ P_2\ \ \mid\ \ \varepsilon
\end{align*}
Here the first action of $S$ \emph{initialises} the inherited attribute of $P$, and the first action of $P$ \emph{reads} $\at{P}{d}$, which was set by an action before $P$ was entered. For the input \texttt{(()())} the output is \texttt{0 1 1}.
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Eliminating left recursion from an SDT}
\label{sec:leftrec}

We go in three small steps: (1) why a top-down parser needs a different grammar, (2) the change of the grammar alone, and (3) how the actions must change with it. The running example is the grammar for sums and differences, $E\to E_1+T\mid E_1-T\mid T$.

\begin{defbox}{Two forms of the same question: SDD or SDT}
An exam may ask you to remove left recursion from an \textbf{SDD} or from an \textbf{SDT}. The work is the same; only the way the semantic rules are written differs.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Rewrite the grammar} (Step 2 below). This is identical in both forms.
\item \textbf{Rewrite the attribute rules.} The new nonterminal $R$ gets an inherited attribute $R.i$ (the result so far) and a synthesized attribute $R.s$ (the final result). The base case initialises $R.i$; each repeated group updates it and passes it to $R_1.i$; $R\to\varepsilon$ copies $R.i$ into $R.s$; the head copies $R.s$ upward. The outcome is an \textbf{SDD}.
\item \textbf{Place the rules inside the productions} (SDT only). Each rule becomes an action in braces: an action that computes an inherited attribute of a symbol goes \emph{immediately before} that symbol, and an action that computes a synthesized attribute of the head goes at the \emph{end} of the body (Section~\ref{sec:lattr}). The outcome is an \textbf{SDT}.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{4.6cm} p{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{Answer as an SDD} & \textbf{Answer as an SDT} \\ \midrule
Semantic rules are written & as equations next to each production & as $\{\dots\}$ actions inside the body \\
Does position matter? & no & yes: inherited attribute before the symbol, synthesized attribute of the head at the end \\
Kind of SDD & L-attributed ($R.i$ is inherited) & the same L-attributed SDD, with its rules placed in the bodies \\
Evaluated by & depth-first, left-to-right traversal & the parser, as it parses (e.g.\ recursive descent) \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Checklist for a full-marks answer:} (a) the new grammar without left recursion; (b) the attributes of the new nonterminal, stating which is inherited and which synthesized; (c) the base-case rule that initialises $R.i$; (d) the update rule for $R_1.i$; (e) the rule $R.s=R.i$ for $\varepsilon$; (f) the rule that copies $R.s$ to the head; (g) for an SDT only, the actions in the correct positions; (h) if an input is given, a trace of $R.i$ and $R.s$.
\end{defbox}

\begin{notebox}{Which SDDs does this cover? (scope)}
The method is the one of the Dragon Book (Section 5.4.4). It removes left recursion from an SDD or SDT in which the left-recursive rules \emph{compute a synthesized attribute of the head from the attributes of the body}: $A\to A_1\,Y\ \{A.a=g(A_1.a,\,Y.y)\}$ and $A\to X\ \{A.a=f(X.x)\}$. In other words, the \emph{given} SDD is S-attributed (or the SDT only prints, as in infix to postfix). The \emph{result} is L-attributed, because the new attribute $R.i$ is inherited. The notes follow the book and do not go beyond this case.
\end{notebox}

\paragraph{Step 1: why bother?} A top-down (recursive-descent) parser has one procedure per nonterminal, and it chooses a production by looking at the next input token. For a left-recursive production the procedure would have to start by calling \emph{itself}:
\begin{lstlisting}
void E() {            // for  E -> E + T
    E();              // calls E() again before reading any token ...
    match('+'); T();  // ... so this line is never reached: infinite recursion
}
\end{lstlisting}
No input is consumed between the two calls, so the parser loops forever. A grammar is \textbf{left-recursive} when a nonterminal can derive a string that starts with itself, as in $A\to A\,\alpha$. A top-down parser needs such a rule rewritten, which is what the rest of this section does.

\paragraph{Step 2: change the grammar (no actions yet).} What does $E\to E+T\mid T$ generate? Just a $T$ followed by any number of ``$+\,T$'' groups: $T$, \ $T+T$, \ $T+T+T$, \dots{} So we can write the same language directly, left to right:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Left-recursive} & \textbf{Right-recursive (no left recursion)} \\ \midrule
$E \to E + T \mid T$ & $E \to T\ R$ \\
 & $R \to +\ T\ R \mid \varepsilon$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$R$ means ``the rest'': zero or more groups of $+\,T$. Both grammars derive the same string $T+T+T$; only the \emph{order of growth} differs:
\begin{align*}
\text{left-recursive:}\quad & E \Rightarrow E+T \Rightarrow E+T+T \Rightarrow T+T+T\\
\text{right-recursive:}\quad & E \Rightarrow T\,R \Rightarrow T+T\,R \Rightarrow T+T+T\,R \Rightarrow T+T+T
\end{align*}
In the first derivation the string grows to the left (the first $T$ is produced last); in the second it grows from left to right, the way we read it.

\begin{defbox}{Recipe: removing immediate left recursion (grammar only)}
\[
A \to A\,\alpha \mid \beta
\qquad\text{becomes}\qquad
A \to \beta\,R, \qquad R \to \alpha\,R \mid \varepsilon .
\]
$\beta$ is what $A$ can start with, and $\alpha$ is what may be repeated after it. With several alternatives, $E\to E+T\mid E-T\mid T$ gives $E\to T\,R$, \ $R\to +\,T\,R\mid -\,T\,R\mid\varepsilon$.
\end{defbox}

\paragraph{Step 3: why the actions must change, too.} The new grammar gives the \emph{same strings} but a \emph{different parse tree}. Figure~\ref{fig:leftrectrees} compares the two trees for \texttt{9-5+2}.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=1.5pt}]
 % (a) left-recursive
 \node[nd] (Ea) at (0,0) {$E$};
 \node[nd] (Eb) at (-1.9,-1.2) {$E$};
 \node[nd] (pl) at (0,-1.2) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (Tc) at (1.3,-1.2) {$T$};
 \node[nd] (Ec) at (-3.3,-2.4) {$E$};
 \node[nd] (mi) at (-1.9,-2.4) {$-$};
 \node[nd] (Tb) at (-0.7,-2.4) {$T$};
 \node[nd] (n2) at (1.3,-2.4) {2};
 \node[nd] (Ta) at (-3.3,-3.6) {$T$};
 \node[nd] (n5) at (-0.7,-3.6) {5};
 \node[nd] (n9) at (-3.3,-4.8) {9};
 \foreach \a/\b in {Ea/Eb,Ea/pl,Ea/Tc,Eb/Ec,Eb/mi,Eb/Tb,Tc/n2,Ec/Ta,Tb/n5,Ta/n9} \draw[gray] (\a)--(\b);
 \node[font=\small,align=center] at (-1.0,-5.8) {(a) left-recursive grammar\\the tree leans \emph{left}: $(9-5)+2$};
 % (b) right-recursive
 \begin{scope}[xshift=8.6cm]
  \node[nd] (E) at (0,0) {$E$};
  \node[nd] (T) at (-1.3,-1.2) {$T$};
  \node[nd] (R1) at (1.3,-1.2) {$R$};
  \node[nd] (a9) at (-1.3,-2.4) {9};
  \node[nd] (m) at (0.3,-2.4) {$-$};
  \node[nd] (T5) at (1.4,-2.4) {$T$};
  \node[nd] (R2) at (2.9,-2.4) {$R$};
  \node[nd] (a5) at (1.4,-3.6) {5};
  \node[nd] (p) at (2.3,-3.6) {$+$};
  \node[nd] (T2) at (3.3,-3.6) {$T$};
  \node[nd] (R3) at (4.5,-3.6) {$R$};
  \node[nd] (a2) at (3.3,-4.8) {2};
  \node[nd] (ep) at (4.5,-4.8) {$\varepsilon$};
  \foreach \a/\b in {E/T,E/R1,T/a9,R1/m,R1/T5,R1/R2,T5/a5,R2/p,R2/T2,R2/R3,T2/a2,R3/ep} \draw[gray] (\a)--(\b);
  \node[font=\small,align=center] at (1.7,-5.8) {(b) right-recursive grammar\\the tree leans \emph{right}};
 \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Parse trees of \texttt{9-5+2} for the two grammars. In (a) the subtree for $9-5$ sits below the $+$, so the tree itself shows the grouping. In (b) the $9$ and the two operations are siblings along a chain of $R$'s; the grouping is no longer visible in the shape.}
\label{fig:leftrectrees}
\end{figure}

In tree (a) the rule $E.val=E_1.val\pm T.val$ gets the grouping $(9-5)+2$ for free, because $9-5$ is a subtree. In tree (b) there is no such subtree. The 9 is a sibling of the whole chain of $R$'s.

\begin{pitfallbox}{The tempting wrong fix}
Why not make the grammar right-recursive in the obvious way, $E\to T-E_1\mid T+E_1\mid T$, and keep the rules $E.val=T.val\pm E_1.val$? The grammar is fine, but the meaning is wrong: for \texttt{9-5+2} we get $9-(5+2)=2$ instead of $(9-5)+2=6$. The tree now groups to the right, and subtraction is not associative. The translation must keep the \emph{left} grouping even though the tree leans right.
\end{pitfallbox}

\paragraph{The idea: carry a running total.} Think of a pocket calculator. You key in 9, then press ``$-5$'' (display shows 4), then ``$+2$'' (display shows 6). You never needed the whole expression; the display always holds \emph{the result so far}. We give $R$ exactly this display:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $R.i$ (\emph{inherited}, handed down from the left) is the result \textbf{so far} when $R$ starts;
\item $R.s$ (\emph{synthesized}, handed back up) is the \textbf{final result} when $R$ is finished.
\end{itemize}
Each rule of the old grammar finds its place in the new one:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item The \textbf{base case} $E\to T$ starts the display: $R.i=T.val$.
\item Each \textbf{repeated group} $+\,T$ or $-\,T$ updates the display and passes it to the next $R$: $R_1.i=R.i\pm T.val$.
\item The \textbf{end} $R\to\varepsilon$ has nothing more to read, so the display is the answer: $R.s=R.i$. The answer is then copied upward through every $R$ to $E$.
\end{enumerate}

\providecommand{\cfx}[1]{\textcolor{blue!70!black}{#1}}
\providecommand{\cgy}[1]{\textcolor{red!70!black}{#1}}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.97\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=4pt,font=\small},
  dn/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},blue!70!black,thick},
  up/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},red!70!black,thick},
  lab/.style={font=\scriptsize,align=center}]
 % top row: running total goes down the chain of R's (left to right)
 \node[box] (t)  at (-3.4,0) {$T.val=9$};
 \node[box] (r0) at (0,0)    {$R.i=9$};
 \node[box] (r1) at (4.4,0)  {$R_1.i=4$};
 \node[box] (r2) at (8.8,0)  {$R_2.i=6$};
 \draw[dn] (t)--(r0) node[midway,above,lab] {start:\\$R.i=T.val$};
 \draw[dn] (r0)--(r1) node[midway,above,lab] {read $-5$:\\$9-5=4$};
 \draw[dn] (r1)--(r2) node[midway,above,lab] {read $+2$:\\$4+2=6$};
 % bottom row: the final answer comes back (right to left)
 \node[box] (s2) at (8.8,-2.4) {$R_2.s=6$};
 \node[box] (s1) at (4.4,-2.4) {$R_1.s=6$};
 \node[box] (s0) at (0,-2.4)   {$R.s=6$};
 \node[box] (e)  at (-3.4,-2.4) {$E.val=6$};
 \draw[up] (r2)--(s2) node[midway,right,lab] {$\varepsilon$:\\$s=i$};
 \draw[up] (s2)--(s1) node[midway,below,lab] {$R_1.s=R_2.s$};
 \draw[up] (s1)--(s0) node[midway,below,lab] {$R.s=R_1.s$};
 \draw[up] (s0)--(e)  node[midway,below,lab] {$E.val=R.s$};
 \node[lab,anchor=east,text=blue!70!black] at (-4.6,0) {handed \emph{down}\\(inherited $R.i$)};
 \node[lab,anchor=east,text=red!70!black]  at (-4.6,-2.4) {handed \emph{back}\\(synthesized $R.s$)};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{The running total of \texttt{9-5+2} after removing left recursion. Top row: each $R$ receives the total so far in $R.i$, updates it with the next operand, and passes it to the next $R$. When the input is used up ($R\to\varepsilon$), the total is turned around (bottom row) and copied back through the $R.s$ attributes to $E.val$.}
\label{fig:runtotal}
\end{figure}

\begin{defbox}{General transformation, SDD form (equations only), with comments}
Same colour means same piece: the \cfx{base case} is blue, the \cgy{repeated part} is red. Nothing is invented; every piece of the old rules is only \emph{moved}.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l p{4.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} & \textbf{Why} \\ \midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textbf{Left-recursive SDD (given)}} \\
$A \to A_1\,\cgy{Y}$ & $\at{A}{a}=\cgy{g}(\at{A_1}{a},\ \cgy{\at{Y}{y}})$ & \\
$A \to \cfx{X}$      & $\at{A}{a}=\cfx{f}(\cfx{\at{X}{x}})$ & \\ \midrule
\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\textbf{After removing left recursion (answer)}} \\
$A \to \cfx{X}\,R$ &
  $\at{R}{i}=\cfx{f}(\cfx{\at{X}{x}})$; \ $\at{A}{a}=\at{R}{s}$ &
  The base case \emph{starts} the running value. The final answer is brought back in $R.s$. \\
$R \to \cgy{Y}\,R_1$ &
  $\at{R_1}{i}=\cgy{g}(\at{R}{i},\ \cgy{\at{Y}{y}})$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ &
  Each repetition \emph{updates} the running value: the old $A_1.a$ is now $R.i$. The answer of the rest is passed up unchanged. \\
$R \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$ &
  \emph{Nothing left} to read, so the running value is the answer. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\at{R}{i}$ is inherited and $\at{R}{s}$ is synthesized. There are no braces and no order of writing; the dependencies alone fix the order of evaluation. The SDD is now L-attributed, not S-attributed.
\end{defbox}

\begin{defbox}{General transformation, SDT form (actions inside the body), with comments}
\begin{align*}
\text{given:}\quad A &\to A_1\,\cgy{Y}\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=\cgy{g}(\at{A_1}{a},\ \cgy{\at{Y}{y}})\ \}\ \mid\ \cfx{X}\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=\cfx{f}(\cfx{\at{X}{x}})\ \}\\[4pt]
\text{answer:}\quad A &\to \cfx{X}\ \{\ \at{R}{i}=\cfx{f}(\cfx{\at{X}{x}})\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=\at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to \cgy{Y}\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=\cgy{g}(\at{R}{i},\ \cgy{\at{Y}{y}})\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}\ \}
\end{align*}
\textbf{Where the actions stand, and why:}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $\{R.i=\dots\}$ and $\{R_1.i=\dots\}$ compute an \emph{inherited} attribute, so they stand \emph{immediately before} the $R$ that uses it (the value must exist before $R$ starts).
\item $\{A.a=R.s\}$ and $\{R.s=R_1.s\}$ compute a \emph{synthesized} attribute of the head, so they stand at the \emph{end} of the body (the value exists only after $R$ has finished).
\item The action of $R\to\varepsilon$ is the only body, so it is both first and last.
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.78\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center},
  up/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},red!70!black,thick}]
 \node[nd] (A0) at (0,0)       {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a=g(A_1.a,\,Y.y)$}};
 \node[nd] (A1) at (-2.8,-1.8) {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a=g(A_2.a,\,Y.y)$}};
 \node[nd] (Y1) at (1.8,-1.8)  {$Y$};
 \node[nd] (A2) at (-5.6,-3.6) {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a=f(X.x)$}};
 \node[nd] (Y2) at (-1.2,-3.6) {$Y$};
 \node[nd] (X)  at (-5.6,-5.4) {$X$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {A0/A1,A0/Y1,A1/A2,A1/Y2,A2/X} \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \draw[up] (X.north east) to[bend right=0] node[right,font=\scriptsize,text=red!70!black] {$x$} ([xshift=3mm]A2.south);
 \draw[up] ([xshift=-4mm,yshift=-1mm]A2.north east) -- ([xshift=-2mm,yshift=1mm]A1.south west);
 \draw[up] ([xshift=-4mm,yshift=-1mm]A1.north east) -- ([xshift=-2mm,yshift=1mm]A0.south west);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{(a) Before: the left-recursive parse tree for the input $X\,Y\,Y$. The values flow \textcolor{red!70!black}{up} the left spine: $f(X.x)$ first, then the first $Y$ is combined, then the second.}
\label{fig:lrflow}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.85\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center},
  up/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},red!70!black,thick},
  dn/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},blue!70!black,thick,dashed}]
 \node[nd] (B0) at (0,0)       {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a=R.s$}};
 \node[nd] (BX) at (-2.6,-1.8) {$X$};
 \node[nd] (R0) at (2.6,-1.8)  {$R$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=f(X.x)$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $s=R_1.s$}};
 \node[nd] (Ya) at (0.2,-4.2)  {$Y$};
 \node[nd] (R1) at (5.2,-4.2)  {$R_1$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=g(R.i,\,Y.y)$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $s=R_2.s$}};
 \node[nd] (Yb) at (2.6,-6.6)  {$Y$};
 \node[nd] (R2) at (7.8,-6.6)  {$R_2$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=g(R_1.i,\,Y.y)$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $s=R_2.i$}};
 \node[nd] (Ep) at (7.8,-8.3)  {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {B0/BX,B0/R0,R0/Ya,R0/R1,R1/Yb,R1/R2,R2/Ep} \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \draw[dn] ([xshift=9mm]R0.south) -- ([xshift=9mm]R1.north);
 \draw[dn] ([xshift=9mm]R1.south) -- ([xshift=9mm]R2.north);
 \draw[up] ([xshift=-9mm]R1.north) -- ([xshift=-9mm]R0.south);
 \draw[up] ([xshift=-9mm]R2.north) -- ([xshift=-9mm]R1.south);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{(b) After: the transformed parse tree for the same input $X\,Y\,Y$. \textcolor{blue!70!black}{Dashed arrows:} the running value $i$ is passed down the chain of $R$'s, starting with $f(X.x)$ and combined with each $Y$ in the same order as in (a). \textcolor{red!70!black}{Solid arrows:} at $R_2\to\varepsilon$ the value is turned around ($s=i$) and comes back up through the $s$ attributes to $A.a$.}
\label{fig:lrflow2}
\end{figure}

\begin{examplebox}{What to write in the exam: the procedure in six steps (SDD or SDT)}
Given $E\to E_1+T\ \{E.val=E_1.val+T.val\}\ \mid\ E_1-T\ \{E.val=E_1.val-T.val\}\ \mid\ T\ \{E.val=T.val\}$. The left column is what to do, the right column is the result for this grammar.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{5.8cm} p{8.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Result for the example} \\ \midrule
1. \textbf{Find the parts.} Which alternatives are left-recursive ($A\to A\,\alpha$), which is the base case ($A\to\beta$)? Note the function each one computes.
  & Recursive: $+\,T$ with $g_1(a,y)=a+y$ and $-\,T$ with $g_2(a,y)=a-y$. Base: $T$ with $f(x)=x$. \\[3pt]
2. \textbf{New grammar.} $A\to\beta\,R$ and $R\to\alpha_1R\mid\alpha_2R\mid\dots\mid\varepsilon$.
  & $E\to T\,R$, \ $R\to +\,T\,R\mid -\,T\,R\mid\varepsilon$ \\[3pt]
3. \textbf{New attributes.} $R.i$ inherited (value so far), $R.s$ synthesized (final value).
  & $R.i$ and $R.s$ hold numbers, like $E.val$. \\[3pt]
4. \textbf{Base case.} Start the running value, bring back the answer.
  & $E\to T\,R$: \ $R.i=T.val$; \ $E.val=R.s$ \\[3pt]
5. \textbf{Each recursive alternative.} New running value = old function with $R.i$ in place of $A_1.a$; pass the answer up.
  & $R\to +\,T\,R_1$: $R_1.i=R.i+T.val$; $R.s=R_1.s$. \ Similarly for $-$: $R_1.i=R.i-T.val$. \\[3pt]
6. \textbf{The empty case.} The running value is the answer.
  & $R\to\varepsilon$: \ $R.s=R.i$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{For an SDD} the six steps are the whole answer. \textbf{For an SDT} add a seventh step: put each equation into the body as an action, the ones for inherited attributes just before their symbol and the ones for synthesized attributes at the end, exactly as in the box above. \textbf{Always finish with a check} on a short input such as \texttt{9-5+2}: the running value must go $9\to4\to6$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1, SDD form: removing left recursion from the SDD of $9-5+2$}
\textbf{Given (left-recursive, S-attributed):}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rule} \\ \midrule
$E \to E_1 + T$ & $\at{E}{val}=\at{E_1}{val}+\at{T}{val}$ \\
$E \to E_1 - T$ & $\at{E}{val}=\at{E_1}{val}-\at{T}{val}$ \\
$E \to T$       & $\at{E}{val}=\at{T}{val}$ \\
$T \to \mathbf{num}$ & $\at{T}{val}=\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Answer (the SDD after removing left recursion):}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rule} \\ \midrule
$E \to T\,R$        & $\at{R}{i}=\at{T}{val}$; \ $\at{E}{val}=\at{R}{s}$ \\
$R \to +\,T\,R_1$   & $\at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}+\at{T}{val}$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to -\,T\,R_1$   & $\at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}-\at{T}{val}$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$ \\
$T \to \mathbf{num}$ & $\at{T}{val}=\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Classification and evaluation.} $\at{T}{val}$, $\at{R}{s}$ and $\at{E}{val}$ are synthesized; $\at{R}{i}$ is inherited. The rule for $R_1.i$ uses the inherited attribute of its parent ($R.i$) and the attribute of the sibling $T$ to its left, so the SDD is \textbf{L-attributed} but no longer S-attributed. A single depth-first, left-to-right traversal evaluates it. For \texttt{9-5+2} the values are $R.i=9,\ 4,\ 6$ and finally $E.val=6$, exactly as in the figure and the table of the next example, which shows the same rules written as an SDT.

\medskip
\textbf{The dependency graph, drawn on the parse tree.} Every attribute instance is a small box next to its node. An arrow $x\to y$ means \emph{``$y$ is computed from $x$''}: the value flows along the arrow (the book's convention, ``used $\to$ defined''; if you read arrows as ``depends on'' they point the other way round). The yellow numbers are one valid evaluation order.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.98\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt},
  ab/.style={draw,rounded corners=1.5pt,inner sep=2pt,font=\scriptsize,fill=white},
  ord/.style={circle,fill=yellow!70,inner sep=1pt,font=\tiny\bfseries},
  tr/.style={gray!55},
  dn/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},blue!70!black,thick},
  up/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},red!70!black,thick},
  lx/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]},black!60,thick}]
 % ---- the parse tree
 \node[nd] (E)  at (0,0)       {$E$};
 \node[nd] (Ta) at (-4.2,-2.4) {$T$};
 \node[nd] (R0) at (1.8,-2.4)  {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n9) at (-4.2,-4.6) {\textbf{num}};
 \node[nd] (mi) at (-0.5,-4.8) {$-$};
 \node[nd] (Tb) at (1.8,-4.8)  {$T$};
 \node[nd] (R1) at (5.8,-4.8)  {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n5) at (1.8,-7.0)  {\textbf{num}};
 \node[nd] (pl) at (4.3,-7.0)  {$+$};
 \node[nd] (Tc) at (6.4,-7.0)  {$T$};
 \node[nd] (R2) at (10.6,-7.0) {$R$};
 \node[nd] (n2) at (6.4,-9.2)  {\textbf{num}};
 \node[nd] (ep) at (10.6,-9.2) {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {E/Ta,E/R0,Ta/n9,R0/mi,R0/Tb,R0/R1,Tb/n5,R1/pl,R1/Tc,R1/R2,Tc/n2,R2/ep}
   \draw[tr] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 % ---- attribute boxes (the yellow number is the evaluation order)
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:10}] (Ev)  at (1.2,0)     {val=6};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:1}]  (Tav) at (-3.1,-2.4) {val=9};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:2}]  (R0i) at (0.65,-2.4) {i=9};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:9}]  (R0s) at (2.95,-2.4) {s=6};
 \node[ab] (l9) at (-3.1,-4.6) {lexval=9};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:3}]  (Tbv) at (2.9,-4.8)  {val=5};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:4}]  (R1i) at (4.7,-4.8)  {i=4};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:8}]  (R1s) at (6.9,-4.8)  {s=6};
 \node[ab] (l5) at (2.9,-7.0) {lexval=5};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:5}]  (Tcv) at (7.5,-7.0)  {val=2};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:6}]  (R2i) at (9.5,-7.0)  {i=6};
 \node[ab,label={[ord]above:7}]  (R2s) at (11.7,-7.0) {s=6};
 \node[ab] (l2) at (7.5,-9.2) {lexval=2};
 % ---- token value -> T.val
 \draw[lx] (l9.north)--(Tav.south);
 \draw[lx] (l5.north)--(Tbv.south);
 \draw[lx] (l2.north)--(Tcv.south);
 % ---- inherited flow: left sibling and parent -> R.i
 \draw[dn] (Tav.east)--(R0i.west);
 \draw[dn] (R0i.south) to[bend right=18] (R1i.north west);
 \draw[dn] (Tbv.east)--(R1i.west);
 \draw[dn] (R1i.south) to[bend right=18] (R2i.north west);
 \draw[dn] (Tcv.east)--(R2i.west);
 % ---- turn-around at epsilon, then synthesized flow back up
 \draw[up] (R2i.south) to[bend right=55] (R2s.south);
 \draw[up] (R2s.north) to[bend right=12] (R1s.south east);
 \draw[up] (R1s.north) to[bend right=12] (R0s.south east);
 \draw[up] (R0s.north) to[bend right=12] (Ev.south east);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Dependency graph of \texttt{9-5+2} for the SDD without left recursion. Grey arrows: the token value becomes $T.val$. \textcolor{blue!70!black}{Blue arrows:} $R.i$ is computed from its \emph{left sibling} ($T.val$) and from its \emph{parent's} $R.i$ and goes down the chain of $R$'s. \textcolor{red!70!black}{Red arrows:} at $R\to\varepsilon$ the value is turned around ($R_2.s=R_2.i$) and copied up to $E.val$.}
\label{fig:lrdepgraph}
\end{figure}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Order} & \textbf{Attribute computed} & \textbf{Rule (from the production)} & \textbf{Value} \\ \midrule
1 & $T.val$ (first $T$) & $T\to\mathbf{num}$ & $9$ \\
2 & $R.i$ & $E\to T\,R$: \ $R.i=T.val$ & $9$ \\
3 & $T.val$ (second $T$) & $T\to\mathbf{num}$ & $5$ \\
4 & $R_1.i$ & $R\to -\,T\,R_1$: \ $R_1.i=R.i-T.val$ & $9-5=4$ \\
5 & $T.val$ (third $T$) & $T\to\mathbf{num}$ & $2$ \\
6 & $R_2.i$ & $R_1\to +\,T\,R_2$: \ $R_2.i=R_1.i+T.val$ & $4+2=6$ \\
7 & $R_2.s$ & $R_2\to\varepsilon$: \ $R_2.s=R_2.i$ & $6$ \\
8 & $R_1.s$ & $R_1\to +\,T\,R_2$: \ $R_1.s=R_2.s$ & $6$ \\
9 & $R.s$ & $R\to -\,T\,R_1$: \ $R.s=R_1.s$ & $6$ \\
10 & $E.val$ & $E\to T\,R$: \ $E.val=R.s$ & $6$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{How to read the tree in three questions.}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \emph{Where does an $R.i$ come from?} It is computed by the rule of the production in which that $R$ appears in the body. The first $R$ gets $T.val$ from its left sibling (rule of $E\to T\,R$). Every later $R_1$ gets $R.i\ (\pm)\ T.val$: the value of its \textbf{parent} $R$ and of its \textbf{left sibling} $T$, combined by the operator of that production.
\item \emph{What happens at the bottom?} $R\to\varepsilon$ has no input left. Its only rule, $R.s=R.i$, \emph{turns the value around}: the running total becomes the final answer.
\item \emph{How does the answer reach the root?} Each $R.s$ is just a copy of the $R.s$ of its child ($R.s=R_1.s$), so the value climbs one $R$ at a time until $E.val=R.s$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1, SDT form: evaluating $9-5+2$ with a left-recursion-free grammar}
The left-recursive grammar $E\to E_1+T\mid E_1-T\mid T$, $T\to\mathbf{num}$, with $E.val=E_1.val\pm T.val$, becomes
\begin{align*}
E &\to T\ \{\ R.i=T.val\ \}\ R\ \{\ E.val=R.s\ \}\\
R &\to +\ T\ \{\ R_1.i=R.i+T.val\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ R.s=R_1.s\ \}\\
  &\mid\ -\ T\ \{\ R_1.i=R.i-T.val\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ R.s=R_1.s\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ R.s=R.i\ \}\\
T &\to \mathbf{num}\ \{\ T.val=\mathbf{num}.lexval\ \}
\end{align*}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.95\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=1.5pt,align=center}]
 \node[nd] (E)  at (0,0) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=6$}};
 \node[nd] (T0) at (-2.6,-1.6) {$T$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=9$}};
 \node[nd] (R0) at (1.4,-1.6) {$R$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=9,\ s=6$}};
 \node[nd] (n9) at (-2.6,-3.2) {\textbf{num}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $9$}};
 \node[nd] (m1) at (-0.4,-3.2) {$-$};
 \node[nd] (T5) at (1.2,-3.2) {$T$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=5$}};
 \node[nd] (R1) at (3.8,-3.2) {$R$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=4,\ s=6$}};
 \node[nd] (n5) at (1.2,-4.8) {\textbf{num}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $5$}};
 \node[nd] (p1) at (2.4,-4.8) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (T2) at (4.0,-4.8) {$T$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=2$}};
 \node[nd] (R2) at (6.2,-4.8) {$R$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $i=6,\ s=6$}};
 \node[nd] (n2) at (4.0,-6.4) {\textbf{num}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $2$}};
 \node[nd] (ep) at (6.2,-6.4) {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {E/T0,E/R0,T0/n9,R0/m1,R0/T5,R0/R1,T5/n5,R1/p1,R1/T2,R1/R2,T2/n2,R2/ep}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Annotated parse tree for \texttt{9-5+2}. The inherited $R.i$ flows down and to the right ($9\to4\to6$: first $9-5$, then $4+2$); the synthesized $R.s$ then carries the final $6$ back up to $E.val$.}
\end{figure}

\textbf{The running total step by step.} Each row is one call of $R$; read $R.i$ as ``the display of the calculator''.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l c l l c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Call} & $R.i$ \textbf{on entry} & \textbf{Next input} & \textbf{Action} & $R.s$ \textbf{on return} \\ \midrule
$E\to T\,R$ & --- & \texttt{9} & $R.i = T.val = 9$ & --- \\
$R$ (1st) & 9 & \texttt{- 5} & $R_1.i = 9-5 = 4$ & 6 \\
$R_1$ (2nd) & 4 & \texttt{+ 2} & $R_2.i = 4+2 = 6$ & 6 \\
$R_2$ (3rd) & 6 & end of input & $\varepsilon$: \ $R_2.s = R_2.i = 6$ & 6 \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The value goes \emph{down} as $R.i$ ($9\to4\to6$) and the final $6$ is copied back \emph{up} through the $R.s$ attributes to $E.val$.

The tree leans to the right, but the arithmetic is still left-associative: $(9-5)+2=6$, not $9-(5+2)=2$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 2, SDD form: the syntax-tree SDD without left recursion}
The syntax-tree SDD of Section~\ref{sec:sddtree} is left-recursive in $E$. Here $\at{R}{i}$ holds a \emph{pointer to the tree built so far}:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rule} \\ \midrule
$E \to T\,R$        & $\at{R}{i}=\at{T}{node}$; \ $\at{E}{node}=\at{R}{s}$ \\
$R \to +\,T\,R_1$   & $\at{R_1}{i}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},\ \at{R}{i},\ \at{T}{node})$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to -\,T\,R_1$   & $\at{R_1}{i}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},\ \at{R}{i},\ \at{T}{node})$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$ \\
$T \to (\,E\,)$     & $\at{T}{node}=\at{E}{node}$ \\
$T \to \mathbf{id}$ & $\at{T}{node}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{entry})$ \\
$T \to \mathbf{num}$ & $\at{T}{node}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{num},\ \at{\mathbf{num}}{val})$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Only the first four rows are new; the three $T$ rows are unchanged. The same rules written as an SDT, together with a trace for \texttt{a - 4 + c}, are in the example of Section~\ref{sec:lattr}. The pattern is the one of Example~1: the base case initialises $R.i$, every repeated group updates it (here: builds a new node with the old tree as its left child), and $\varepsilon$ hands the result up.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 3, both forms: counting the items of a list}
The grammar $L\to L_1\,\tok{','}\,\mathbf{id}\mid\mathbf{id}$ generates lists such as \texttt{x, y, z}. The attribute $\at{L}{count}$ is the number of identifiers.

\textbf{Left-recursive SDD (given).}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
$L \to L_1\ \tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}$ & $\at{L}{count}=\at{L_1}{count}+1$ \\
$L \to \mathbf{id}$ & $\at{L}{count}=1$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Step 1, the grammar.} $L$ is an $\mathbf{id}$ followed by any number of ``$\tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}$'' groups, so $L\to\mathbf{id}\ R$ and $R\to\tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}\ R\mid\varepsilon$.

\textbf{Step 2, the SDD form.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
$L \to \mathbf{id}\ R$ & $\at{R}{i}=1$; \ $\at{L}{count}=\at{R}{s}$ \\
$R \to \tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}\ R_1$ & $\at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}+1$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{Step 3, the SDT form} (every inherited attribute is computed just before its symbol, every synthesized attribute of the head at the end):
\begin{align*}
L &\to \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \at{R}{i}=1\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{L}{count}=\at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to \tok{','}\ \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}+1\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}\ \}
\end{align*}
\textbf{Trace for \texttt{x, y, z}.} $R.i=1$ after \texttt{x}; after \texttt{, y} the next $R$ gets $i=2$; after \texttt{, z} the next gets $i=3$; $R\to\varepsilon$ gives $R.s=3$, which is copied up to $L.count=3$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 4: two recursive alternatives (the general case)}
Real grammars have several recursive alternatives, such as $E\to E_1+T\mid E_1-T\mid T$. The general form is
\[
A \to A_1\,Y\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=g(\at{A_1}{a},\ \at{Y}{y})\ \}\ \mid\ A_1\,Z\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=h(\at{A_1}{a},\ \at{Z}{z})\ \}\ \mid\ X\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=f(\at{X}{x})\ \}.
\]
\textbf{SDD form of the answer.} Every recursive alternative becomes one alternative of $R$, with its \emph{own} function:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
$A \to X\,R$ & $\at{R}{i}=f(\at{X}{x})$; \ $\at{A}{a}=\at{R}{s}$ \\
$R \to Y\,R_1$ & $\at{R_1}{i}=g(\at{R}{i},\ \at{Y}{y})$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to Z\,R_1$ & $\at{R_1}{i}=h(\at{R}{i},\ \at{Z}{z})$; \ $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$ \\
$R \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{SDT form of the answer.}
\begin{align*}
A &\to X\ \{\ \at{R}{i}=f(\at{X}{x})\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{A}{a}=\at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to Y\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=g(\at{R}{i},\ \at{Y}{y})\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\\
  &\mid\ Z\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=h(\at{R}{i},\ \at{Z}{z})\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}\ \}
\end{align*}
The sum-and-difference grammar is the instance $Y=+\,T$, $Z=-\,T$, $g(a,y)=a+y$, $h(a,z)=a-z$, $f(x)=x$. If there are \emph{several base cases} ($A\to X\mid W$), each gets its own copy of the first rule: $A\to X\,R$ with $f_1$ and $A\to W\,R$ with $f_2$. If the SDD computes \emph{several attributes}, each one gets its own pair $R.i_k$, $R.s_k$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Common mistakes when removing left recursion}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Wrong operand order.} The new running value is $R.i - T.val$ (old value first), never $T.val - R.i$. It does not matter for $+$ and $*$, but it does for $-$ and $/$.
\item \textbf{Forgetting to start the accumulator,} that is, the rule $R.i=f(X.x)$ in the base case; or forgetting to bring the answer back with $A.a=R.s$.
\item \textbf{Forgetting the $\varepsilon$ rule} $R.s=R.i$. Without it the final value is never turned around.
\item \textbf{In an SDT: putting the action for $R_1.i$ after $R_1$.} An inherited attribute must be computed \emph{before} the symbol that uses it.
\item \textbf{Fixing only the grammar.} The right-recursive grammar with the old rules gives the wrong grouping (see the pitfall box above); the attributes must be rewritten, too.
\end{itemize}
\end{pitfallbox}

\subsection{Converting an L-attributed SDD into an SDT}
\label{sec:lattr}
\begin{defbox}{Rules for converting an L-attributed SDD to an SDT}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{Inherited attribute} of a nonterminal $X$ in the body: embed the action that computes it \emph{immediately before} $X$ in the body. If several inherited attributes of $X$ depend on one another, order the actions appropriately.
\item \textbf{Synthesized attribute} of the head $A$: place the action that computes it at the \emph{end} of the production body.
\end{enumerate}
\end{defbox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example: syntax tree for a left-recursion-free grammar}
After removing left recursion from $E \to E_1 + T \mid E_1 - T \mid T$ (the SDD form of these rules is Example~2 of Section~\ref{sec:leftrec}):
\begin{align*}
E &\to T\ \{\ \at{R}{i} = \at{T}{node}\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{E}{node} = \at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to +\ T\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},\at{R}{i},\at{T}{node})\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s} = \at{R_1}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to -\ T\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},\at{R}{i},\at{T}{node})\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s} = \at{R_1}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s} = \at{R}{i}\ \}\\
T &\to \mathbf{id}\ \{\ \at{T}{node} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{id},\at{\mathbf{id}}{entry})\ \}\\
T &\to \mathbf{num}\ \{\ \at{T}{node} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Leaf}(\mathbf{num},\at{\mathbf{num}}{val})\ \}
\end{align*}
Here $R.i$ (inherited) carries the tree built \emph{so far}; $R.s$ (synthesized) returns the final tree. For \texttt{a - 4 + c} the tree $((a - 4) + c)$ is produced with the correct left-associativity.

\textbf{Trace for \texttt{a - 4 + c}.} Now the ``running total'' is a \emph{pointer to the tree built so far}. The nodes are named $p_1,\dots,p_5$ in the order of creation, exactly as in Section~\ref{sec:sddtree}.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l c l L{5.6cm} c@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Call} & $R.i$ \textbf{on entry} & \textbf{Next input} & \textbf{Action} & $R.s$ \textbf{on return} \\ \midrule
$E\to T\,R$ & --- & \texttt{a} & $T.node = p_1$ (leaf $a$); \ $R.i = p_1$ & --- \\
$R$ (1st) & $p_1$ & \texttt{- 4} & $T.node = p_2$ (leaf 4); \ $R_1.i = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},p_1,p_2) = p_3$ & $p_5$ \\
$R_1$ (2nd) & $p_3$ & \texttt{+ c} & $T.node = p_4$ (leaf $c$); \ $R_2.i = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},p_3,p_4) = p_5$ & $p_5$ \\
$R_2$ (3rd) & $p_5$ & end & $\varepsilon$: \ $R_2.s = R_2.i = p_5$ & $p_5$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The same five nodes are created in the same order as with the left-recursive SDD, so the syntax tree is identical (Figure~\ref{fig:sddtree-grow}); only the way the pointer travels differs: it is handed down in $R.i$ and returned in $R.s$.
\end{examplebox}

%====================================================================
\subsection{Application: the structure of a type (array types)}
\label{sec:typestruct}

\paragraph{The task.} A declaration such as \texttt{int [2][3]} must be turned into a \textbf{type expression}: the array type \textit{array}$(2,\ \textit{array}(3,\ \mathit{integer}))$, an array of 2 elements, each of which is an array of 3 integers. The compiler later uses this tree to compute sizes and offsets (Section~\ref{sec:exprtac}) and to check types.

\paragraph{The grammar.} $T\to B\,C$, \ $B\to\mathbf{int}\mid\mathbf{float}$, \ $C\to\tok{[}\,\mathbf{num}\,\tok{]}\,C_1\mid\varepsilon$. The nonterminal $B$ is the basic type and $C$ is the (possibly empty) list of dimensions.

\paragraph{The SDD.} The basic type $B.t$ is known at the \emph{left}, but it has to end up at the \emph{bottom} of the array type, where the last dimension is. So we pass it down in an inherited attribute $C.b$ (``base'') and build the type on the way back up in the synthesized attribute $C.t$.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$T \to B\,C$ & $\at{T}{t}=\at{C}{t}$; \ $\at{C}{b}=\at{B}{t}$ \\
$B \to \mathbf{int}$ & $\at{B}{t}=\mathit{integer}$ \\
$B \to \mathbf{float}$ & $\at{B}{t}=\mathit{float}$ \\
$C \to \tok{[}\,\mathbf{num}\,\tok{]}\,C_1$ & $\at{C}{t}=\mathit{array}(\at{\mathbf{num}}{val},\ \at{C_1}{t})$; \ $\at{C_1}{b}=\at{C}{b}$ \\
$C \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{C}{t}=\at{C}{b}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$B.t$, $C.t$ and $T.t$ are synthesized; $C.b$ is inherited. The SDD is \textbf{L-attributed}: $C.b$ of $C$ depends on its left sibling $B$, and $C_1.b$ depends on the inherited attribute of its parent. It is not S-attributed, because the base type has to travel \emph{downward}.

\begin{examplebox}{Example: the type of \texttt{int [2][3]}}
The parse tree and the values of the attributes are in Figure~\ref{fig:typetree}.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center}]
 \node[nd] (T)  at (0,0) {$T$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t=\mathit{array}(2,\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer}))$}};
 \node[nd] (B)  at (-5.4,-1.8) {$B$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t=\mathit{integer}$}};
 \node[nd] (C)  at (1.8,-1.8) {$C$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b=\mathit{integer}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t=\mathit{array}(2,\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer}))$}};
 \node[nd] (in) at (-5.4,-3.7) {\textbf{int}};
 \node[nd] (l1) at (-1.6,-3.7) {$\tok{[}$};
 \node[nd] (n2) at (-0.6,-3.7) {\textbf{num}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=2$}};
 \node[nd] (r1) at (0.7,-3.7) {$\tok{]}$};
 \node[nd] (C1) at (4.2,-3.7) {$C$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b=\mathit{integer}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t=\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer})$}};
 \node[nd] (l2) at (1.4,-5.9) {$\tok{[}$};
 \node[nd] (n3) at (2.4,-5.9) {\textbf{num}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $val=3$}};
 \node[nd] (r2) at (3.7,-5.9) {$\tok{]}$};
 \node[nd] (C2) at (6.6,-5.9) {$C$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b=\mathit{integer}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t=\mathit{integer}$}};
 \node[nd] (ep) at (6.6,-7.8) {$\varepsilon$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {T/B,T/C,B/in,C/l1,C/n2,C/r1,C/C1,C1/l2,C1/n3,C1/r2,C1/C2,C2/ep}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Annotated parse tree of \texttt{int [2][3]}. The base type \textit{integer} flows \emph{down} through the inherited attributes $C.b$ to the empty production; the array type is assembled on the way \emph{up} in the synthesized attributes $C.t$.}
\label{fig:typetree}
\end{figure}
The order in which the values are computed (depth-first, left to right):
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Rule used} & \textbf{Value} \\ \midrule
1 & $B\to\mathbf{int}$ & $B.t=\mathit{integer}$ \\
2 & $T\to B\,C$ & $C.b=B.t=\mathit{integer}$ \\
3 & $C\to\tok{[}2\tok{]}\,C_1$ & $C_1.b=C.b=\mathit{integer}$ \\
4 & $C_1\to\tok{[}3\tok{]}\,C_2$ & $C_2.b=C_1.b=\mathit{integer}$ \\
5 & $C_2\to\varepsilon$ & $C_2.t=C_2.b=\mathit{integer}$ \\
6 & $C_1\to\tok{[}3\tok{]}\,C_2$ & $C_1.t=\mathit{array}(3,\ C_2.t)=\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer})$ \\
7 & $C\to\tok{[}2\tok{]}\,C_1$ & $C.t=\mathit{array}(2,\ C_1.t)=\mathit{array}(2,\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer}))$ \\
8 & $T\to B\,C$ & $T.t=C.t$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Steps 1 to 4 go \emph{down} (inherited attributes), steps 5 to 8 come back \emph{up} (synthesized attributes). The innermost dimension, 3, is applied first, so the result is $\mathit{array}(2,\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer}))$ and not the other way round.
\end{examplebox}

\paragraph{As an SDT.} Following the rules of Section~\ref{sec:lattr}, the action that computes the inherited attribute $C.b$ goes immediately before $C$, and the actions that compute synthesized attributes go at the end:
\begin{align*}
T &\to B\ \{\ \at{C}{b}=\at{B}{t}\ \}\ C\ \{\ \at{T}{t}=\at{C}{t}\ \}\\
B &\to \mathbf{int}\ \{\ \at{B}{t}=\mathit{integer}\ \}\ \mid\ \mathbf{float}\ \{\ \at{B}{t}=\mathit{float}\ \}\\
C &\to \tok{[}\ \mathbf{num}\ \tok{]}\ \{\ \at{C_1}{b}=\at{C}{b}\ \}\ C_1\ \{\ \at{C}{t}=\mathit{array}(\at{\mathbf{num}}{val},\ \at{C_1}{t})\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{C}{t}=\at{C}{b}\ \}
\end{align*}
The grammar is right-recursive, so a top-down parser can use it directly; no left recursion has to be removed. Section~\ref{sec:impl-lattr} turns it into a recursive-descent parser.

\begin{pitfallbox}{Why not synthesized attributes only?}
Suppose we try $C\to\tok{[}\mathbf{num}\tok{]}\,C_1$ with $C.t=\mathit{array}(num.val,\ C_1.t)$ and $C\to\varepsilon$ with $C.t=B.t$. The base type of $B$ is not visible inside $C$ (it is a sibling of $C$'s parent), so $C\to\varepsilon$ cannot read it. This is exactly why the inherited attribute $C.b$ is needed: it carries $B.t$ down to the place where it is used.
\end{pitfallbox}

%--------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Implementing L-attributed SDDs during top-down parsing}
\label{sec:impl-lattr}

An L-attributed SDD can be evaluated in a single left-to-right depth-first traversal, which is exactly the order in which a top-down parser works.
