So an SDT for an L-attributed SDD can be executed \emph{while parsing}, without building the parse tree. There are three ways to do this with a top-down parser; they differ in where the attribute values are kept.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{4.2cm} p{6.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Parser} & \textbf{Where attributes live} \\ \midrule
A. Arguments and return values & recursive descent & inherited attributes are \emph{parameters} of the procedure of the nonterminal, synthesized attributes are its \emph{return values} \\
B. On-the-fly generation & recursive descent & the big ``code'' attribute is not built as a string; its pieces are \emph{emitted} (printed) as soon as they are known \\
C. Parser-stack records & table-driven LL (stack) & values are kept in \emph{records on the parser stack} and are copied from record to record \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\subsubsection{Method A: recursive descent with arguments and return values}

\begin{defbox}{Recipe: one procedure per nonterminal}
For a nonterminal $A$ with inherited attributes $a_1,\dots$ and synthesized attributes $s_1,\dots$, write a procedure \texttt{A($a_1,\dots$)} that returns $s_1,\dots$. In its body, handle the chosen production from left to right:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item for a terminal: \texttt{match(token)} (and save its attribute, e.g.\ \texttt{lexval}, in a local variable if it is needed);
\item for a nonterminal $X$: compute its inherited attributes from the variables seen so far, \textbf{call} \texttt{X(\dots)}, and store the returned synthesized attributes in local variables;
\item for an action: write it as a statement at that point;
\item at the end: \texttt{return} the synthesized attributes of $A$.
\end{enumerate}
Because the SDD is L-attributed, every value an inherited attribute needs has already been computed when the call is made.
\end{defbox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example A1: the array type of \texttt{int [2][3]}}
The SDT of the previous subsection becomes three procedures. \texttt{C(b)} receives the inherited attribute $C.b$ as its argument and returns $C.t$:
\begin{lstlisting}
Type T() {
    Type b = B();              // B.t
    return C(b);               // C.b = B.t ;  T.t = C.t
}
Type B() {
    if (look == INT)  { match(INT);   return integer; }   // B.t
    else              { match(FLOAT); return float;   }
}
Type C(Type b) {               // b is the inherited attribute C.b
    if (look == '[') {
        match('[');  n = lexval;  match(NUM);  match(']');
        Type c1 = C(b);        // C1.b = C.b
        return array(n, c1);   // C.t = array(num.val, C1.t)
    }
    return b;                  // C -> epsilon:  C.t = C.b
}
\end{lstlisting}
Trace for \texttt{int [2][3]}: \texttt{T()} calls \texttt{B()} which returns \textit{integer}; then \texttt{C(integer)} reads \texttt{[2]} and calls \texttt{C(integer)}, which reads \texttt{[3]} and calls \texttt{C(integer)}; this call sees no \texttt{[}, so it returns \textit{integer}. The returns then build $\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer})$ and finally $\mathit{array}(2,\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer}))$. The values travel \emph{down} as arguments and \emph{up} as return values, exactly as in Figure~\ref{fig:typetree}.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example A2: sums and differences without left recursion}
For the SDT of Example~1 in Section~\ref{sec:leftrec}, the inherited attribute $R.i$ is the parameter of \texttt{R} and $R.s$ is its return value. The terminal \textbf{num} has the attribute \texttt{lexval}.
\begin{lstlisting}
int E() { int t = T();  return R(t); }       // R.i = T.val ;  E.val = R.s

int R(int i) {                               // i is R.i
    if (look == '+') { match('+'); int t = T(); return R(i + t); }
    if (look == '-') { match('-'); int t = T(); return R(i - t); }
    return i;                    // R -> epsilon:  R.s = R.i
    // R1.i = R.i +/- T.val  is the argument of the recursive call
}

int T() { int v = lexval;  match(NUM);  return v; }   // T.val = num.lexval
\end{lstlisting}
For \texttt{9-5+2}: \texttt{E()} gets $9$ from \texttt{T()} and calls \texttt{R(9)}; \texttt{R(9)} reads \texttt{-5} and calls \texttt{R(4)}; \texttt{R(4)} reads \texttt{+2} and calls \texttt{R(6)}; \texttt{R(6)} finds the end of the input and returns $6$, which every caller returns unchanged. The pointer-based variant for the syntax tree of Section~\ref{sec:lattr} has the same shape: \texttt{Node R(Node i)} returns \texttt{R(new Node('+', i, t))} instead of \texttt{R(i + t)}, and \texttt{return i} at the end.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example A3: code for a \texttt{while} statement}
The \textbf{while} statement of the Dragon Book (Chapter 5) is $S\to\mathbf{while}\,(\,C\,)\,S_1$. The attributes are: $S.next$ (inherited; the label after the statement), $C.true$ and $C.false$ (inherited; the jump targets of the condition), and the synthesized $S.code$ and $C.code$. The SDT creates two labels $L_1$ and $L_2$:
\begin{align*}
S \to \mathbf{while}\ \tok{(}\ &\{\ L_1=\mathit{new}();\ L_2=\mathit{new}();\ \at{C}{false}=\at{S}{next};\ \at{C}{true}=L_2\ \}\ C\ \tok{)}\\
&\{\ \at{S_1}{next}=L_1\ \}\ S_1\ \{\ \at{S}{code}=\mathit{label}\cc L_1\cc\at{C}{code}\cc\mathit{label}\cc L_2\cc\at{S_1}{code}\ \}
\end{align*}
The generated code is $L_1\!:\ C.code$ (it jumps to $L_2$ if the condition is true and to $S.next$ if false), then $L_2\!:\ S_1.code$, which ends with a jump back to $S_1.next=L_1$. (The flow-of-control SDD later in these notes, taken from Chapter~6 of the book, writes the same translation with an explicit \texttt{goto begin}.)

As a recursive-descent procedure, $S.next$ is the argument, $S.code$ the return value (a string), and the labels are local variables:
\begin{lstlisting}
string S(label next) {
    string Scode, Ccode;               // code strings
    label L1, L2;                      // local labels
    if (look == WHILE) {
        match(WHILE);  match('(');
        L1 = new();  L2 = new();
        Ccode = C(next, L2);           // C.false = S.next, C.true = L2
        match(')');
        Scode = S(L1);                 // S1.next = L1
        return "label" || L1 || Ccode || "label" || L2 || Scode;
    }
    else { /* other statement types */ }
}
\end{lstlisting}
Each call of \texttt{S} or \texttt{C} returns the code of its own part, and the caller concatenates the pieces in the order of the SDD.
\end{examplebox}

\subsubsection{Method B: on-the-fly code generation}
Method A builds the whole code of a statement as one long string and returns it, so the code of a big program is copied over and over as it is returned upward. We can avoid this when the main attribute (here $S.code$) is a \emph{concatenation of the pieces produced by its children, in the same order as the children appear in the body, with fixed strings between them}. Then, instead of building the string, each piece is simply \textbf{printed at the moment it is known}; the order of the printing is the order of the concatenation. The SDT of Example~A3 changes in just two places: the construction of $S.code$ disappears and two \textit{print} actions are added.
\begin{align*}
S \to \mathbf{while}\ \tok{(}\ &\{\ L_1=\mathit{new}();\ L_2=\mathit{new}();\ \at{C}{false}=\at{S}{next};\ \at{C}{true}=L_2;\ \mathit{print}(\text{``label''},L_1)\ \}\ C\ \tok{)}\\
&\{\ \at{S_1}{next}=L_1;\ \mathit{print}(\text{``label''},L_2)\ \}\ S_1
\end{align*}
\begin{lstlisting}
void S(label next) {                   // S.code is not returned: it is printed
    label L1, L2;
    if (look == WHILE) {
        match(WHILE);  match('(');
        L1 = new();  L2 = new();
        print("label", L1);            // the first piece of S.code
        C(next, L2);                   // C prints its own code
        match(')');
        print("label", L2);            // the next piece
        S(L1);                         // S1 prints its own code
    }
    else { /* other statement types */ }
}
\end{lstlisting}
The output is the same as before, but no code string is ever copied. This is the same idea as the incremental translation with \textit{gen} of Section~\ref{sec:exprtac}.

\begin{pitfallbox}{When on-the-fly generation is not possible}
Printing is correct only if the pieces appear in the code in the same order as the symbols in the production body. If a rule used $S.code=S_2.code\cc S_1.code$ (the code of the \emph{later} symbol first), the parser would reach $S_1$ first and print its code too early. In that case Method A, which returns strings, is needed.
\end{pitfallbox}

\subsubsection{Method C: attributes on the stack of a table-driven LL parser}
A table-driven LL parser has no procedures to hold local variables, so the attribute values are kept on its \textbf{stack} in extra records. The stack is drawn with its top at the \emph{left}; the next thing to be done is the leftmost entry. The rules below (the book calls them action records and synthesize records) are all we need:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item The \textbf{inherited attributes} of a nonterminal $A$ are stored in the stack entry of $A$.
\item Every \textbf{action} of the SDT becomes an \emph{action record} pushed at its position in the body. An action that computes an inherited attribute of $X$ stands immediately above $X$, so it can write into the entry of $X$.
\item Every nonterminal $X$ in a body gets a \textbf{synthesize record} $X_s$ pushed immediately \emph{below} $X$. The action at the end of $X$'s body stores the synthesized attribute of $X$ there.
\item \textbf{Copying.} An action record holds copies of the values it needs. They are copied into it at three moments: when the head of the production is expanded (its inherited attributes), when a terminal is matched (its \textit{lexval}), and when a synthesize record reaches the top of the stack (it is popped and its content is copied into the action record below it).
\item A record at the top of the stack is processed by executing the action and popping it.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{examplebox}{Example C1: the \texttt{while} statement on the LL stack}
Take the on-the-fly SDT of Method B, whose production is $S\to\mathbf{while}\ \tok{(}\ a_1\ C\ \tok{)}\ a_2\ S_1$, where $a_1$ is the first action (creates $L_1,L_2$, sets $C$'s attributes) and $a_2$ is the second (sets $S_1.next$). Let $S.next=x$, and let the new labels be $L_1=y$ and $L_2=z$. Only inherited attributes occur, so there are no synthesize records.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.3cm} p{8.8cm} l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Moment} & \textbf{Stack (top at the left)} & \textbf{Output} \\ \midrule
Just before expanding $S$ & $S\,[next=x]\ \dots$ & \\
Immediately after expanding $S$ &
$\mathbf{while}\ \ \tok{(}\ \ [a_1:\ S.next=x,\ L_1,\ L_2]\ \ C\,[true,false]\ \ \tok{)}\ \ [a_2:\ L_1,\ L_2]\ \ S_1\,[next]\ \dots$ & \\
After \textbf{while}, \texttt{(} are matched and $a_1$ runs &
$C\,[true=z,\ false=x]\ \ \tok{)}\ \ [a_2:\ L_1=y,\ L_2=z]\ \ S_1\,[next]\ \dots$ & \texttt{label y} \\
After $C$ and \texttt{)} are processed and $a_2$ runs &
$S_1\,[next=y]\ \dots$ & \texttt{label z} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Note the copying: when $S$ is expanded, the entry of $S$ disappears, so its inherited attribute $S.next=x$ is first \emph{copied} into the record of $a_1$. Action $a_1$ then creates $y$ and $z$, writes $C$'s attributes into the entry of $C$ directly below it, and copies $y$ and $z$ into the record of $a_2$, which will need them later.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example C2: synthesize records for \texttt{9-5+2}}
Use the SDT of Example~1 in Section~\ref{sec:leftrec}, with the actions named:
\begin{align*}
E &\to T\ \{a_1:\ R.i=T.val\}\ R\ \{a_2:\ E.val=R.s\}\\
R &\to +\ T\ \{b_1:\ R_1.i=R.i+T.val\}\ R_1\ \{b_2:\ R.s=R_1.s\}\\
  &\mid\ -\ T\ \{b_1:\ R_1.i=R.i-T.val\}\ R_1\ \{b_2:\ R.s=R_1.s\}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{c:\ R.s=R.i\}\\
T &\to \mathbf{num}\ \{d:\ T.val=\mathbf{num}.lexval\}
\end{align*}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.2cm} p{7.8cm} l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Moment} & \textbf{Stack (top at the left)} & \textbf{Input} \\ \midrule
Start & $E\ E_s$ & \texttt{9-5+2} \\
$E$ expanded & $T\ T_s\ a_1\ R\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & \texttt{9-5+2} \\
$T$ done ($T_s{=}9$ copied into $a_1$), $a_1$ run & $R[i{=}9]\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & \texttt{-5+2} \\
$R$ expanded by $-$ ($R.i=9$ copied into $b_1$) & $-\ T\ T_s\ [b_1:\ R.i{=}9]\ R\ R_s\ b_2\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & \texttt{-5+2} \\
$T$ done ($T.val=5$), $b_1$ run: $R_1.i=9-5=4$ & $R[i{=}4]\ R_s\ b_2\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & \texttt{+2} \\
second $R$ expanded by $+$, $b_1$ run: $R_1.i=4+2=6$ & $R[i{=}6]\ R_s\ b_2\ R_s\ b_2\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & end \\
$R\to\varepsilon$, action $c$: $R.s=R.i$ & $R_s{=}6\ b_2\ R_s\ b_2\ R_s\ a_2\ E_s$ & end \\
each $b_2$ copies $R_1.s$ into its $R_s$, then $a_2$ & $E_s{=}6$ & end \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The inherited value $R.i$ moves \emph{right} through the action records ($9\to4\to6$), and the final $6$ moves \emph{left} through the synthesize records, one for each $R$, until it reaches $E_s$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{notebox}{Which method to use?}
Method A is the easiest to write and to read, and it handles any L-attributed SDD; the price is the strings that are returned. Method B is Method A without the strings and is the choice for code generation. Method C is what a generated, table-driven LL parser does; it is rarely written by hand. All three execute the same SDT, and all three need an L-attributed SDD: an inherited attribute that depends on a symbol to its right could not be evaluated, because that symbol has not been parsed yet.
\end{notebox}


\section{Translation of Expressions and Assignments}
%====================================================================

\subsection{SDD for three-address code}
\label{sec:exprtac}
We use the following:
\begin{itemize}
\item \at{E}{addr}: the address (name, constant, or temporary) that holds the value of $E$.
\item \at{E}{code} (or \at{S}{code}): the three-address instructions that evaluate $E$ (or execute $S$).
\item $\mathit{newTemp}()$ (written \textbf{new} \textit{Temp}): returns a fresh temporary $t_1, t_2,\dots$ in successive calls.
\item $\gen{\cdots}$: builds a three-address instruction. $\cc$ denotes concatenation of instruction sequences.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.3cm} L{11.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{id} = E\ ;$ &
$\at{S}{code} = \at{E}{code} \cc \gen{\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr}}$ \\
$E \to E_1 + E_2$ &
$\at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}()$\\ &
$\at{E}{code} = \at{E_1}{code} \cc \at{E_2}{code} \cc \gen{\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \tok{+}\ \at{E_2}{addr}}$ \\
$E \to E_1 * E_2$ &
$\at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}()$\\ &
$\at{E}{code} = \at{E_1}{code} \cc \at{E_2}{code} \cc \gen{\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \tok{*}\ \at{E_2}{addr}}$ \\
$E \to -\,E_1$ &
$\at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}()$\\ &
$\at{E}{code} = \at{E_1}{code} \cc \gen{\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \tok{minus}\ \at{E_1}{addr}}$ \\
$E \to (\,E_1\,)$ &
$\at{E}{addr} = \at{E_1}{addr}$;\quad $\at{E}{code} = \at{E_1}{code}$ \\
$E \to \mathbf{id}$ &
$\at{E}{addr} = \mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})$;\quad $\at{E}{code} = \tok{''}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{SDD to produce three-address code for expressions and assignments (Dragon Book Fig.~6.19).}
\label{tab:expr-sdd}
\end{table}

\begin{notebox}{Remark}
Here $\mathit{top}$ is the current symbol table; $\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\textit{name})$ returns the symbol-table entry. The code of a node is the \emph{concatenation} of its children's code followed by one new instruction, so the instructions are produced in postfix (evaluation) order. The $\at{}{code}$ attribute is a long string, so this SDD is inefficient in practice, which motivates the incremental approach below.
\end{notebox}

The grammar $E\to E_1+E_2\mid E_1*E_2\mid-E_1\mid(E_1)\mid\mathbf{id}$ is ambiguous. Precedence ($-$ highest, then $*$, then $+$) and left-associativity are supplied either by parser-generator declarations or by rewriting the grammar with levels $E, T, F$. This does not change the semantic rules: they are written once per \emph{operator}, whatever the grammar levels are called. The $*$ rule is the $+$ rule with the operator replaced; the rule for binary $-$ and for $/$ is identical again.

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: $a = b * -c + b * -c\ ;$}
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=1.5pt,align=center}]
 \node[nd] (S)  at (0,0) {$S$};
 \node[nd] (id) at (-3.5,-1.3) {\textbf{id}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a$}};
 \node[nd] (eq) at (-2,-1.3) {$=$};
 \node[nd] (E)  at (0,-1.3) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_5$}};
 \node[nd] (sc) at (2.8,-1.3) {$;$};
 \node[nd] (E1) at (-2.4,-3) {$E_1$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_2$}};
 \node[nd] (pl) at (0,-3) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (E2) at (2.4,-3) {$E_2$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_4$}};
 \node[nd] (b1) at (-3.6,-4.7) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b$}};
 \node[nd] (st1) at (-2.4,-4.7) {$*$};
 \node[nd] (m1) at (-0.9,-4.7) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t_1$}};
 \node[nd] (b2) at (1.2,-4.7) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b$}};
 \node[nd] (st2) at (2.4,-4.7) {$*$};
 \node[nd] (m2) at (3.9,-4.7) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $t_3$}};
 \node[nd] (c1) at (-0.9,-6.2) {$-\ E\ (c)$};
 \node[nd] (c2) at (3.9,-6.2) {$-\ E\ (c)$};
 \foreach \a/\b in {S/id,S/eq,S/E,S/sc,E/E1,E/pl,E/E2,E1/b1,E1/st1,E1/m1,E2/b2,E2/st2,E2/m2,m1/c1,m2/c2}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Concatenating the code in the order of evaluation gives:
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = minus c
t2 = b * t1
t3 = minus c
t4 = b * t3
t5 = t2 + t4
a  = t5
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Incremental translation (SDT with \texttt{gen} emitting directly)}
Building \at{E}{code} as a string copies code repeatedly. In an \emph{incremental} scheme, $\mathit{gen}()$ \textbf{appends} the instruction to an output stream/array as soon as the action is executed. The code attribute is not stored. Since actions run in the correct order (postfix), the instructions come out in the right order.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{2.6cm} L{12cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Translation scheme (actions in braces)} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{id} = E\ ;$ & $\{\ \mathit{gen}(\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr});\ \}$ \\
$E \to E_1 + E_2$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \tok{+}\ \at{E_2}{addr});\ \}$ \\
$\ \ \mid\ E_1 * E_2$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \tok{*}\ \at{E_2}{addr});\ \}$ \\
$\ \ \mid\ -E_1$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr} = \mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \tok{minus}\ \at{E_1}{addr});\ \}$ \\
$\ \ \mid\ (E_1)$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr} = \at{E_1}{addr};\ \}$ \\
$\ \ \mid\ \mathbf{id}$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr} = \mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme});\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Incremental SDT for expressions (all actions are at the right end, so it is a postfix SDT).}
\end{table}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example (incremental SDT): \texttt{x = a * b + c * d ;}}
The rules are executed in post-order, from left to right, and every rule that creates a temporary emits \emph{one} instruction at the moment its node is completed. The rules $E\to\mathbf{id}$ only set \textit{addr} and emit nothing. The red numbers show the order in which instructions are emitted.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=1.5pt,align=center},
  no/.style={circle,fill=red!70!black,text=white,inner sep=1pt,minimum size=4mm,font=\scriptsize\bfseries}]
 \node[nd] (S)  at (0,0) {$S$};
 \node[nd] (id) at (-4.2,-1.4) {\textbf{id}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $x$}};
 \node[nd] (eq) at (-2.6,-1.4) {$=$};
 \node[nd] (E)  at (0,-1.4) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_3$}};
 \node[nd] (sc) at (3.4,-1.4) {$;$};
 \node[nd] (E1) at (-2.6,-3.2) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_1$}};
 \node[nd] (pl) at (0,-3.2) {$+$};
 \node[nd] (E2) at (2.6,-3.2) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $addr=t_2$}};
 \node[nd] (a) at (-4.0,-5) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $a$}};
 \node[nd] (m1) at (-2.6,-5) {$*$};
 \node[nd] (b) at (-1.2,-5) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $b$}};
 \node[nd] (c) at (1.2,-5) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $c$}};
 \node[nd] (m2) at (2.6,-5) {$*$};
 \node[nd] (d) at (4.0,-5) {$E$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $d$}};
 \foreach \a/\b in {S/id,S/eq,S/E,S/sc,E/E1,E/pl,E/E2,E1/a,E1/m1,E1/b,E2/c,E2/m2,E2/d}
   \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \node[no] at (-3.7,-3.2) {1};
 \node[no] at (3.6,-3.2) {2};
 \node[no] at (1.3,-1.4) {3};
 \node[no] at (0.5,0) {4};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Parse tree of \texttt{x = a * b + c * d ;} with the order of emission.}
\end{figure}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{\#} & \textbf{Node completed} & \textbf{Instruction emitted} \\ \midrule
1 & $E\to E_1*E_2$ \ (\texttt{a * b}) & \texttt{t1 = a * b} \\
2 & $E\to E_1*E_2$ \ (\texttt{c * d}) & \texttt{t2 = c * d} \\
3 & $E\to E_1+E_2$ & \texttt{t3 = t1 + t2} \\
4 & $S\to\mathbf{id}=E\,;$ & \texttt{x = t3} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Note that \texttt{c * d} is completed before the $+$ node: precedence makes $*$ bind tighter, so \texttt{c * d} is a deeper node of the tree than the $+$.
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Addressing array elements}
Let an array be stored in a contiguous block of memory with base address $\mathit{base}$ and element width $w$.

\paragraph{One-dimensional array} $A[\mathit{low}\ \dots\ \mathit{high}]$:
\[
\text{address of } A[i] \;=\; \mathit{base} + (i - \mathit{low}) \times w
\]
If $\mathit{low}=0$ this is simply $\mathit{base} + i \times w$.

\paragraph{Two-dimensional array} (row-major) $A[n_1][n_2]$ with element width $w$:
\[
\text{address of } A[i_1][i_2] \;=\; \mathit{base} + (i_1 \times n_2 + i_2) \times w
\]
\paragraph{$k$ dimensions} $A[n_1]\cdots[n_k]$ (row-major, 0-based):
\[
\mathit{base} + \bigl( (\cdots((i_1 n_2 + i_2) n_3 + i_3) \cdots ) n_k + i_k \bigr)\times w
\]
Equivalently, using the \emph{widths of sub-arrays} $w_1 = n_2 \cdots n_k \cdot w,\ w_2 = n_3\cdots n_k \cdot w, \dots,\ w_k = w$:
\[
\mathit{base} + i_1 \times w_1 + i_2 \times w_2 + \cdots + i_k \times w_k.
\]
This form is the one used in the translation scheme.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  cell/.style={draw,minimum width=1.9cm,minimum height=0.8cm,font=\small}]
 \foreach \i/\n/\o in {0/a[0][0]/0, 1/a[0][1]/4, 2/a[0][2]/8, 3/a[1][0]/12, 4/a[1][1]/16, 5/a[1][2]/20} {
   \node[cell,fill=blue!8] at (\i*1.9,0) {\n};
   \node[font=\scriptsize,below] at (\i*1.9,-0.5) {\o};
 }
 \foreach \i in {3,4,5} \node[cell,fill=green!10] at (\i*1.9,0) {};
 \foreach \i/\n in {3/a[1][0],4/a[1][1],5/a[1][2]} \node[font=\small] at (\i*1.9,0) {\n};
 \draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt}] (-0.95,0.55)--(4.75,0.55) node[midway,above=6pt,font=\small]{row 0 \ ($i_1=0$)};
 \draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt}] (4.85,0.55)--(10.45,0.55) node[midway,above=6pt,font=\small]{row 1 \ ($i_1=1$)};
 \node[font=\small,anchor=east] at (-1.1,-0.5) {offset:};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Row-major layout of \texttt{int a[2][3]} ($w=4$). Offset of $a[i][j]$ is $i\times 12 + j\times 4$.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Translation of array references}
Grammar (with nonterminal $L$ for an array location):
\begin{align*}
S &\to \mathbf{id} = E\ ;\ \mid\ L = E\ ;\\
E &\to E_1 + E_2\ \mid\ \mathbf{id}\ \mid\ L\\
L &\to \mathbf{id}\,[\,E\,]\ \mid\ L_1\,[\,E\,]
\end{align*}
Attributes of $L$: \at{L}{array} (symbol-table entry of the array), \at{L}{type} (type of the \emph{sub-array} generated by $L$), \at{L}{addr} (a temporary accumulating the \emph{offset}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{2.4cm} L{12.2cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic action} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{id}=E\ ;$ & $\{\ \mathit{gen}(\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$S \to L=E\ ;$ & $\{\ \mathit{gen}(\at{L}{array}.\mathit{base}\ \tok{[}\ \at{L}{addr}\ \tok{]}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$E \to E_1+E_2$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \tok{+}\ \at{E_2}{addr});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$E \to \mathbf{id}$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr}=\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$E \to L$ & $\{\ \at{E}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{L}{array}.\mathit{base}\ \tok{[}\ \at{L}{addr}\ \tok{]});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$L \to \mathbf{id}\,[\,E\,]$ & $\{\ \at{L}{array}=\mathit{top}.\mathit{get}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme});\ \at{L}{type}=\at{L}{array}.\mathit{type}.\mathit{elem};$\\
 & $\ \ \at{L}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{L}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr}\ \tok{*}\ \at{L}{type}.\mathit{width});\ \}$ \\[2pt]
$L \to L_1\,[\,E\,]$ & $\{\ \at{L}{array}=\at{L_1}{array};\ \at{L}{type}=\at{L_1}{type}.\mathit{elem};$\\
 & $\ \ t=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \at{L}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();$\\
 & $\ \ \mathit{gen}(t\ \tok{=}\ \at{E}{addr}\ \tok{*}\ \at{L}{type}.\mathit{width});\ \mathit{gen}(\at{L}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \at{L_1}{addr}\ \tok{+}\ t);\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Semantic actions for array references (Dragon Book Fig.~6.22).}
\end{table}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: $c + a[i][j]$ where \texttt{a} is \texttt{int a[2][3]}}
Types: \at{a}{type} $= \mathit{array}(2,\ \mathit{array}(3,\ \mathit{integer}))$, so the width of one row is $3\times 4 = 12$ and of an element is $4$.

\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $L \to \mathbf{id}[E]$ with $E = i$: $\at{L}{type}=\mathit{array}(3,\mathit{integer})$, width 12 \;$\Rightarrow$\; \texttt{t1 = i * 12}.
\item $L \to L_1[E]$ with $E = j$: $\at{L}{type}=\mathit{integer}$, width 4 \;$\Rightarrow$\; \texttt{t2 = j * 4}, \texttt{t3 = t1 + t2}.
\item $E \to L$: \texttt{t4 = a[t3]}.
\item $E \to E_1 + E_2$: \texttt{t5 = c + t4}.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = i * 12
t2 = j * 4
t3 = t1 + t2
t4 = a[t3]
t5 = c + t4
\end{lstlisting}

And for the assignment $x = a[i][j]$ the code is the first four instructions followed by \texttt{x = t4}.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: a three-dimensional array and a store}
Let \texttt{int a[2][3][4]} (so $w=4$). The widths of the sub-arrays are $w_3=4$ (one element), $w_2=4\cdot4=16$ (one innermost row of 4 elements) and $w_1=3\cdot4\cdot4=48$ (one $3\times4$ plane); the whole array occupies $2\cdot48=96$ bytes. The statement \texttt{x = a[i][j][k];} becomes
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = i * 48
t2 = j * 16
t3 = t1 + t2
t4 = k * 4
t5 = t3 + t4
t6 = a[t5]
x  = t6
\end{lstlisting}
Each extra subscript adds exactly two instructions (a multiplication and an addition), as in the rule for $L\to L_1[E]$. For a \emph{store}, \texttt{a[i][j] = x;} with \texttt{int a[2][3]} uses the rule $S\to L=E\,;$, which ends with an indexed assignment instead of an indexed load:
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = i * 12
t2 = j * 4
t3 = t1 + t2
a[t3] = x
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Index versus offset}
In \texttt{t4 = a[t3]} the value in \texttt{t3} is a \emph{byte offset} from the base of \texttt{a}, not an element index as in the C source. That is why every subscript is multiplied by the width of the thing it selects. (In the book's three-address code, \texttt{x = y[i]} means ``the value at the memory location $i$ units beyond $y$''.)
\end{pitfallbox}

%====================================================================
\section{Type Checking and Type Conversion}
%====================================================================

\paragraph{Type expressions} represent the type of a language construct: basic types (\texttt{int}, \texttt{float}, \texttt{char}, \texttt{boolean}, \texttt{void}), \textit{array}$(n, T)$, \textit{record}, pointer, and function types $s \to t$.

\paragraph{Type checking} comes in two forms: \textbf{synthesis} (the type of an expression is built from the types of its sub-expressions, e.g., for $E_1 + E_2$) and \textbf{inference} (the type of a construct is determined from the way it is used).

\begin{examplebox}{Example: three static checks}
Static checking attaches a rule to a production and reports an error when the rule fails. With \texttt{int a[10]; int n; float f;}:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l L{5.6cm} L{4.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Source} & \textbf{Rule that fails} & \textbf{Message} \\ \midrule
\texttt{x = a[2.5];} & in $L\to\mathbf{id}[E]$ the index $E$ must have type \texttt{int} & array index must be an integer \\
\texttt{y = a + n;} & $+$ is defined for numeric types only; $a$ has type $\mathit{array}(10,\texttt{int})$ & operand of $+$ is not a number \\
\texttt{if (f) x = 1;} & the condition in \texttt{if (}$E$\texttt{)} must have type \texttt{boolean} & \texttt{float} used as condition (C would accept it and test $f\ne0$) \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Each check is just one more semantic rule: it computes nothing, it only compares attribute values and calls an error routine, which is another example of a rule with a side effect.
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Type conversion (coercion)}
For $E \to E_1 + E_2$ where operand types may differ (e.g., \texttt{int} and \texttt{float}), the compiler inserts conversions. Conversions that cannot lose information in principle (\emph{widening}) may be inserted by the compiler automatically (\emph{coercion}); the reverse direction (\emph{narrowing}) needs an explicit cast written by the programmer. Figure~\ref{fig:typehier} shows the usual hierarchy. In these notes we use only \texttt{int} $<$ \texttt{float}.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  t/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,minimum width=1.5cm,minimum height=0.7cm,font=\small\ttfamily,fill=blue!6},
  wide/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.5mm]},thick,green!45!black},
  narrow/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.5mm]},dashed,red!70!black}]
 \node[t] (by) at (0,0) {byte};
 \node[t] (sh) at (2.3,0) {short};
 \node[t] (in) at (4.6,0) {int};
 \node[t] (lo) at (6.9,0) {long};
 \node[t] (fl) at (9.2,0) {float};
 \node[t] (do) at (11.5,0) {double};
 \node[t] (ch) at (4.6,1.4) {char};
 \foreach \a/\b in {by/sh,sh/in,in/lo,lo/fl,fl/do} \draw[wide] (\a)--(\b);
 \draw[wide] (ch)--(in);
 \draw[narrow] (do.south) to[bend left=25] node[below,font=\scriptsize]{narrowing: explicit cast, may lose information} (by.south);
 \node[font=\scriptsize,green!45!black,anchor=south] at (7.9,0.45) {widening: automatic};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Widening (solid) and narrowing (dashed) conversions between primitive types, Java-style. Converting \texttt{long} to \texttt{float} is allowed implicitly although a few low-order digits may be rounded.}
\label{fig:typehier}
\end{figure}
For $E \to E_1 + E_2$ (and likewise for $-$, $*$, $/$) the result has the \emph{wider} of the two operand types,
\[
\at{E}{type} = \max(\at{E_1}{type},\ \at{E_2}{type}),
\]
and the helper $\mathit{widen}(a, t, w)$ converts address $a$ of type $t$ to type $w$:

\begin{lstlisting}
Addr widen(Addr a, Type t, Type w) {
    if (t == w) return a;
    else if (t == integer && w == float) {
        temp = new Temp();
        gen(temp '=' '(float)' a);
        return temp;
    }
    else error;
}
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.1cm} L{11.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic action} \\ \midrule
$E \to E_1 + E_2$ &
$\{\ \at{E}{type}=\max(\at{E_1}{type},\at{E_2}{type});$\\
& $\ \ a_1=\mathit{widen}(\at{E_1}{addr},\at{E_1}{type},\at{E}{type});$\\
& $\ \ a_2=\mathit{widen}(\at{E_2}{addr},\at{E_2}{type},\at{E}{type});$\\
& $\ \ \at{E}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ a_1\ \tok{+}\ a_2);\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{examplebox}{Example}
Let \texttt{x} be \texttt{float}, and \texttt{i, j} be \texttt{int}. For $x = i * j + x$ we get:
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = i * j          // int * int -> int
t2 = (float) t1     // widen to float
t3 = t2 + x         // float + float
x  = t3
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: widening on \emph{both} sides, $x = i + j * x$}
Let \texttt{x} be \texttt{float} and \texttt{i, j} be \texttt{int}. The code is emitted when each node is completed, so the product $j*x$ (completed first) comes before the sum:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $E\to E_1*E_2$ with $E_1=j$ (\texttt{int}) and $E_2=x$ (\texttt{float}): $E.type=\texttt{float}$; $\mathit{widen}(j)$ emits \texttt{t1 = (float) j}; $\mathit{widen}(x)$ does nothing; then \texttt{t2 = t1 * x}.
\item $E\to E_1+E_2$ with $E_1=i$ (\texttt{int}) and $E_2$ of type \texttt{float}: $\mathit{widen}(i)$ emits \texttt{t3 = (float) i}; then \texttt{t4 = t3 + t2}.
\item $S\to\mathbf{id}=E\,;$ emits \texttt{x = t4}.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = (float) j
t2 = t1 * x
t3 = (float) i
t4 = t3 + t2
x  = t4
\end{lstlisting}
Note that $i$, which is written first, is converted \emph{after} $j$: the order of the instructions follows the order in which the nodes are completed (post-order), not the order of the source text.
\end{examplebox}

%====================================================================
\section{Control Flow: Boolean Expressions and Statements}
%====================================================================

In programming languages, boolean expressions are used for two purposes:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item to \textbf{compute logical values} (e.g., \texttt{flag = a < b \&\& c;}), and
\item as \textbf{conditional expressions} in \texttt{if}, \texttt{while}, and \texttt{for} (flow-of-control).
\end{enumerate}
We use the grammar
\[
B \to B \,|\!|\, B \ \mid\ B\ \&\&\ B\ \mid\ !\,B\ \mid\ (B)\ \mid\ E\ \mathit{rel}\ E\ \mid\ \mathbf{true}\ \mid\ \mathbf{false}
\]
with $|\!|$ standing for the C operator \texttt{||}; \texttt{||} and \texttt{\&\&} are left-associative, \texttt{||} has lower precedence than \texttt{\&\&}, which is lower than \texttt{!}.

\subsection{Start here: a tiny example with labels}
\label{sec:labelintro}

\paragraph{The story.} A computer follows the instructions of a program one after the other, like a child walking down a corridor with many numbered doors. A \texttt{goto} instruction says: ``do not go on to the next door; run to the door with this name instead''. An \texttt{if} test says: ``if the answer is yes, run to door \emph{T}; if no, run to door \emph{F}''. The names of the doors are called \textbf{labels}.

The compiler has a problem: before it can write ``run to door \emph{T}'', it must \emph{know the name} of door \emph{T}. The scheme of this section solves it like a school with a head teacher: \textbf{the boss decides the door names first and tells each worker}, and only then does the worker write its instructions.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{11.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Word} & \textbf{Meaning in plain language} \\ \midrule
label & The name of a door (an instruction), written $L_1, L_2, \dots$ \\
$\mathit{newlabel}()$ & A ticket machine: each press prints a brand-new, unused door name. \\
$\mathit{label}(L)$ & Fix the name tag $L$ on the \emph{next} instruction that is written, so that jumps to $L$ arrive there. \\
\at{S}{next} & A sticky note on the statement $S$ that says: ``the door \emph{right after you} is called \dots''. \\
\at{B}{true} & A sticky note on the test $B$: ``if you are \textbf{true}, run to door \dots''. \\
\at{B}{false} & A sticky note on the test $B$: ``if you are \textbf{false}, run to door \dots''. \\
code & The list of instructions that a piece of the program turns into. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
These three sticky notes (\at{S}{next}, \at{B}{true}, \at{B}{false}) are the \emph{key attributes} of the whole section. They are \textbf{inherited}: they are written by the \emph{parent} and read by the child. That is exactly the boss telling the worker where the exits are, \emph{before} the worker starts.

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1: \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1;} and then the program ends}
The rules used (they are given in full in the next subsections) are
\begin{align*}
P &\to S: && \at{S}{next}=\mathit{newlabel}(),\qquad \at{P}{code}=\at{S}{code}\cc\mathit{label}(\at{S}{next})\\
S &\to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1: && \at{B}{true}=\mathit{newlabel}(),\ \ \at{B}{false}=\at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next},\\
  & && \at{S}{code}=\at{B}{code}\cc\mathit{label}(\at{B}{true})\cc\at{S_1}{code}\\
B &\to E_1\ \mathit{rel}\ E_2: && \at{B}{code}=\text{``\texttt{if} $E_1$ \textit{rel} $E_2$ \texttt{goto} \at{B}{true}''}\cc\text{``\texttt{goto} \at{B}{false}''}
\end{align*}
\textbf{Order of events} (first downward for the sticky notes, then upward for the code):
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $P\to S$ presses the ticket machine: $L_1$. It sticks \at{S}{next}$=L_1$ on $S$: ``after you comes door $L_1$, the end of the program''.
\item $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1$ presses the machine again: $L_2$. It sticks \at{B}{true}$=L_2$ on $B$, and copies its own note $L_1$ onto $B$ as \at{B}{false} and onto $S_1$ as \at{S_1}{next}.
\item $B$ now \emph{knows} both door names and writes its two jumps. $S_1$ (the assignment) writes \texttt{x = 1}.
\item $S$ puts its pieces together in order, inserting the name tag $L_2$ in front of $S_1$'s code. Finally $P$ appends the name tag $L_1$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.9\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=3pt,align=center},
  note/.style={font=\scriptsize,align=center},
  dn/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},blue!70!black,thick,dashed},
  up/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},red!70!black,thick}]
 \node[nd] (P)  at (0,0)     {$P$};
 \node[nd] (S)  at (0,-2.0)  {$S$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{next}=L_1$}};
 \node[nd] (B)  at (-3.8,-4.4) {$B$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{true}=L_2$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{false}=L_1$}};
 \node[nd] (S1) at (3.8,-4.4)  {$S_1$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{next}=L_1$}};
 \node[nd] (e1) at (-3.8,-6.4) {\texttt{a < b}};
 \node[nd] (e2) at (3.8,-6.4)  {\texttt{x = 1}};
 \node[nd] (kw) at (-1.0,-4.4) {\texttt{if (}};
 \node[nd] (cp) at (1.2,-4.4)  {\texttt{)}};
 \foreach \a/\b in {P/S,S/B,S/S1,S/kw,S/cp,B/e1,S1/e2} \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 % notes going down (blue) and code coming up (red)
 \draw[dn] ([xshift=-4mm]P.south) -- ([xshift=-4mm]S.north);
 \node[note,text=blue!70!black,anchor=east] at (-0.7,-1.0) {$P$ presses the machine: $L_1$};
 \draw[dn] ([xshift=-5mm,yshift=-1mm]S.south west) -- ([xshift=-1mm,yshift=2mm]B.north);
 \node[note,text=blue!70!black,anchor=east] at (-4.6,-3.0) {$S$ presses the machine: $L_2$\\and tells $B$ both names};
 \draw[dn] ([xshift=5mm,yshift=-1mm]S.south east) -- ([xshift=1mm,yshift=2mm]S1.north);
 \node[note,text=blue!70!black,anchor=west] at (3.1,-2.8) {tells $S_1$:\\``your next is $L_1$''};
 \draw[up] ([xshift=4mm]S.north) -- ([xshift=4mm]P.south);
 \node[note,text=red!70!black,anchor=west] at (0.7,-1.0) {$S.code$ comes up};
 \draw[up] ([xshift=3mm,yshift=-1mm]e1.north) -- ([xshift=3mm,yshift=1mm]B.south);
 \node[note,text=red!70!black,anchor=east] at (-4.4,-5.4) {\texttt{if a<b goto L2}\\\texttt{goto L1}};
 \draw[up] ([xshift=3mm,yshift=-1mm]e2.north) -- ([xshift=3mm,yshift=1mm]S1.south);
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{The sticky notes of Example~1. \textcolor{blue!70!black}{Blue dashed arrows:} the door names travel \emph{down}, from the parent to its children, before any instruction is written (inherited attributes). \textcolor{red!70!black}{Red arrows:} the instructions travel \emph{up} and are put together in order (synthesized attribute \textit{code}).}
\label{fig:labelintro}
\end{figure}

\textbf{The generated code, instruction by instruction.} The right column tells you which sticky note each instruction needed, who had written it, and when:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{10.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{\ \ \ \ if a < b goto L2} &
 \at{B}{true}$=L_2$. Set by the parent rule $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1$, \emph{before} $B$ wrote any code. Used here: ``if the test is true, run to $L_2$''. \\[4pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} &
 \at{B}{false}$=L_1$. This is a \emph{copy} of \at{S}{next}$=L_1$, which came from $P\to S$. Used here: ``if the test is false, run to $L_1$, behind the whole \textbf{if}''. \\[4pt]
\texttt{L2: x = 1} &
 The name tag $L_2=\at{B}{true}$ is stuck on this instruction by the rule of $S$, with $\mathit{label}(\at{B}{true})$. So the true jump above arrives exactly at the beginning of the then part. The statement $S_1$ was told \at{S_1}{next}$=L_1$, but an assignment never jumps, so it does not use it. \\[4pt]
\texttt{L1:} &
 The name tag $L_1=\at{S}{next}$ is stuck on the instruction that follows by $P\to S$, with $\mathit{label}(\at{S}{next})$. Nothing follows here (the program ends), but the tag must exist, because \texttt{goto L1} refers to it. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to remember:} a door name is \emph{invented once} (by $\mathit{newlabel}()$), then \emph{handed down} by copying, and finally \emph{fixed on exactly one instruction} by $\mathit{label}(\dots)$. Every jump that mentions the name arrives at that instruction.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 2: \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1; else x = 2;} and then the program ends}
The rule is $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mathbf{else}\ S_2$. Two new door names are needed this time, one for each branch: \at{B}{true}$=\mathit{newlabel}()=L_2$ and \at{B}{false}$=\mathit{newlabel}()=L_3$. Both statements are told \at{S_1}{next}$=\at{S_2}{next}=\at{S}{next}=L_1$.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{10.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{\ \ \ \ if a < b goto L2} & \at{B}{true}$=L_2$, new, set by the $\mathbf{if}$-$\mathbf{else}$ rule. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L3} & \at{B}{false}$=L_3$, also new now: false must go to the \textbf{else} part, not behind the statement. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L2: x = 1} & Tag $L_2$ is fixed here by $\mathit{label}(\at{B}{true})$: the beginning of the then part. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} & \emph{Generated by the rule itself:} after the then part we must run \emph{over} the else part. The destination is \at{S}{next}$=L_1$, the note that came from $P\to S$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L3: x = 2} & Tag $L_3$ is fixed here by $\mathit{label}(\at{B}{false})$: the beginning of the else part. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L1:} & Tag $L_1$ is fixed by $P\to S$, behind everything. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to notice:} compare with Example~1. There, \at{B}{false} was a \emph{copy} of \at{S}{next}. Here it is a \emph{new} name, because the false branch now has work to do (the else part). The \texttt{goto L1} in the middle is the reason \at{S}{next} has to be known to $S$ itself.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 3: a loop, \texttt{while (a < b) x = x + 1;} and then the program ends}
The rule is $S\to\mathbf{while}\,(B)\,S_1$. It uses a \emph{local} name $\mathit{begin}$ (not an attribute): $\mathit{begin}=\mathit{newlabel}()=L_2$ marks the top of the loop. Also \at{B}{true}$=\mathit{newlabel}()=L_3$, \at{B}{false}$=\at{S}{next}=L_1$, and \at{S_1}{next}$=\mathit{begin}=L_2$: ``when the body is finished, you must run back to the top''.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{10.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{L2: if a < b goto L3} & Tag $L_2=\mathit{begin}$ is fixed here by $\mathit{label}(\mathit{begin})$: the \emph{top of the loop}, where we come back to. \at{B}{true}$=L_3$ is the body. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} & \at{B}{false}$=L_1$ is a copy of \at{S}{next}: the way out of the loop. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L3: x = x + 1} & Tag $L_3=\at{B}{true}$, the start of the body. The body was told \at{S_1}{next}$=L_2$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L2} & \emph{Generated by the rule itself} with $\mathit{gen}(\texttt{goto}\ \mathit{begin})$: after the body, go back to the test. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L1:} & Tag $L_1=\at{S}{next}$, fixed by $P\to S$. This is where the loop exits. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to notice:} the loop needs one more name than the \textbf{if}: $\mathit{begin}$, the door to which the body jumps \emph{back}. The sticky note \at{S_1}{next}$=\mathit{begin}$ is how the body learns it.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{notebox}{The key idea, and what comes later}
In this scheme \textbf{every jump target is a name that someone has already invented and handed down} through the inherited attributes \at{S}{next}, \at{B}{true} and \at{B}{false}. The code is assembled from the leaves upward, but the \emph{names} are passed \emph{downward}. This works because a name can be handed down before the code of the children is written. When the target cannot be known in advance, the notes use \textbf{backpatching} (Section~\ref{sec:backpatch}): leave the jump blank, and fill the blank when the target becomes known. Section~\ref{sec:backpatch} starts with similar small examples (an \textbf{if}, an \textbf{if}-\textbf{else} and a \textbf{while}), so you can compare the two schemes side by side.
\end{notebox}


\subsection{Short-circuit (jumping) code}
In \textbf{short-circuit code}, the operators \texttt{\&\&}, \texttt{||} and \texttt{!} do not appear in the generated code. Instead a boolean expression is translated into jumps to labels: control \emph{reaches} one position if the expression is true and another if it is false. In
\[
\texttt{if (x < 100 || x > 200 \&\& x != y) x = 0;}
\]
the first test \texttt{x<100} being true makes the other tests unnecessary. The generated code is:

\begin{lstlisting}
        if x < 100 goto L2
        goto L3
L3:     if x > 200 goto L4
        goto L1
L4:     if x != y goto L2
        goto L1
L2:     x = 0
L1:     ...
\end{lstlisting}
(Compare with \emph{value} code, which would compute 0/1 into temporaries and use \texttt{\&\&} and \texttt{||} at run time.)

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  t/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,minimum width=2.1cm,minimum height=0.8cm,fill=blue!6,font=\small\ttfamily},
  o/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,minimum width=2.1cm,minimum height=0.8cm,fill=green!12,font=\small\ttfamily},
  e/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.3mm]},thick},
  yes/.style={font=\scriptsize\bfseries,text=green!45!black,fill=white,inner sep=1pt},
  no/.style={font=\scriptsize\bfseries,text=red!70!black,fill=white,inner sep=1pt}]
 \node[t] (a) at (0,0) {x < 100};
 \node[t] (b) at (3.6,0) {x > 200};
 \node[t] (c) at (7.2,0) {x != y};
 \node[o] (s) at (7.2,-2.4) {x = 0};
 \node[draw,circle,minimum size=0.9cm,fill=gray!15,font=\small] (x) at (10.8,0) {exit};
 \draw[e] (a) -- node[no]{false} (b);
 \draw[e] (b) -- node[yes]{true} (c);
 \draw[e] (c) -- node[no]{false} (x);
 \draw[e] (c) -- node[yes]{true} (s);
 \draw[e] (a.south) to[bend right=25] node[yes,pos=0.45]{true} (s.west);
 \draw[e] (b.north) to[bend left=40] node[no,pos=0.5]{false} (x.north);
 \draw[e] (s.east) to[bend right=25] (x.south);
 \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=east] at (3.2,-0.7) {label \texttt{L3}};
 \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=east] at (6.8,-0.7) {label \texttt{L4}};
 \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=west] at (7.35,-3.0) {label \texttt{L2}};
 \node[font=\scriptsize,anchor=west] at (11.3,0) {label \texttt{L1}};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Control-flow view of \texttt{if (x < 100 || x > 200 \&\& x != y) x = 0;}. Each test jumps on its own outcome; no boolean value is ever computed. A \emph{true} arrow out of $x<100$ skips the other tests.}
\end{figure}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Short-circuit evaluation is a semantic guarantee, not only an optimisation}
In \texttt{if (p != NULL \&\& p->x > 0) \dots} the second operand must \emph{not} be evaluated when $p$ is null, otherwise the program dereferences a null pointer. Jumping code gives this behaviour for free: the test of \texttt{p->x} is only reached through the \emph{true} exit of \texttt{p != NULL}. Value code that first computes both operands would crash.
\end{pitfallbox}

\paragraph{Boolean values in assignments.} A boolean expression is also used to compute a \emph{value}, e.g.\ \texttt{flag = a < b \&\& c;}. The jumping code is reused, and the two exits set the variable:
\begin{lstlisting}
        if a < b goto L1
        goto L3
L1:     if c goto L2
        goto L3
L2:     flag = true
        goto L4
L3:     flag = false
L4:
\end{lstlisting}

\subsection{Flow-of-control statements: SDD with inherited labels}
Grammar for statements:
\[
P \to S, \qquad S \to \mathbf{assign}\ \mid\ \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mid\ \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mathbf{else}\ S_2\ \mid\ \mathbf{while}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mid\ S_1\,S_2
\]
\textbf{Inherited attributes} (labels):
\begin{itemize}
\item \at{B}{true}: the label to jump to if $B$ is true; \at{B}{false}: the label if $B$ is false.
\item \at{S}{next}: the label of the instruction that follows the code for $S$.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Synthesized attribute}: \at{S}{code}, \at{B}{code}. The function $\mathit{newlabel}()$ creates a fresh label $L_1, L_2, \dots$, and $\lbl{L}$ attaches label $L$ to the next instruction.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.6cm} L{11cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$P \to S$ &
$\at{S}{next}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{P}{code}=\at{S}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{S}{next}}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{assign}$ &
$\at{S}{code}=\at{\mathbf{assign}}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1$ &
$\at{B}{true}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{B}{false}=\at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next}$\\
& $\at{S}{code}=\at{B}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B}{true}}\cc\at{S_1}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mathbf{else}\ S_2$ &
$\at{B}{true}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{B}{false}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{S_1}{next}=\at{S_2}{next}=\at{S}{next}$\\
& $\at{S}{code}=\at{B}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B}{true}}\cc\at{S_1}{code}\cc\gen{\goto\ \at{S}{next}}\cc\lbl{\at{B}{false}}\cc\at{S_2}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{while}\,(B)\,S_1$ &
$\mathit{begin}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{B}{true}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{B}{false}=\at{S}{next}$;\quad $\at{S_1}{next}=\mathit{begin}$\\
& $\at{S}{code}=\lbl{\mathit{begin}}\cc\at{B}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B}{true}}\cc\at{S_1}{code}\cc\gen{\goto\ \mathit{begin}}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to S_1\,S_2$ &
$\at{S_1}{next}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\quad $\at{S_2}{next}=\at{S}{next}$\\
& $\at{S}{code}=\at{S_1}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{S_1}{next}}\cc\at{S_2}{code}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{SDD for flow-of-control statements (Dragon Book Fig.~6.36). \at{S}{next}, \at{B}{true}, and \at{B}{false} are inherited.}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  blk/.style={draw,minimum width=4.2cm,minimum height=0.75cm,font=\small,fill=blue!6},
  lab/.style={font=\footnotesize\ttfamily,anchor=east}]
 % if-else
 \node[blk] (b) at (0,0) {$B.code$};
 \node[blk] (s1) at (0,-0.75) {$S_1.code$};
 \node[blk,fill=orange!15] (g) at (0,-1.5) {\texttt{goto S.next}};
 \node[blk] (s2) at (0,-2.25) {$S_2.code$};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,-0.75) {B.true:};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,-2.25) {B.false:};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,-3.0) {S.next:};
 \node[font=\small] at (0,-3.9) {(a) \textbf{if} (B) $S_1$ \textbf{else} $S_2$};
 % while
 \begin{scope}[xshift=8.6cm]
 \node[blk] (wb) at (0,0) {$B.code$};
 \node[blk] (ws) at (0,-0.75) {$S_1.code$};
 \node[blk,fill=orange!15] (wg) at (0,-1.5) {\texttt{goto begin}};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,0) {begin:};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,-0.75) {B.true:};
 \node[lab] at (-2.2,-2.0) {B.false/S.next:};
 \node[font=\small] at (0,-3.9) {(b) \textbf{while} (B) $S_1$};
 \end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Layout of the generated code. $B.code$ contains jumps to \textit{B.true} or \textit{B.false}; it ``falls through'' to neither, so the labels can be placed anywhere.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{SDD for boolean expressions (jumping code)}
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.1cm} L{11.5cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$B \to B_1 \,|\!|\, B_2$ &
$\at{B_1}{true}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B_1}{false}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{true}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{false}=\at{B}{false}$\\
& $\at{B}{code}=\at{B_1}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B_1}{false}}\cc\at{B_2}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to B_1\ \&\&\ B_2$ &
$\at{B_1}{true}=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\ \ $\at{B_1}{false}=\at{B}{false}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{true}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{false}=\at{B}{false}$\\
& $\at{B}{code}=\at{B_1}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B_1}{true}}\cc\at{B_2}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to !\,B_1$ &
$\at{B_1}{true}=\at{B}{false}$;\ \ $\at{B_1}{false}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B}{code}=\at{B_1}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to E_1\ \mathit{rel}\ E_2$ &
$\at{B}{code}=\at{E_1}{code}\cc\at{E_2}{code}\cc\gen{\mathbf{if}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \mathit{rel.op}\ \at{E_2}{addr}\ \goto\ \at{B}{true}}\cc\gen{\goto\ \at{B}{false}}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to \mathbf{true}$ & $\at{B}{code}=\gen{\goto\ \at{B}{true}}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to \mathbf{false}$ & $\at{B}{code}=\gen{\goto\ \at{B}{false}}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{SDD to generate jumping code for boolean expressions (Dragon Book Fig.~6.37).}
\end{table}

\begin{notebox}{How to remember the \texttt{||} and \texttt{\&\&} rules}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $B_1 \,||\, B_2$: if $B_1$ is true we are done (go to \textit{B.true}); if false, \emph{test $B_2$}, so $B_1.\mathit{false}$ is a new label placed in front of $B_2.\mathit{code}$.
\item $B_1 \,\&\&\, B_2$: if $B_1$ is false we are done (go to \textit{B.false}); if true, \emph{test $B_2$}, so $B_1.\mathit{true}$ is a new label in front of $B_2.\mathit{code}$.
\item $!\,B_1$: simply \emph{swap} the true and false labels.
\end{itemize}
\end{notebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: \texttt{if (x < 100 || x > 200 \&\& x != y) x = 0;}}
\textbf{Step 1.} $P \to S$: \at{S}{next} $= L_1$.

\textbf{Step 2.} $S \to \mathbf{if}(B)S_1$: \at{B}{true} $= L_2$, \at{B}{false} $= L_1$, \at{S_1}{next} $= L_1$.

\textbf{Step 3.} $B = B_1 \,||\, B_2$, where $B_1 \equiv x<100$ and $B_2 \equiv (x>200 \;\&\&\; x\ne y)$:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $B_1$: true $= L_2$, false $= L_3$ (new).
\item $B_2$: true $= L_2$, false $= L_1$.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Step 4.} $B_2 = B_3 \,\&\&\, B_4$ with $B_3 \equiv x>200$, $B_4 \equiv x \ne y$:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item $B_3$: true $= L_4$ (new), false $= L_1$.
\item $B_4$: true $= L_2$, false $= L_1$.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Step 5.} Assemble: $B_1.code \cc L_3{:}\ B_2.code$, with $B_2.code = B_3.code \cc L_4{:}\ B_4.code$, followed by $L_2{:}\ S_1.code$ and finally $L_1{:}$

\begin{lstlisting}
        if x < 100 goto L2
        goto L3
L3:     if x > 200 goto L4
        goto L1
L4:     if x != y goto L2
        goto L1
L2:     x = 0
L1:
\end{lstlisting}
Observe the redundant jumps (\texttt{goto L3} immediately followed by \texttt{L3:}) -- a later optimisation phase or the \emph{backpatching} method with fall-through can remove them.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: \texttt{while (a < b) \{ if (c < d) x = y + z; \}}}
\begin{lstlisting}
L1:     if a < b goto L2        // begin / B.code
        goto L0                 // B.false = S.next
L2:     if c < d goto L3        // inner if: B.true = L3
        goto L1                 // inner S.next = begin = L1
L3:     t1 = y + z
        x = t1
        goto L1                 // goto begin
L0:                             // S.next
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Avoiding redundant gotos (fall-through)}
Instead of
\begin{lstlisting}
        if x > 200 goto L4
        goto L1
L4:     ...
\end{lstlisting}
we can generate the shorter form \texttt{ifFalse x > 200 goto L1} followed by the code at \texttt{L4}: if the true label is the next instruction (denote it by the special label \textit{fall}), only one jump is needed. The rule for $B \to E_1\ \mathit{rel}\ E_2$ becomes: let $\mathit{test} = E_1.addr\ rel.op\ E_2.addr$:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Condition} & \textbf{Generated jump code} \\ \midrule
\at{B}{true}\ $\ne$ \textit{fall} and \at{B}{false}\ $\ne$ \textit{fall} & \texttt{if test goto B.true} \quad \texttt{goto B.false} \\
\at{B}{true}\ $\ne$ \textit{fall} and \at{B}{false} = \textit{fall} & \texttt{if test goto B.true} \\
\at{B}{true} = \textit{fall} and \at{B}{false}\ $\ne$ \textit{fall} & \texttt{ifFalse test goto B.false} \\
both = \textit{fall} & (no instruction) \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{The statement and operator rules change too.} Let \textit{fall} mean ``the next instruction in sequence''. The new rules (Dragon Book Section 6.6.5) are:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{2.9cm} L{11.7cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1$ & $\at{B}{true}=\textit{fall}$;\ \ $\at{B}{false}=\at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next}$;\ \ $\at{S}{code}=\at{B}{code}\cc\at{S_1}{code}$ \\[3pt]
$B\to B_1\,|\!|\,B_2$ &
$\at{B_1}{true}=$ (\at{B}{true} if $\ne$ \textit{fall}, else a new label);\ \ $\at{B_1}{false}=\textit{fall}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{true}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{false}=\at{B}{false}$\\
& $\at{B}{code}=\at{B_1}{code}\cc\at{B_2}{code}$ if $\at{B}{true}\ne\textit{fall}$; otherwise $\at{B_1}{code}\cc\at{B_2}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B_1}{true}}$ \\[3pt]
$B\to B_1\ \&\&\ B_2$ &
$\at{B_1}{true}=\textit{fall}$;\ \ $\at{B_1}{false}=$ (\at{B}{false} if $\ne$ \textit{fall}, else a new label);\ \ $\at{B_2}{true}=\at{B}{true}$;\ \ $\at{B_2}{false}=\at{B}{false}$\\
& $\at{B}{code}=\at{B_1}{code}\cc\at{B_2}{code}$ if $\at{B}{false}\ne\textit{fall}$; otherwise $\at{B_1}{code}\cc\at{B_2}{code}\cc\lbl{\at{B_1}{false}}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The extra label in the ``otherwise'' case is needed because when $B_1$ is decisive the control must jump \emph{past} $B_2$, to the end of $B.code$.

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: the same statement with fall-through}
For \texttt{if (x < 100 || x > 200 \&\& x != y) x = 0;} start with $B.true=\textit{fall}$ and $B.false=L_1$. For $B_1\,||\,B_2$: since $B.true=\textit{fall}$, $B_1.true=L_2$ (new) and $B_1.false=\textit{fall}$; $B_2$ inherits $B.true=\textit{fall}$ and $B.false=L_1$. For $B_2=B_3\,\&\&\,B_4$: $B_3.true=B_4.true=\textit{fall}$ and $B_3.false=B_4.false=L_1$. The relational rule then gives
\begin{lstlisting}
        if x < 100 goto L2
        ifFalse x > 200 goto L1
        ifFalse x != y goto L1
L2:     x = 0
L1:
\end{lstlisting}
Only 4 instructions (3 tests and the assignment) instead of 7, and no useless \texttt{goto}s.
\end{examplebox}

%====================================================================
\section{Backpatching}
\label{sec:backpatch}
%====================================================================

\providecommand{\blk}{\textcolor{red!70!black}{\texttt{\_}}}
\providecommand{\fv}[1]{\textcolor{green!45!black}{\textbf{\texttt{#1}}}}

\subsection{The problem and the idea}

\paragraph{The problem: a jump to a place that does not exist yet.} Look at the code for \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1;} followed by some later statement. The compiler writes the three-address instructions one after the other and numbers them:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l l@{}}
\texttt{100:} & \texttt{if a < b goto } \texttt{?} & \emph{true: go to the \textbf{then} part (instruction 102)} \\
\texttt{101:} & \texttt{goto } \texttt{?} & \emph{false: skip the then part; but where does the next statement start?} \\
\texttt{102:} & \texttt{x = 1} & \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
When instruction 101 is written, the compiler has not yet seen the statement after the \textbf{if}, so it cannot know the number after which to jump. A jump to an instruction that has not been generated yet is a \textbf{forward jump}. Every \textbf{if}, \textbf{while} and boolean expression with $\&\&$ or $||$ produces such jumps, so the compiler needs a trick.

\paragraph{Two ways out.} The earlier scheme (the flow-of-control SDD with inherited labels, earlier in this chapter) \emph{invents a label name in advance} and passes it down to the code that needs it: that needs inherited attributes (the names must be handed down before the code is written). A second solution is to translate twice, with the first pass only counting instructions. \textbf{Backpatching} does it in one pass, with synthesized attributes only.

\begin{defbox}{The idea of backpatching in three sentences}
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item When a jump is generated and its target is not known, write the jump \textbf{with a blank target} (\texttt{goto \blk}).
\item Write down the \textbf{number of that instruction} in a \textbf{list}: all jumps that must go to the same place share one list.
\item As soon as the target becomes known, go through the list and \textbf{patch} (fill in) the blank of every instruction on it.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{Analogy:} you are writing a book and, in many places, you write ``see page \blk'' because the index page is not finished. You keep a list of the places that need that page number. When the index gets its page number, you walk through the list once and fill in every blank.
\end{defbox}

\begin{minipage}{\textwidth}
\paragraph{Words you will meet.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{10.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Word} & \textbf{Meaning in plain language} \\ \midrule
instruction number & The position of a three-address instruction in the output, for example 100, 101, 102. Jumps refer to these numbers. \\
$\mathit{nextinstr}$ & The number that the \emph{next} instruction to be written will get. It is a counter that grows by 1 with every instruction. \\
blank target \blk & A jump written as \texttt{goto \blk}: the destination will be filled in later. \\
list of jumps & A set of instruction numbers, such as $\{100,102\}$, whose jumps share one destination. \\
\at{B}{truelist} & The jumps out of the code of the boolean expression $B$ that must go to the place where \emph{$B$ is true}. \\
\at{B}{falselist} & The same for \emph{$B$ is false}. \\
\at{S}{nextlist} & The jumps out of the statement $S$ that must go to the instruction that comes \emph{right after $S$}. \\
marker $M$ & An empty production $M\to\varepsilon$ whose only job is to take a \emph{bookmark}: $\at{M}{instr}=\mathit{nextinstr}$, the number of the next instruction at that moment. It stands exactly where we need to know an address; writing $M$ is the same as writing the action $\{\at{M}{instr}=\mathit{nextinstr}\}$ at that point of the production. \\
marker $N$ & An empty production $N\to\varepsilon$ that writes one \texttt{goto \blk} (used to jump over the \textbf{else} part) and keeps its number in $\at{N}{nextlist}$. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{minipage}

\begin{defbox}{Backpatching primitives}
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mathit{makelist}(i)$: create a new list containing only $i$, an index into the instruction array; return a pointer to it. \emph{Example:} $\mathit{makelist}(100)=\{100\}$.
\item $\mathit{merge}(p_1, p_2)$: concatenate the lists pointed to by $p_1$ and $p_2$; return the merged list. \emph{Example:} $\mathit{merge}(\{100\},\{102\})=\{100,102\}$.
\item $\mathit{backpatch}(p, i)$: insert $i$ as the target label for each instruction on the list $p$. \emph{Example:} $\mathit{backpatch}(\{100,102\},\,104)$ turns \texttt{100: if a<b goto \blk} into \texttt{100: if a<b goto 104} and does the same for 102.
\item $\mathit{nextinstr}$: global variable containing the index of the next instruction to be generated. $\mathit{emit}(\dots)$ appends an instruction and increments it.
\end{itemize}
\end{defbox}

\paragraph{Why only synthesized attributes?} The translation rules run when the parser has \emph{finished} a piece of the input (a post-order evaluation, as for every S-attributed SDD). At that moment the lists \at{B}{truelist}, \at{B}{falselist}, \at{S}{nextlist} are already complete, and they are simply handed upward to the parent. The destination is never needed in advance, so nothing has to be passed \emph{down}.

\subsection{Start here: four small examples by hand}
The formal rules follow in the next two subsections. First we see what they do on four tiny programs. In every example the relational test is between simple names, so a test \texttt{a < b} needs exactly \emph{two} instructions: \texttt{if a < b goto \blk} (the true jump) and \texttt{goto \blk} (the false jump). The first instruction of the whole program has number 100.

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1: \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1;} followed by \texttt{y = 2;}}
The rules used (given in full later): $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,M\,S_1$ with $\mathit{backpatch}(B.truelist,\ M.instr)$ and $S.nextlist=\mathit{merge}(B.falselist,\ S_1.nextlist)$, and $L\to L_1\,M\,S$ with $\mathit{backpatch}(L_1.nextlist,\ M.instr)$.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{The condition $B$ is translated.} Write \texttt{100: if a < b goto \blk} and \texttt{101: goto \blk}. \ $B.truelist=\{100\}$, $B.falselist=\{101\}$. Both targets are blank.
\item \textbf{The marker $M$} comes next and takes a bookmark: $M.instr=102$, because 102 is the number the next instruction will get. This is where the \emph{then} part starts. \ $\mathit{backpatch}(\{100\},102)$ fills the true jump: \texttt{100: if a < b goto 102}.
\item \textbf{The statement $S_1$} is written: \texttt{102: x = 1}. An assignment never jumps, so $S_1.nextlist=\mathbf{null}$ (empty). The \textbf{if} statement concludes: $S.nextlist=\mathit{merge}(\{101\},\mathbf{null})=\{101\}$. In words: ``instruction 101 must go to whatever comes after the \textbf{if}''. The blank stays.
\item \textbf{The next statement starts.} The marker $M$ of the statement list takes a bookmark: $M.instr=103$. \ $\mathit{backpatch}(\{101\},103)$ fills the last blank: \texttt{101: goto 103}. Then \texttt{103: y = 2} is written.
\end{enumerate}
The code as it grows (red \blk\ is a blank, green is a value that was just filled in):
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{after step 1} & \textbf{after step 3} & \textbf{after step 4 (final)} \\ \midrule
\texttt{100} & \texttt{if a<b goto }\blk & \texttt{if a<b goto }\fv{102} & \texttt{if a<b goto 102} \\
\texttt{101} & \texttt{goto }\blk & \texttt{goto }\blk & \texttt{goto }\fv{103} \\
\texttt{102} & & \texttt{x = 1} & \texttt{x = 1} \\
\texttt{103} & & & \texttt{y = 2} \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to remember:} the \emph{true} jump was patched \emph{immediately} (the then part follows right behind it), and the \emph{false} jump had to wait on a list until the next statement was known.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 2: why lists? \texttt{if (a < b || c < d) x = 1;}}
Now there are \emph{two} tests, and \emph{both} jump to the then part when they succeed. The rule is $B\to B_1\,||\,M\,B_2$: $\mathit{backpatch}(B_1.falselist,\ M.instr)$, $B.truelist=\mathit{merge}(B_1.truelist,\ B_2.truelist)$, $B.falselist=B_2.falselist$. In words: ``if the first test is false, try the second test; the expression is true if either test is true''.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item \textbf{$B_1$} is \texttt{a < b}: \texttt{100: if a<b goto \blk}, \texttt{101: goto \blk}. \ $B_1.truelist=\{100\}$, $B_1.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item \textbf{The marker $M$} takes a bookmark: $M.instr=102$, the start of the second test. \ $\mathit{backpatch}(B_1.falselist,102)$ fills \texttt{101: goto 102}: ``if $a<b$ is false, go and test $c<d$''.
\item \textbf{$B_2$} is \texttt{c < d}: \texttt{102: if c<d goto \blk}, \texttt{103: goto \blk}. \ $B_2.truelist=\{102\}$, $B_2.falselist=\{103\}$.
\item \textbf{$B$ is finished.} $B.truelist=\mathit{merge}(\{100\},\{102\})=\{100,102\}$ \ \emph{(two jumps, one destination)}, and $B.falselist=\{103\}$.
\item \textbf{The \textbf{if} rule:} the marker for the then part has $M.instr=104$. \ $\mathit{backpatch}(\{100,102\},104)$ fills \emph{both} true jumps with one call. Then \texttt{104: x = 1} is written, and $S.nextlist=\{103\}$, which is patched later (say, with 105).
\end{enumerate}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.95\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=3pt,align=center},
  note/.style={font=\scriptsize,align=left}]
 \node[nd] (B)  at (0,0)    {$B$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{truelist}=\{100,102\}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{falselist}=\{103\}$}};
 \node[nd] (B1) at (-5.4,-2.4) {$B_1$ \ (\texttt{a<b})\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{truelist}=\{100\}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{falselist}=\{101\}$}};
 \node[nd] (or) at (-2.0,-2.4) {$||$};
 \node[nd] (M)  at (0.6,-2.4)  {$M$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{instr}=102$}};
 \node[nd] (B2) at (4.6,-2.4)  {$B_2$ \ (\texttt{c<d})\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{truelist}=\{102\}$}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{falselist}=\{103\}$}};
 \foreach \a/\b in {B/B1,B/or,B/M,B/B2} \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \node[note,anchor=north west] at (-6.9,-4.0) {\textbf{1.}\ \texttt{101} is patched when $M$ is read:\\ $\mathit{backpatch}(B_1.\mathit{falselist}=\{101\},\ M.\mathit{instr}=102)$};
 \node[note,anchor=north west] at (-0.6,-4.0) {\textbf{2.}\ when $B$ is finished the two true lists are joined:\\ $B.\mathit{truelist}=\mathit{merge}(\{100\},\{102\})=\{100,102\}$};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{The attributes of \texttt{a<b || c<d}. The false jump of the first test is patched at once (to the second test); the true jumps of both tests wait together on one list and are patched together later.}
\end{figure}
The final code, with the destination 105 supplied by whatever follows:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{No.} & \textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Comment} \\ \midrule
\texttt{100} & \texttt{if a<b goto 104} & first test true $\to$ then part \\
\texttt{101} & \texttt{goto 102} & first test false $\to$ try the second test \\
\texttt{102} & \texttt{if c<d goto 104} & second test true $\to$ then part \\
\texttt{103} & \texttt{goto 105} & second test false $\to$ skip the then part \\
\texttt{104} & \texttt{x = 1} & the then part \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to remember:} a list is needed because \emph{several jumps share one destination}. $\mathit{merge}$ collects them, and one $\mathit{backpatch}$ call fixes all of them.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 3: the marker $N$ and the \textbf{else} part: \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1; else x = 2;} followed by \texttt{y = 3;}}
The rule is $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(B)\,M_1\,S_1\,N\,\mathbf{else}\,M_2\,S_2$. After the then part is executed we must \emph{jump over} the else part; the marker $N$ writes that jump, and its number goes on a list.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $B$: \texttt{100: if a<b goto \blk}, \texttt{101: goto \blk}. \ $B.truelist=\{100\}$, $B.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item $M_1.instr=102$ (start of the then part). \ $\mathit{backpatch}(B.truelist,102)$: \texttt{100: if a<b goto 102}.
\item $S_1$ is \texttt{102: x = 1}.
\item \textbf{The marker $N$} writes \texttt{103: goto \blk}; $N.nextlist=\{103\}$. This jump is only executed after the then part, and it must go behind the else part: the destination is not known yet.
\item $M_2.instr=104$ (start of the else part). \ $\mathit{backpatch}(B.falselist,104)$: \texttt{101: goto 104}. \ Then $S_2$ is \texttt{104: x = 2}.
\item The statement ends: $S.nextlist=\mathit{merge}(S_1.nextlist,\ N.nextlist,\ S_2.nextlist)=\{103\}$. The next statement starts at 105, so the statement list patches \texttt{103: goto 105} (and \texttt{y = 3} is \texttt{105}).
\end{enumerate}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{No.} & \textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Written by} & \textbf{Blank filled by} \\ \midrule
\texttt{100} & \texttt{if a<b goto 102} & $B$ (true jump)  & $M_1$ (start of then part) \\
\texttt{101} & \texttt{goto 104}        & $B$ (false jump) & $M_2$ (start of else part) \\
\texttt{102} & \texttt{x = 1}           & $S_1$ & \\
\texttt{103} & \texttt{goto 105}        & $N$ (skip the else part) & the next statement (via $S.nextlist$) \\
\texttt{104} & \texttt{x = 2}           & $S_2$ & \\
\texttt{105} & \texttt{y = 3}           & next statement & \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to remember:} the \emph{false} jump is patched at once now (to the else part); the \emph{only} jump that waits for the next statement is the one written by $N$.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 4: a loop, \texttt{while (a < b) x = x + 1;} followed by \texttt{y = 3;}}
The rule is $S\to\mathbf{while}\,M_1\,(B)\,M_2\,S_1$. A loop must jump \emph{back} to the test after every pass; that target is known early, so it needs no list. The ordinary exit jump is the one that waits.
\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $M_1.instr=100$ is a bookmark of the beginning of the test (it will be the target of the jump \emph{back}).
\item $B$: \texttt{100: if a<b goto \blk}, \texttt{101: goto \blk}. \ $B.truelist=\{100\}$, $B.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item $M_2.instr=102$ (start of the body). \ $\mathit{backpatch}(B.truelist,102)$: \texttt{100: if a<b goto 102}.
\item $S_1$ (the body) is \texttt{102: x = x + 1}. It does not jump: $S_1.nextlist=\mathbf{null}$.
\item The rule now closes the loop: $\mathit{backpatch}(S_1.nextlist,\ M_1.instr)$ (nothing to patch here), then $\mathit{emit}(\texttt{goto }M_1.instr)$ writes \texttt{103: goto 100}, a jump with a \emph{known} target. Finally $S.nextlist=B.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item The next statement starts at 104, so the statement list patches \texttt{101: goto 104}.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}c l l l@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{No.} & \textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Written by} & \textbf{Blank filled by} \\ \midrule
\texttt{100} & \texttt{if a<b goto 102} & $B$ (true jump)  & $M_2$ (start of body) \\
\texttt{101} & \texttt{goto 104}        & $B$ (false jump) & the next statement (via $S.nextlist$) \\
\texttt{102} & \texttt{x = x + 1}       & $S_1$ & \\
\texttt{103} & \texttt{goto 100}        & the \textbf{while} rule & known at once ($M_1.instr$) \\
\texttt{104} & \texttt{y = 3}           & next statement & \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\textbf{What to remember:} a jump \emph{back} never needs backpatching, because its target is already behind us. Only \emph{forward} jumps wait on lists.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{notebox}{Cheat sheet: who fills which blank?}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item The \textbf{true} jump of a test is filled by the marker at the start of the code that must run when it is true (the then part, the loop body, or the second operand of $\&\&$).
\item The \textbf{false} jump is filled either by the marker at the start of the else part, or by the marker at the start of the second operand of $||$, or it \emph{waits} on $S.nextlist$.
\item The jump written by $N$ always waits on $S.nextlist$.
\item $S.nextlist$ is filled by whoever starts the \emph{next statement} (the rule $L\to L_1\,M\,S$) or, at the end of the program, by the code that follows.
\end{itemize}
\end{notebox}


\subsection{Boolean expressions}
The four examples above used the rules informally. Here they are in full, for boolean expressions first and then for statements. Read each rule together with the example it belongs to: $B\to B_1\,||\,M\,B_2$ is Example~2, the \textbf{if} rules are Examples~1 and~3, the \textbf{while} rule is Example~4.

Synthesized attributes: \at{B}{truelist} (instructions that jump when $B$ is true) and \at{B}{falselist} (when false). A \textbf{marker nonterminal} $M \to \varepsilon$ records the index of the next instruction (\at{M}{instr}).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.3cm} L{11.3cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic action} \\ \midrule
$B \to B_1 \,|\!|\, M\ B_2$ &
$\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B_1}{falselist},\ \at{M}{instr});$\\
& $\ \ \at{B}{truelist}=\mathit{merge}(\at{B_1}{truelist},\ \at{B_2}{truelist});\ \ \at{B}{falselist}=\at{B_2}{falselist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to B_1\ \&\&\ M\ B_2$ &
$\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B_1}{truelist},\ \at{M}{instr});$\\
& $\ \ \at{B}{truelist}=\at{B_2}{truelist};\ \ \at{B}{falselist}=\mathit{merge}(\at{B_1}{falselist},\ \at{B_2}{falselist});\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to !\,B_1$ &
$\{\ \at{B}{truelist}=\at{B_1}{falselist};\ \at{B}{falselist}=\at{B_1}{truelist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to (\,B_1\,)$ &
$\{\ \at{B}{truelist}=\at{B_1}{truelist};\ \at{B}{falselist}=\at{B_1}{falselist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to E_1\ \mathit{rel}\ E_2$ &
$\{\ \at{B}{truelist}=\mathit{makelist}(\mathit{nextinstr});\ \at{B}{falselist}=\mathit{makelist}(\mathit{nextinstr}+1);$\\
& $\ \ \mathit{emit}(\tok{if}\ \at{E_1}{addr}\ \mathit{rel.op}\ \at{E_2}{addr}\ \tok{goto}\ \_);\ \ \mathit{emit}(\tok{goto}\ \_);\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to \mathbf{true}$ &
$\{\ \at{B}{truelist}=\mathit{makelist}(\mathit{nextinstr});\ \mathit{emit}(\tok{goto}\ \_);\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$B \to \mathbf{false}$ &
$\{\ \at{B}{falselist}=\mathit{makelist}(\mathit{nextinstr});\ \mathit{emit}(\tok{goto}\ \_);\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$M \to \varepsilon$ &
$\{\ \at{M}{instr}=\mathit{nextinstr};\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Translation scheme for boolean expressions using backpatching (Dragon Book Fig.~6.43).}
\end{table}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: $x < 100 \;||\; x > 200 \;\&\&\; x \ne y$ (instructions start at index 100)}
\textbf{Parse structure:} $B \Rightarrow B_1 \,||\, M_1\, B_2$, where $B_2 \Rightarrow B_3 \,\&\&\, M_2\, B_4$.

\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $B_1 \equiv x<100$: emit \texttt{100: if x<100 goto \_}, \texttt{101: goto \_}\\
 \hspace*{1em} $B_1.truelist=\{100\}$, $B_1.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item $M_1.instr = 102$.
\item $B_3 \equiv x>200$: emit \texttt{102: if x>200 goto \_}, \texttt{103: goto \_}\\
 \hspace*{1em} $B_3.truelist=\{102\}$, $B_3.falselist=\{103\}$.
\item $M_2.instr = 104$.
\item $B_4 \equiv x\ne y$: emit \texttt{104: if x!=y goto \_}, \texttt{105: goto \_}\\
 \hspace*{1em} $B_4.truelist=\{104\}$, $B_4.falselist=\{105\}$.
\item Complete $B_2 \to B_3 \,\&\&\, M_2\, B_4$:\ \ $\mathit{backpatch}(\{102\},104)$ $\Rightarrow$ instruction 102 becomes \texttt{if x>200 goto 104}.\\
 \hspace*{1em} $B_2.truelist=\{104\}$, $B_2.falselist=\mathit{merge}(\{103\},\{105\})=\{103,105\}$.
\item Complete $B \to B_1 \,||\, M_1\, B_2$:\ \ $\mathit{backpatch}(\{101\},102)$ $\Rightarrow$ instruction 101 becomes \texttt{goto 102}.\\
 \hspace*{1em} $B.truelist=\mathit{merge}(\{100\},\{104\})=\{100,104\}$, $B.falselist=\{103,105\}$.
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{Result} (still incomplete -- the lists will be patched by the enclosing statement):
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  if x < 100 goto _      <- on truelist
101:  goto 102
102:  if x > 200 goto 104
103:  goto _                 <- on falselist
104:  if x != y goto _       <- on truelist
105:  goto _                 <- on falselist
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  ins/.style={draw,anchor=west,minimum width=5.4cm,minimum height=0.62cm,font=\small\ttfamily},
  idx/.style={font=\small\ttfamily,anchor=east},
  pat/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},thick,blue!70!black}]
 \node[idx] at (-0.1,0)     {100};  \node[ins,fill=green!18] (i0) at (0,0)     {if x < 100 goto \_};
 \node[idx] at (-0.1,-0.62) {101};  \node[ins,fill=blue!10]  (i1) at (0,-0.62) {goto 102};
 \node[idx] at (-0.1,-1.24) {102};  \node[ins,fill=blue!10]  (i2) at (0,-1.24) {if x > 200 goto 104};
