 \node[idx] at (-0.1,-1.86) {103};  \node[ins,fill=red!15]   (i3) at (0,-1.86) {goto \_};
 \node[idx] at (-0.1,-2.48) {104};  \node[ins,fill=green!18] (i4) at (0,-2.48) {if x != y goto \_};
 \node[idx] at (-0.1,-3.10) {105};  \node[ins,fill=red!15]   (i5) at (0,-3.10) {goto \_};
 \draw[pat] (i1.east) .. controls +(0.9,0) and +(0.9,0) .. (i2.east);
 \draw[pat] (i2.east) .. controls +(1.5,0) and +(1.5,0) .. (i4.east);
 \node[font=\footnotesize,anchor=west,blue!70!black] at (7.0,-0.62) {101 patched with $M_1$ (102)};
 \node[font=\footnotesize,anchor=west,blue!70!black] at (7.6,-1.6) {102 patched with $M_2$ (104)};
 \node[draw,fill=green!18,minimum size=3mm,inner sep=0pt] at (7.2,-2.5) {};
 \node[font=\footnotesize,anchor=west] at (7.45,-2.5) {$B.truelist=\{100,104\}$};
 \node[draw,fill=red!15,minimum size=3mm,inner sep=0pt] at (7.2,-3.1) {};
 \node[font=\footnotesize,anchor=west] at (7.45,-3.1) {$B.falselist=\{103,105\}$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
Blue jumps are already complete. The green and red jumps have a blank target; they are exactly the entries of the two lists that the parent will patch.
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Flow-of-control statements}
Synthesized attribute \at{S}{nextlist}: list of jumps to the instruction that follows $S$. We use markers $M \to \varepsilon$ (records \textit{nextinstr}) and $N \to \varepsilon$ (emits an incomplete \texttt{goto} and returns it on a list).

Grammar:
\begin{align*}
S &\to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1 \mid \mathbf{if}\,(B)\,S_1\ \mathbf{else}\ S_2 \mid \mathbf{while}\,(B)\,S_1 \mid \{\,L\,\} \mid A\,;\\
L &\to L_1\,S \mid S
\end{align*}
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{4.3cm} L{10.3cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic action} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\ M\ S_1$ &
$\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B}{truelist},\ \at{M}{instr});$\ \ $\at{S}{nextlist}=\mathit{merge}(\at{B}{falselist},\ \at{S_1}{nextlist});\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{if}\,(B)\ M_1\ S_1\ N\ \mathbf{else}\ M_2\ S_2$ &
$\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B}{truelist},\ \at{M_1}{instr});\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B}{falselist},\ \at{M_2}{instr});$\\
& $\ \ \mathit{temp}=\mathit{merge}(\at{S_1}{nextlist},\ \at{N}{nextlist});$\\
& $\ \ \at{S}{nextlist}=\mathit{merge}(\mathit{temp},\ \at{S_2}{nextlist});\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{while}\ M_1\,(B)\ M_2\ S_1$ &
$\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{S_1}{nextlist},\ \at{M_1}{instr});$\\
& $\ \ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{B}{truelist},\ \at{M_2}{instr});$\\
& $\ \ \at{S}{nextlist}=\at{B}{falselist};\ \mathit{emit}(\tok{goto}\ \at{M_1}{instr});\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \{\ L\ \}$ & $\{\ \at{S}{nextlist}=\at{L}{nextlist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$S \to A\ ;$ & $\{\ \at{S}{nextlist}=\mathbf{null};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$L \to L_1\ M\ S$ & $\{\ \mathit{backpatch}(\at{L_1}{nextlist},\ \at{M}{instr});\ \at{L}{nextlist}=\at{S}{nextlist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$L \to S$ & $\{\ \at{L}{nextlist}=\at{S}{nextlist};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$M \to \varepsilon$ & $\{\ \at{M}{instr}=\mathit{nextinstr};\ \}$ \\[3pt]
$N \to \varepsilon$ & $\{\ \at{N}{nextlist}=\mathit{makelist}(\mathit{nextinstr});\ \mathit{emit}(\tok{goto}\ \_);\ \}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Translation of statements with backpatching (Dragon Book Fig.~6.46).}
\end{table}

\begin{notebox}{Why the markers?}
$M$ \emph{remembers a position}: ``this is where the code for $S_1$ (or for the test) begins''. $N$ \emph{generates the jump over the else-part} and returns it for later patching. In the \textbf{while} rule the loop-back jump uses $M_1$, which was recorded \emph{before} $B.code$ was generated.
\end{notebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: \texttt{while (a < b) \{ if (c < d) x = y + z; \}}}
Assume numbering starts at 100; $M_1.instr=100$.

\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
\item $B \equiv a<b$: \texttt{100: if a<b goto \_}, \texttt{101: goto \_}; $\ B.truelist=\{100\}$, $B.falselist=\{101\}$.
\item $M_2.instr = 102$.
\item Inner $B' \equiv c<d$: \texttt{102: if c<d goto \_}, \texttt{103: goto \_}; $\ B'.truelist=\{102\}$, $B'.falselist=\{103\}$.
\item $M.instr = 104$; assignment $x=y+z$: \texttt{104: t1 = y + z}, \texttt{105: x = t1}; $S_1.nextlist=\mathbf{null}$.
\item Inner \textbf{if}: $\mathit{backpatch}(\{102\},104)$; $\ S.nextlist=\mathit{merge}(\{103\},\mathbf{null})=\{103\}$.
\item \textbf{while}: $\mathit{backpatch}(\{103\},100)$ (i.e., \texttt{103: goto 100}); $\ \mathit{backpatch}(\{100\},102)$ (i.e., \texttt{100: if a<b goto 102}); $\ S.nextlist=B.falselist=\{101\}$; emit \texttt{106: goto 100}.
\end{enumerate}
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  if a < b goto 102
101:  goto _              <- S.nextlist, patched by whoever follows
102:  if c < d goto 104
103:  goto 100
104:  t1 = y + z
105:  x = t1
106:  goto 100
107:  ...                 <- (101 will be backpatched with 107)
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Worked example: \texttt{if (a < b) x = 1; else x = 2;}}
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  if a < b goto 102      // B.truelist = {100}, patched to M1 = 102
101:  goto 104               // B.falselist = {101}, patched to M2 = 104
102:  x = 1
103:  goto _                 // N: on S.nextlist
104:  x = 2
105:  ...                    // 103 is patched to 105 by the enclosing construct
\end{lstlisting}
\end{examplebox}

\subsection{Comparison: inherited labels vs.\ backpatching}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}L{3.3cm} L{5.6cm} L{5.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
 & \textbf{Label-based SDD (Fig.~6.36/6.37)} & \textbf{Backpatching (Fig.~6.43/6.46)} \\ \midrule
Attributes & Inherited labels (\textit{true, false, next}) & Synthesized lists (\textit{truelist, falselist, nextlist}) \\
Class of SDD & L-attributed & S-attributed (with markers $M$, $N$) \\
Best suited for & Top-down / recursive-descent (or AST traversal) & Single-pass generation with synthesized attributes (post-order) \\
Code storage & Attribute \at{}{code}, or incremental \textit{gen} & Instruction array; patched later \\
Labels & Symbolic labels generated eagerly & Instruction indices known late \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

%====================================================================
\section{Switch Statements and Procedure Calls}
\label{sec:switchcall}

Both constructs fit the pattern of this chapter: an SDD with attributes, and an SDT that is obtained from it. We use the label-based style of Section~\ref{sec:labelintro}: $\at{S}{next}$ is the name of the door right after $S$, handed down by the parent, and $\mathit{newlabel}()$, $\mathit{label}(L)$ and $\mathit{gen}(\dots)$ have the meaning given there. The expression rules of Section~\ref{sec:exprtac} provide $\at{E}{addr}$ (where the value of $E$ lives) and $\at{E}{code}$.

%--------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Switch statement}
\label{sec:switch}

\paragraph{The intuition: a menu with a doorman.} Think of a hotel reception. The guest shows a number (the value $t$ of the selector $E$); the doorman looks at a list (\emph{if the number is 1, go to room 1; if 2, room 2; \dots; otherwise the default room}) and sends the guest to the right room. Each room is the code of one \textbf{case}. We write the rooms first and the doorman's list at the \emph{end}, for two reasons: (1) while we write the rooms we do not yet know how many cases there will be, so the list can only be completed at the end; and (2) all tests stand together in one block, so a good code generator can replace them by a \textbf{jump table} (one indexed jump) when the values are dense.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  blk/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,minimum width=6.2cm,minimum height=7.5mm,align=left,font=\small,inner xsep=6pt},
  gt/.style={-{Stealth[length=2.2mm]},thick}]
 \node[blk,fill=gray!10] (e)  at (0,0)    {code to evaluate $E$ into $t$};
 \node[blk,fill=blue!8] (g)   at (0,-1.1) {\texttt{goto test}};
 \node[blk,fill=green!10] (c1) at (0,-2.4) {\texttt{L1: } code of $S_1$\ \ \texttt{goto next}};
 \node[blk,fill=green!10] (c2) at (0,-3.5) {\texttt{L2: } code of $S_2$\ \ \texttt{goto next}};
 \node[blk,fill=green!10] (cd) at (0,-4.6) {\texttt{Ld: } default code\ \ \texttt{goto next}};
 \node[blk,fill=red!8] (t)    at (0,-6.2) {\texttt{test:}\ if $t=V_1$ goto L1\\\phantom{\texttt{test:}\ }if $t=V_2$ goto L2\\\phantom{\texttt{test:}\ }goto Ld};
 \node[blk,fill=gray!10] (n)  at (0,-7.7) {\texttt{next:}\ the statement after the switch};
 \draw[gt,blue!70!black] (g.west) to[out=180,in=180,looseness=2.2] (t.west);
 \draw[gt,red!70!black] (t.east) to[out=0,in=0,looseness=1.1] (c1.east);
 \draw[gt,red!70!black] (t.east) to[out=0,in=0,looseness=1.5] (c2.east);
 \draw[gt,red!70!black] (t.east) to[out=0,in=0,looseness=1.9] (cd.east);
 \node[font=\scriptsize,text=blue!70!black,anchor=east,align=center] at (-3.7,-3.6) {the first jump\\skips the rooms};
 \node[font=\scriptsize,text=red!70!black,anchor=west,align=center] at (4.3,-4.2) {the doorman\\sends the guest\\to the right room};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The layout of the code of a \textbf{switch}. The rooms (case bodies) come first and the doorman's list (the tests) comes last. Each room ends with \texttt{goto next}, which leaves the switch.}
\label{fig:switchlayout}
\end{figure}

The same layout as code:
\begin{lstlisting}
        code to evaluate E into t
        goto test
L1:     code for S1
        goto next
L2:     code for S2
        goto next
 ...
Ld:     code for the default part
        goto next
test:   if t = V1 goto L1
        if t = V2 goto L2
        ...
        goto Ld
next:
\end{lstlisting}
\texttt{case} instructions may replace the tests: \texttt{case t V1 L1}, \dots, \texttt{case t t Ld} (the last entry is the default).

\paragraph{The grammar.} To keep it small, the default part is last and the cases come in a list:
\begin{align*}
S &\to \mathbf{switch}\ \tok{(}\,E\,\tok{)}\ \tok{\{}\ \mathit{CL}\ \mathbf{default}\ \tok{:}\ S_1\ \tok{\}}\\
\mathit{CL} &\to C\ \mathit{CL}_1\ \mid\ \varepsilon\\
C &\to \mathbf{case}\ \mathbf{num}\ \tok{:}\ S_1
\end{align*}
$\mathit{CL}$ is the list of cases and $C$ is one case. The statements $S_1$ inside are arbitrary statements, for example assignments or further \textbf{if} and \textbf{switch} statements.

\paragraph{The attributes: who holds what.}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l p{8.0cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Attribute} & \textbf{Kind} & \textbf{What it holds, and when it is used} \\ \midrule
\at{S}{next} & inherited & The door after the whole switch; given by the parent. Every case body ends with a jump to it. \\
\at{CL}{next}, \at{C}{next} & inherited & A copy of \at{S}{next}, passed down to the cases so that each one can write \texttt{goto next}. \\
\at{CL}{t}, \at{C}{t} & inherited & The address of the selector value (a copy of \at{E}{addr}), passed down so that each case can write \emph{its own} test \texttt{if t = V goto L}. \\
\at{C}{code} & synthesized & The code of one case: its label, the body, and \texttt{goto next}. \\
\at{C}{test} & synthesized & The one test instruction of this case: \texttt{if t = V goto L}. \\
\at{CL}{code}, \at{CL}{tests} & synthesized & The bodies of all cases put together, and the tests of all cases put together. Two separate strings. \\
$L$, $\mathit{test}$, $L_d$ & local names & Door names invented by $\mathit{newlabel}()$ inside a rule: one for each case, one for the test block, one for the default part. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{defbox}{The SDD for the switch statement}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{4.1cm} p{9.9cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{switch}\,(E)\,\{$\newline $\quad\mathit{CL}\ \mathbf{default}:\,S_1\,\}$ &
 $\mathit{test}=\mathit{newlabel}()$; \ $L_d=\mathit{newlabel}()$;\newline
 $\at{CL}{t}=\at{E}{addr}$; \ $\at{CL}{next}=\at{S}{next}$; \ $\at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next}$;\newline
 $\at{S}{code}=\at{E}{code}\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \mathit{test})\cc\at{CL}{code}\cc\mathit{label}(L_d)\cc\at{S_1}{code}$\newline
 $\qquad\cc\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \at{S}{next})\cc\mathit{label}(\mathit{test})\cc\at{CL}{tests}\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ L_d)$ \\[6pt]
$\mathit{CL} \to C\ \mathit{CL}_1$ &
 $\at{C}{t}=\at{CL_1}{t}=\at{CL}{t}$; \ $\at{C}{next}=\at{CL_1}{next}=\at{CL}{next}$;\newline
 $\at{CL}{code}=\at{C}{code}\cc\at{CL_1}{code}$; \ $\at{CL}{tests}=\at{C}{test}\cc\at{CL_1}{tests}$ \\[6pt]
$\mathit{CL} \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{CL}{code}=\varepsilon$; \ $\at{CL}{tests}=\varepsilon$ \\[6pt]
$C \to \mathbf{case}\ \mathbf{num}:\ S_1$ &
 $L=\mathit{newlabel}()$; \ $\at{S_1}{next}=\at{C}{next}$;\newline
 $\at{C}{code}=\mathit{label}(L)\cc\at{S_1}{code}\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \at{C}{next})$;\newline
 $\at{C}{test}=\mathit{gen}(\tok{if}\ \at{C}{t}\ \tok{=}\ \at{\mathbf{num}}{val}\ \tok{goto}\ L)$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
(Here $\varepsilon$ denotes the empty string.) \textbf{How to read it.} Two strings grow side by side while the list of cases is traversed: \at{CL}{code} collects the \emph{bodies}, \at{CL}{tests} collects the \emph{tests}. The rule for $S$ then places them in two different spots of the final code: bodies first, tests last. Every case builds \emph{both} of its pieces itself, because it knows both its own label $L$ and its own value.
\end{defbox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1: the code and the attributes of a switch}
\begin{lstlisting}
switch (c) {
    case 1:  x = 10;
    case 2:  x = 20;
    default: x = 0;
}
\end{lstlisting}
The selector is a variable, so $\at{E}{code}$ is empty and $\at{E}{addr}=c$. The parent gave $\at{S}{next}=L_1$. The SDD (with the label numbers in order of creation: $\mathit{test}=L_2$, case~1 $=L_3$, case~2 $=L_4$, $L_d=L_5$) produces:
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{9.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L2} & $\mathit{gen}(\texttt{goto }\mathit{test})$ in the rule for $S$. The name $\mathit{test}=L_2$ was invented at the start of the rule, so the jump can be written \emph{before} the test block exists. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L3: x = 10} & Case~1: $L=L_3$ is $\mathit{newlabel}()$ of this case; $\mathit{label}(L)$ puts the tag in front of the body \at{S_1}{code}. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} & $\mathit{gen}(\texttt{goto }\at{C}{next})$. \at{C}{next}$=L_1$ is a copy of \at{S}{next}, which came down from the parent. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L4: x = 20} & Case~2, the same way: $L=L_4$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} & The same \at{C}{next}$=L_1$ again. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L5: x = 0} & The default part: $\mathit{label}(L_d)$ with $L_d=L_5$, then \at{S_1}{code}. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L1} & $\mathit{gen}(\texttt{goto }\at{S}{next})$ after the default body. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L2: if c = 1 goto L3} & $\mathit{label}(\mathit{test})$, then the first piece of \at{CL}{tests}: \at{C}{test} of case~1, with \at{C}{t}$=c$ (copied down from \at{E}{addr}), value $1$ and its own label $L_3$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ if c = 2 goto L4} & \at{C}{test} of case~2: value $2$, label $L_4$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{\ \ \ \ goto L5} & $\mathit{gen}(\texttt{goto }L_d)$: no case matched, go to the default part. \\[3pt]
\texttt{L1:} & $\mathit{label}(\at{S}{next})$ placed by the \emph{parent} rule $P\to S$, not by the switch. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Because the tests are together at the end and the values are dense ($1,2$), a code generator may replace them by a jump table: after the range check $1\le c\le 2$ one indexed jump through the table $[L_3,L_4]$. (Each body ends with \texttt{goto L1}: this is the Pascal-style \textbf{case}; see the note on \textbf{break} below.)
\end{examplebox}

\begin{notebox}{Break and fall-through (C and Java)}
The SDD above writes \texttt{goto next} after \emph{every} body, which is the behaviour of Pascal-like \textbf{case} statements. In C and Java a body \emph{falls through} into the next one unless it ends with \textbf{break}. To translate those, write the \texttt{goto next} only for a \textbf{break} (the rule $S\to\mathbf{break}\,;$ is $\at{S}{code}=\mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \at{S}{next})$ with the right $\mathit{next}$) and omit the automatic one at the end of $C\to\mathbf{case}\,\mathbf{num}:\,S_1$.
\end{notebox}

\paragraph{The SDT.} The SDD builds two long strings. An SDT can instead print the pieces at once, as in Method~B of Section~\ref{sec:impl-lattr}. The bodies are printed immediately, in order, but the tests cannot, because they must come \emph{after} all the bodies. So we \emph{collect} the pairs (value, label) in a synthesized list attribute \at{CL}{list} and print the tests at the very end. Here $\mathit{gen}$ appends an instruction to the output at once. The actions for inherited attributes stand immediately before the symbol that uses them (Section~\ref{sec:lattr}).
\begin{align*}
S \to \mathbf{switch}\ \tok{(}\,E\,\tok{)}\ \tok{\{}\ &\{\ \mathit{test}=\mathit{newlabel}();\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \mathit{test});\ \at{CL}{next}=\at{S}{next}\ \}\ \mathit{CL}\\
 \mathbf{default}\ \tok{:}\ &\{\ L_d=\mathit{newlabel}();\ \mathit{label}(L_d);\ \at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next}\ \}\ S_1\\
 &\{\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \at{S}{next});\ \mathit{label}(\mathit{test});\\
 &\quad\mathbf{for}\ \text{each}\ (V,L)\ \text{in}\ \at{CL}{list}:\\
 &\qquad\mathit{gen}(\tok{if}\ \at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ V\ \tok{goto}\ L);\\
 &\quad\mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ L_d)\ \}\ \tok{\}}\\[6pt]
\mathit{CL} \to\ &\{\ \at{C}{next}=\at{CL}{next}\ \}\ C\ \{\ \at{CL_1}{next}=\at{CL}{next}\ \}\ \mathit{CL}_1\\
 &\{\ \at{CL}{list}=[\at{C}{pair}]\ \mathbin{+\!\!+}\ \at{CL_1}{list}\ \}\\
\mathit{CL} \to\ &\varepsilon\ \{\ \at{CL}{list}=[\ ]\ \}\\[6pt]
C \to \mathbf{case}\ \mathbf{num}\ \tok{:}\ &\{\ L=\mathit{newlabel}();\ \mathit{label}(L);\ \at{S_1}{next}=\at{C}{next}\ \}\ S_1\\
 &\{\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{goto}\ \at{C}{next});\ \at{C}{pair}=(\at{\mathbf{num}}{val},\,L)\ \}
\end{align*}
The expression $E$ prints its own code before the first action, so the instructions come out in the right order: $E$'s code, \texttt{goto test}, the bodies, the default body, the tests.

\begin{examplebox}{Example 2: how the list of pairs is built}
For the switch of Example~1, parsing proceeds from left to right. Read the right column to see the list grow.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3.4cm} p{5.6cm} p{4.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Moment} & \textbf{Printed} & \textbf{List attributes} \\ \midrule
after \texttt{switch (c) \{} & \texttt{goto L2} & \\
case~1 parsed & \texttt{L3: x = 10}, \texttt{goto L1} & $C.pair=(1,L_3)$ \\
case~2 parsed & \texttt{L4: x = 20}, \texttt{goto L1} & $C.pair=(2,L_4)$ \\
$\mathit{CL}\to\varepsilon$ & & $CL.list=[\,]$ \\
back in $\mathit{CL}$ (case~2) & & $CL.list=[(2,L_4)]$ \\
back in $\mathit{CL}$ (case~1) & & $CL.list=[(1,L_3),(2,L_4)]$ \\
\texttt{default:} body & \texttt{L5: x = 0}, \texttt{goto L1} & \\
the closing \texttt{\}} & \texttt{L2:}, \texttt{if c = 1 goto L3}, \texttt{if c = 2 goto L4}, \texttt{goto L5} & the list is read once, from the front \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The pairs are \emph{prepended} one level at a time, so the final list has the cases in the order of the program, and the tests come out in that order.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{pitfallbox}{One global queue breaks nested switches}
A common textbook description keeps the pairs in \emph{one queue}, and ``at the end of the switch'' reads it. With a \textbf{switch} inside a case of another \textbf{switch}, the inner cases would be appended to the \emph{outer} queue (or the queue would be reset), and the tests of the outer switch would jump to labels of the inner one. Making the list a \emph{synthesized attribute} \at{CL}{list} gives every switch its own list for free: the list of the inner switch is finished and used inside the inner $S$ and never reaches the outer one.
\end{pitfallbox}

%--------------------------------------------------------------------
\subsection{Procedure calls}
\label{sec:proccall}

\paragraph{The intuition: ordering a pizza by phone.} First you decide every topping, then you read the toppings out one by one, and only then you say ``please deliver''. A call is the same: the compiler must (1) work out the value of every argument, (2) hand the values over to the callee, and (3) transfer control. Three-address code has one instruction for each of these: the arguments are first computed into temporaries, then each is announced with \texttt{param}, and then one \texttt{call} jumps to the procedure.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{10.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Meaning} \\ \midrule
\texttt{param x} & Put the value of \texttt{x} where the callee will find its next parameter (for example, push it on the stack). \\
\texttt{call p, n} & Call the procedure \texttt{p}, which has \texttt{n} parameters (the last $n$ \texttt{param} instructions). Control returns to the next instruction. \\
\texttt{y = call p, n} & The same, for a procedure that returns a value; the value is stored into \texttt{y}. \\
\texttt{return y} & In the callee: give the value \texttt{y} back to the caller (\texttt{return} alone if there is no value). \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{The grammar.} A call may be a statement (\texttt{call p(a, b);}) or part of an expression (\texttt{y = f(a, b) + 1;}). $A$ is the possibly empty list of arguments and $\mathit{Elist}$ is a non-empty one:
\begin{align*}
S &\to \mathbf{call}\ \mathbf{id}\ \tok{(}\,A\,\tok{)}\ \tok{;}\ \mid\ \mathbf{return}\ E\ \tok{;}\ \mid\ \mathbf{return}\ \tok{;}\\
E &\to \mathbf{id}\ \tok{(}\,A\,\tok{)}\\
A &\to \mathit{Elist}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\\
\mathit{Elist} &\to \mathit{Elist}_1\ \tok{,}\ E\ \mid\ E
\end{align*}

\paragraph{The attributes.} The \textbf{key idea} is that the $\mathit{param}$ instructions can only be written \emph{after} all arguments have been evaluated. So the addresses of the arguments are collected in a \emph{list} that travels upward, and the $\mathit{param}$ instructions are written once, in the rule for the call.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l l p{8.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Attribute} & \textbf{Kind} & \textbf{What it holds, and when it is used} \\ \midrule
\at{E}{addr} & synthesized & The address that holds the value of $E$: a name, a constant, or a temporary. For a call it is a new temporary that receives the result. \\
\at{E}{code} & synthesized & The instructions that compute $E$. \\
\at{Elist}{list}, \at{A}{list} & synthesized & The \emph{addresses} of the arguments, in order: $[\mathit{addr}_1,\mathit{addr}_2,\dots]$. Used in the rule for the call to write the $\mathit{param}$ instructions and to count the arguments. \\
\at{Elist}{code}, \at{A}{code} & synthesized & The code that evaluates \emph{all} arguments, from left to right. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
All the attributes are synthesized, so the SDD is S-attributed and can be evaluated by a post-order traversal of the parse tree. The notation $\mathit{params}(L)$ means $\mathit{gen}(\tok{param}\ a_1)\cc\dots\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{param}\ a_n)$ for the list $L=[a_1,\dots,a_n]$, and $|L|$ is its length.

\begin{defbox}{The SDD for procedure calls}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{9.8cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Production} & \textbf{Semantic rules} \\ \midrule
$S \to \mathbf{call}\ \mathbf{id}\,(A)\,;$ & $\at{S}{code}=\at{A}{code}\cc\mathit{params}(\at{A}{list})\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{call}\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{addr},\ |\at{A}{list}|)$ \\[3pt]
$E \to \mathbf{id}\,(A)$ & $\at{E}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}()$;\newline $\at{E}{code}=\at{A}{code}\cc\mathit{params}(\at{A}{list})\cc\mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \tok{call}\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{addr},\ |\at{A}{list}|)$ \\[3pt]
$S \to \mathbf{return}\ E\,;$ & $\at{S}{code}=\at{E}{code}\cc\mathit{gen}(\tok{return}\ \at{E}{addr})$ \\
$S \to \mathbf{return}\,;$ & $\at{S}{code}=\mathit{gen}(\tok{return})$ \\[3pt]
$A \to \mathit{Elist}$ & $\at{A}{list}=\at{Elist}{list}$; \ $\at{A}{code}=\at{Elist}{code}$ \\
$A \to \varepsilon$ & $\at{A}{list}=[\ ]$; \ $\at{A}{code}=\varepsilon$ \\[3pt]
$\mathit{Elist} \to \mathit{Elist}_1\,,\,E$ & $\at{Elist}{list}=\at{Elist_1}{list}\ \mathbin{+\!\!+}\ [\at{E}{addr}]$ \ (add at the end);\newline $\at{Elist}{code}=\at{Elist_1}{code}\cc\at{E}{code}$ \\
$\mathit{Elist} \to E$ & $\at{Elist}{list}=[\at{E}{addr}]$; \ $\at{Elist}{code}=\at{E}{code}$ \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{defbox}
For the call $E\to\mathbf{id}\,(A)$ the order of the pieces is the whole story: first the code that evaluates the arguments (\at{A}{code}), then the \texttt{param} instructions, then the \texttt{call}.

\paragraph{The SDT.} With actions at the right end (a postfix SDT) and with $\mathit{gen}$ appending instructions at once, the argument code is printed by the expression rules as each argument is parsed, from left to right. Only the list has to be built:
\begin{align*}
\mathit{Elist} &\to E\ \{\ \at{Elist}{list}=[\at{E}{addr}]\ \}\\
\mathit{Elist} &\to \mathit{Elist}_1\ \tok{,}\ E\ \{\ \at{Elist}{list}=\at{Elist_1}{list}\ \mathbin{+\!\!+}\ [\at{E}{addr}]\ \}\\
A &\to \mathit{Elist}\ \{\ \at{A}{list}=\at{Elist}{list}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{A}{list}=[\ ]\ \}\\
E &\to \mathbf{id}\ \tok{(}\,A\,\tok{)}\ \{\ \at{E}{addr}=\mathbf{new}\ \mathit{Temp}();\ \mathbf{for}\ \text{each}\ a\ \text{in}\ \at{A}{list}:\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{param}\ a);\\
 &\qquad\mathit{gen}(\at{E}{addr}\ \tok{=}\ \tok{call}\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{addr},\ |\at{A}{list}|)\ \}\\
S &\to \mathbf{call}\ \mathbf{id}\ \tok{(}\,A\,\tok{)}\ \tok{;}\ \{\ \mathbf{for}\ \text{each}\ a\ \text{in}\ \at{A}{list}:\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{param}\ a);\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{call}\ \at{\mathbf{id}}{addr},\ |\at{A}{list}|)\ \}\\
S &\to \mathbf{return}\ E\ \tok{;}\ \{\ \mathit{gen}(\tok{return}\ \at{E}{addr})\ \}
\end{align*}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 1: \texttt{y = f(a + b, c * 2);}}
Use the assignment rule of Section~\ref{sec:exprtac}, $S\to\mathbf{id}=E\,;$, and the rule $E\to\mathbf{id}\,(A)$. The right column shows the attributes involved.
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{9.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{t1 = a + b} & The first argument. The rule $E\to E_1+E_2$ makes the temporary $t_1$: \at{E}{addr}$=t_1$. Then $\mathit{Elist}\to E$ starts the list: \at{Elist}{list}$=[t_1]$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{t2 = c * 2} & The second argument: \at{E}{addr}$=t_2$. Now $\mathit{Elist}\to\mathit{Elist}_1,E$ appends it: \at{Elist}{list}$=[t_1,t_2]$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{param t1} & The rule $E\to\mathbf{id}(A)$ runs \emph{after} all the arguments are evaluated. \at{A}{list}$=[t_1,t_2]$; $\mathit{params}$ writes the first element. \\[3pt]
\texttt{param t2} & The second element of \at{A}{list}. \\[3pt]
\texttt{t3 = call f, 2} & The new temporary \at{E}{addr}$=t_3$ was created by this rule; $2=|\at{A}{list}|$ is the number of arguments. \\[3pt]
\texttt{y = t3} & The assignment rule copies \at{E}{addr}: $y=t_3$. (A later optimisation merges the last two instructions into \texttt{y = call f, 2}.) \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{examplebox}

\begin{examplebox}{Example 2: a call inside a call, \texttt{z = f(a, g(b));}}
This example shows why the arguments are kept in a \emph{list} that belongs to each call. The parse tree and the lists are in Figure~\ref{fig:nestedcall}.
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\resizebox{0.92\textwidth}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  every node/.style={font=\small},
  nd/.style={inner sep=2pt,align=center},
  em/.style={font=\scriptsize,align=left,text=red!70!black}]
 \node[nd] (E0) at (0,0)     {$E$ \ \texttt{f(A)}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{addr}=t_2$}};
 \node[nd] (A0) at (0,-1.7)  {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{list}=[a,t_1]$}};
 \node[nd] (L0) at (0,-3.4)  {$\mathit{Elist}$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{list}=[a,t_1]$}};
 \node[nd] (L1) at (-3.6,-5.3) {$\mathit{Elist}_1$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{list}=[a]$}};
 \node[nd] (E2) at (3.6,-5.3)  {$E$ \ \texttt{g(A)}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{addr}=t_1$}};
 \node[nd] (E1) at (-3.6,-7.1) {$E$ \ \texttt{a}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{addr}=a$}};
 \node[nd] (A2) at (3.6,-7.1)  {$A$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{list}=[b]$}};
 \node[nd] (L2) at (3.6,-8.8)  {$\mathit{Elist}$\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{list}=[b]$}};
 \node[nd] (E3) at (3.6,-10.5) {$E$ \ \texttt{b}\\[-1pt]{\scriptsize $\mathit{addr}=b$}};
 \foreach \a/\b in {E0/A0,A0/L0,L0/L1,L0/E2,L1/E1,E2/A2,A2/L2,L2/E3} \draw[gray] (\a.south)--(\b.north);
 \node[em,anchor=west] at (5.9,-5.3) {\textbf{emits first} (inner call):\\\texttt{param b}\\\texttt{t1 = call g, 1}};
 \node[em,anchor=west] at (2.6,-0.2) {\textbf{emits last} (outer call):\\\texttt{param a}\\\texttt{param t1}\\\texttt{t2 = call f, 2}};
\end{tikzpicture}}
\caption{Parse tree of \texttt{f(a, g(b))} with the list attributes (the tokens \texttt{f}, \texttt{(}, \texttt{,}, \texttt{)} are not drawn). Each call has its own $A.\mathit{list}$. The inner call finishes first and emits its \texttt{param} and \texttt{call}; the outer call can only write its \texttt{param} instructions when its own list is complete.}
\label{fig:nestedcall}
\end{figure}
\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l p{9.4cm}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Instruction} & \textbf{Key attributes: what they hold, who set them, when they are used} \\ \midrule
\texttt{param b} & Rule $E\to\mathbf{id}(A)$ of the \emph{inner} call \texttt{g(b)}: \at{A}{list}$=[b]$. This happens while the outer argument list is still being built. \\[3pt]
\texttt{t1 = call g, 1} & \at{E}{addr}$=t_1$ is the result of the inner call; \at{Elist}{list} of the outer call becomes $[a,t_1]$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{param a} & Rule $E\to\mathbf{id}(A)$ of the \emph{outer} call: its list $[a,t_1]$ is complete only now. \\[3pt]
\texttt{param t1} & The second element of the outer list: the \emph{value} of \texttt{g(b)}. \\[3pt]
\texttt{t2 = call f, 2} & \at{E}{addr}$=t_2$ of the outer call; $|\at{A}{list}|=2$. \\[3pt]
\texttt{z = t2} & The assignment rule. \\ \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
If the \texttt{param} instructions of \texttt{f} were written as soon as each argument was evaluated, \texttt{param a} would come \emph{before} \texttt{param b} of the inner call, and the parameters of the two calls would be mixed up on the stack.
\end{examplebox}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Why not one global queue for the arguments?}
Many descriptions keep the arguments in a single global queue: $\mathit{Elist}\to E$ starts the queue and $\mathit{Elist}\to\mathit{Elist}_1,E$ appends to it. With the call $f(a,\ g(b))$ the queue of $f$ is $[a]$ when the inner call starts; $\mathit{Elist}\to E$ of $g(b)$ then \emph{resets} the same queue to $[b]$ and destroys the pending $a$. Keeping the list as a \emph{synthesized attribute} of $\mathit{Elist}$ gives every call its own list, exactly as with the switch statement.
\end{pitfallbox}

\begin{notebox}{What the callee does with it}
The notes stop at the three-address code. At run time, \texttt{param} places the values where the procedure expects them (for example on the stack, in an activation record), \texttt{call} saves the return address and jumps, and \texttt{return} stores the value and jumps back. The count \texttt{n} in \texttt{call p, n} tells the machine how many of the last \texttt{param} instructions belong to this call, which is why nested calls need no extra bookkeeping.
\end{notebox}


%====================================================================

\section{A Complete Worked Example}
%====================================================================

\begin{examplebox}{Translating a small program fragment to three-address code}
Source fragment (all variables are \texttt{int}; \texttt{a} is \texttt{int a[10]}, so $w=4$):
\begin{lstlisting}
i = 0;
while (i < n && a[i] != key)
    i = i + 1;
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Using the label-based SDD.} $P \to S$ gives \at{S}{next} $=L_0$. For the sequence $S_1\,S_2$ (the assignment, then the loop) a label $L_1 = \at{S_1}{next}$ is placed between them. For the loop, $\mathit{begin}=L_2$, \at{B}{true} $=L_3$, \at{B}{false} $=L_0$, and the body's \at{S_1}{next} $= L_2$. For $B = B_1 \,\&\&\, B_2$ a new label $L_4 = B_1.\mathit{true}$ is created, with $B_1.\mathit{false}=L_0$, $B_2.\mathit{true}=L_3$, $B_2.\mathit{false}=L_0$.

\begin{lstlisting}
        i = 0
L1:
L2:     if i < n goto L4     // B1 (begin of while)
        goto L0              // B1 false -> exit loop
L4:     t1 = i * 4           // B2: a[i] != key
        t2 = a[t1]
        if t2 != key goto L3
        goto L0              // B false -> exit loop
L3:     t3 = i + 1           // loop body
        i = t3
        goto L2              // back to begin
L0:
\end{lstlisting}
(\texttt{L1} and \texttt{L2} label the same instruction; a later pass can merge them.)

\textbf{Using backpatching} (instruction numbering from 100). Here $M_1.\mathit{instr}=101$ (start of loop), the marker of $\&\&$ gives $103$, and $M_2.\mathit{instr}=107$ (start of body).
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  i = 0
101:  if i < n goto 103         // patched by the && marker
102:  goto _                    // on B.falselist = S.nextlist
103:  t1 = i * 4
104:  t2 = a[t1]
105:  if t2 != key goto 107     // truelist patched with M2 = 107
106:  goto _                    // on B.falselist = S.nextlist
107:  t3 = i + 1
108:  i = t3
109:  goto 101                  // emitted by the while rule (M1)
110:  ...                       // {102, 106} are backpatched with 110
\end{lstlisting}
Both of the incomplete jumps at 102 and 106 form \at{S}{nextlist} of the loop; whoever follows the loop will patch them with the index of the next instruction, here 110.
\end{examplebox}

%====================================================================
\section{Summary and Key Takeaways}
%====================================================================

\begin{itemize}
\item Intermediate code (syntax trees, DAGs, three-address code in quadruple/triple form) decouples front and back ends and enables machine-independent optimisation.
\item A CFG gives syntax (is it a legal program, what is its structure) but not meaning. An \textbf{SDD} attaches \emph{attributes} to grammar symbols and \emph{semantic rules} to productions; \textbf{synthesized} attributes flow upward, \textbf{inherited} attributes flow downward or sideways. \textbf{S-attributed} $\subset$ \textbf{L-attributed}.
\item An \textbf{SDT} embeds the actions in the grammar and fixes the order of evaluation. S-attributed SDDs $\to$ \emph{postfix SDTs} (evaluated in post-order). L-attributed SDDs can be implemented during top-down parsing.
\item Expressions: \at{E}{addr} holds the result; \textit{gen} emits TAC; temporaries are created by \textit{newTemp}. Array references use offsets computed from the widths of sub-arrays.
\item Boolean expressions are translated into \textbf{jumping code}, using the inherited labels \textit{true/false}. Statements use \textit{next}.
\item \textbf{Removing left recursion} (needed for top-down parsing) works for an SDD and for an SDT alike: rewrite the grammar as $A\to X\,R$, $R\to Y\,R\mid\varepsilon$; initialise the inherited $R.i$ in the base case, update it in each repeated group, copy $R.i$ to the synthesized $R.s$ at $\varepsilon$, and copy $R.s$ to the head. For an SDD write these as equations (the result is L-attributed); for an SDT place them as actions, inherited ones just before their symbol and synthesized ones of the head at the end.
\item \textbf{Switch and procedure calls} follow the same pattern. A switch writes the case bodies first and collects (value, label) pairs in a synthesized list that is turned into the tests at the end; a call writes the code of all arguments first, then one \texttt{param} per entry of a synthesized argument list, then \texttt{call p, n}. Each switch and each call has its own list, so nesting works.
\item An L-attributed SDT can be executed during \textbf{top-down parsing} in three ways: (A) recursive descent in which the inherited attributes are arguments and the synthesized attributes are return values; (B) on-the-fly generation, where the pieces of the code attribute are printed instead of being concatenated (possible when they appear in the same order as in the body); (C) a table-driven LL parser that keeps the attributes in action records and synthesize records on its stack.
\item \textbf{Backpatching} replaces inherited labels by synthesized lists (\textit{makelist, merge, backpatch}), allowing one-pass generation with markers $M$ and $N$.
\end{itemize}

\begin{pitfallbox}{Common mistakes (check your own answers against these)}
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item Reading a \emph{value number} as the value of the expression. It is only the index of a record.
\item Forgetting that a DAG shares a node only if \emph{operator and both children} match; $b+a$ and $a+b$ are different unless the operands are put in canonical order.
\item Using $B.true$ where $S.next$ is meant: $S.next$ is where control goes \emph{after} a statement, $B.true$ is where it goes \emph{into} the body.
\item Off-by-one errors in backpatching: $M.instr$ is the index of the \emph{next} instruction to be emitted, not of the last one.
\item Treating array subscripts as indices in the three-address code; they are scaled to byte offsets.
\item Evaluating both operands of $\&\&$ / $||$ in jumping code, which loses short-circuit behaviour.
\end{itemize}
\end{pitfallbox}

%====================================================================
\section{Practice Problems}
%====================================================================

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.}]
\item Translate $x = a * (b + c) - d / (b + c)$ into (a) a syntax tree and DAG, (b) three-address code, (c) quadruples and triples (and the order list that turns the triples into indirect triples).
\item Using the SDD of Table~\ref{tab:expr-sdd}, annotate the parse tree and generate TAC for $a = -(b + c) * (b + c)$.
\item For \texttt{int a[5][4]} (int width $=4$), write the TAC for $x = a[i][j] + a[j][i]$. Give the width used at each dimension.
\item Generate jumping code (with inherited labels) for \texttt{if (a < b \&\& !(c < d)) x = 1; else x = 2;}
\item Show, step by step, the \textit{truelist}/\textit{falselist} and the final code (with backpatching, starting at 100) for $a<b \;||\; c<d \;\&\&\; e<f$.
\item Using backpatching, translate: \texttt{while (x < 10) \{ x = x + 1; if (x == 5) y = 0; \}}
\item Classify as S-attributed, L-attributed, or neither: (a) $A \to B\,C$ with $C.i = B.s$ and $A.s = C.s$; (b) $A \to B\,C$ with $B.i = C.s$; (c) the SDD of Table~\ref{tab:expr-sdd}.
\item Write the SDT for $E \to E_1 * E_2 \mid E_1 + E_2 \mid (E) \mid \mathbf{id}$ that prints the \emph{postfix} form, and show its output for \texttt{a + b * (c + d)}.
\item Build the array of value-number records for $a*b + a*b + c$ and give the three-address code obtained from it.
\item Translate \texttt{switch (n) \{ case 1: s = 1; break; case 5: s = 2; break; default: s = 0; \}} using the scheme of Section~\ref{sec:switch}.
\item Let \texttt{x} be \texttt{float} and \texttt{i, j} be \texttt{int}. Using the widening rules, write the code for $x = i + j * x$.
\item Generate the fall-through jumping code for \texttt{if (a < b || c < d) x = 1;} starting with $B.true=\textit{fall}$.
\item Convert to SSA form: \texttt{x = 1; if (c) x = x + 1; y = x * 2;}
\item Convert to SSA form and draw the blocks: \texttt{k = 1; while (k < 10) k = k * 2; return k;}
\item For $x=(a+b)-(c*d)$, write the triples, then reorder the two sub-expressions. State what must be edited (a) with triples, (b) with indirect triples.
\item For each task say whether a CFG with its parse tree suffices or an SDD is needed: (a) accept \texttt{a = b + c;}, (b) reject the use of an undeclared variable, (c) compute the value of \texttt{3 * 5 + 4}, (d) check that both operands of $+$ are numbers, (e) produce the parse tree of \texttt{a = b + c;}.
\item Using the SDD of Section~\ref{sec:sddtree}, build the syntax tree of \texttt{a - (b + 3) - c}. Name the nodes $p_1,p_2,\dots$ in the order in which they are created, and say which rules create no node.
\item Using the postfix SDT of Section~\ref{sec:infix2postfix}, list the order of the \textit{print} actions for \texttt{(a+b)*c - d/e} and give the postfix output. Then give the output of the right-recursive scheme of Section~\ref{sec:sdt} for the same input.
\item The SDD $B\to B_1\,D\mid D$, \ $\at{B}{val}=2\cdot\at{B_1}{val}+\at{D}{val}$ \ (for $B\to B_1D$), \ $\at{B}{val}=\at{D}{val}$ \ (for $B\to D$), \ $D\to\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{1}$ with $\at{D}{val}=0$ or $1$, computes the value of a binary number. Remove the left recursion and give the result (a) as an SDD and (b) as an SDT. Evaluate it for \texttt{101}.
\item Using the SDD of Section~\ref{sec:typestruct}, annotate the parse tree of \texttt{float [4][5][6]} and give the resulting type expression. Then write the recursive-descent procedure \texttt{C(b)}.
\item For the list-count SDT of Example~3 in Section~\ref{sec:leftrec}, write recursive-descent procedures \texttt{L()} and \texttt{R(i)} in which the inherited attribute is an argument and the synthesized attribute is a return value.
\item Consider $S\to\mathbf{if}\,(\,C\,)\,S_1$ with the inherited attributes $S.next$, $C.true$, $C.false$ and $S_1.next$, where the code is $C.code$, then the label $C.true$, then $S_1.code$, and $C.false=S_1.next=S.next$. Write an SDT that generates the code \emph{on the fly} (with \textit{print}) and the corresponding recursive-descent procedure \texttt{S(next)}. Why is on-the-fly generation possible here?
\item Remove the left recursion from the SDD $T\to T_1*F$ \ ($\at{T}{val}=\at{T_1}{val}\cdot\at{F}{val}$), $T\to T_1/F$ \ ($\at{T}{val}=\at{T_1}{val}\,/\,\at{F}{val}$), $T\to F$ \ ($\at{T}{val}=\at{F}{val}$), $F\to\mathbf{num}$ \ ($\at{F}{val}=\at{\mathbf{num}}{lexval}$). Give the answer (a) as an SDD and (b) as an SDT, and evaluate \texttt{12/3*2}.
\item Using the SDT of Section~\ref{sec:proccall}, translate \texttt{z = f(g(x), y + 1);} and give the list $A.\mathit{list}$ of each call.
\item Using the switch SDT of Section~\ref{sec:switch} with $\at{S}{next}=L_1$, give the code and the list $\mathit{CL}.\mathit{list}$ for \texttt{switch (n) \{ case 3: s = 1; case 7: s = 2; default: s = 0; \}}. Why can the tests not be printed while the cases are being parsed?
\end{enumerate}

\subsection*{Answers to selected problems}
\textbf{Q1(b).} The DAG shares $b+c$, so the TAC is
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = b + c
t2 = a * t1
t3 = d / t1
t4 = t2 - t3
x  = t4
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q3.} Row width $=4\times4=16$; element width $=4$.
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = i * 16
t2 = j * 4
t3 = t1 + t2
t4 = a[t3]
t5 = j * 16
t6 = i * 4
t7 = t5 + t6
t8 = a[t7]
t9 = t4 + t8
x  = t9
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q4.} \at{B}{true}$=L_2$, \at{B}{false}$=L_3$, \at{S}{next}$=L_1$; for $B_1\,\&\&\,B_2$ with $B_2=\,!B_3$: $B_1.\mathit{true}=L_4$, $B_3.\mathit{true}=L_3$, $B_3.\mathit{false}=L_2$.
\begin{lstlisting}
        if a < b goto L4
        goto L3
L4:     if c < d goto L3
        goto L2
L2:     x = 1
        goto L1
L3:     x = 2
L1:
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q7.} (a) L-attributed ($C.i$ depends on the left sibling $B$) but not S-attributed. (b) Neither (inherited attribute of $B$ depends on the right sibling $C$). (c) S-attributed (and hence L-attributed).

\textbf{Q8.} Postfix SDT: $E \to E_1 * E_2\ \{\mathit{print}(\tok{'*'})\}$, $E \to E_1 + E_2\ \{\mathit{print}(\tok{'+'})\}$, $E \to (E)$, $E \to \mathbf{id}\ \{\mathit{print}(\at{\mathbf{id}}{lexeme})\}$ (with precedence/associativity declared or via an unambiguous grammar). Output for \texttt{a + b * (c + d)}: \texttt{a b c d + * +}.

\textbf{Q2.} Unary minus binds tighter than $*$, so the expression is $(-(b+c))*(b+c)$. The SDD (no DAG) computes $b+c$ twice:
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = b + c
t2 = minus t1
t3 = b + c
t4 = t2 * t3
a  = t4
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q5.} $B_1\equiv a<b$ at 100--101, $M_1=102$, $B_3\equiv c<d$ at 102--103, $M_2=104$, $B_4\equiv e<f$ at 104--105. Completing $B_3\,\&\&\,M_2\,B_4$ patches $\{102\}$ with 104 and gives $\mathit{truelist}=\{104\}$, $\mathit{falselist}=\{103,105\}$. Completing $B_1\,||\,M_1\,B_2$ patches $\{101\}$ with 102 and gives $\mathit{truelist}=\{100,104\}$, $\mathit{falselist}=\{103,105\}$.
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  if a < b goto _     <- truelist
101:  goto 102
102:  if c < d goto 104
103:  goto _              <- falselist
104:  if e < f goto _     <- truelist
105:  goto _              <- falselist
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q6.} $M_1=100$; test at 100--101; $M_2=102$; body: \texttt{102: t1 = x + 1}, \texttt{103: x = t1}; inner marker 104; inner test 104--105; inner marker 106; \texttt{106: y = 0}. The inner \textbf{if} has $\mathit{nextlist}=\{105\}$, which the \textbf{while} patches with $M_1=100$.
\begin{lstlisting}
100:  if x < 10 goto 102
101:  goto _              <- S.nextlist (patched later)
102:  t1 = x + 1
103:  x = t1
104:  if x == 5 goto 106
105:  goto 100
106:  y = 0
107:  goto 100
108:  ...                 <- 101 is patched with 108
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q9.} Records: 1 \texttt{id} $a$; 2 \texttt{id} $b$; 3 $(*,1,2)$; (second $a*b$ is found, value number 3); 4 $(+,3,3)$; 5 \texttt{id} $c$; 6 $(+,4,5)$. Code: \texttt{t1 = a * b}, \texttt{t2 = t1 + t1}, \texttt{t3 = t2 + c}.

\textbf{Q10.}
\begin{lstlisting}
        goto test
L1:     s = 1
        goto next
L2:     s = 2
        goto next
L3:     s = 0
        goto next
test:   if n = 1 goto L1
        if n = 5 goto L2
        goto L3
next:
\end{lstlisting}
Here the values 1 and 5 are \emph{sparse}, so the code generator would prefer a binary search or the comparison chain above to a jump table of five entries.

\textbf{Q11.}
\begin{lstlisting}
t1 = (float) j
t2 = t1 * x
t3 = (float) i
t4 = t3 + t2
x  = t4
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q12.} $B.true=\textit{fall}$, $B.false=L_1$; for $B_1\,||\,B_2$: $B_1.true=L_2$ (new), $B_1.false=\textit{fall}$, $B_2.true=\textit{fall}$, $B_2.false=L_1$.
\begin{lstlisting}
        if a < b goto L2
        ifFalse c < d goto L1
L2:     x = 1
L1:
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q13.} The test block branches to the then-block (which defines $x_2$) or directly to the join. At the join the two predecessors are the then-block and the test block, so
\begin{lstlisting}
B0:  x1 = 1
     if c goto B1 else B2
B1:  x2 = x1 + 1               (then-block)
B2:  x3 = phi(x2, x1)          (join: pred. 1 = B1, pred. 2 = B0)
     y1 = x3 * 2
\end{lstlisting}
Note that a $\varphi$ is needed even though there is no \textbf{else}: the ``missing'' branch is itself an edge into the join, carrying $x_1$.

\textbf{Q14.}
\begin{lstlisting}
B0:  k1 = 1
B1:  k2 = phi(k1, k3)          (pred. 1 = B0, pred. 2 = B2)
     if k2 < 10 goto B2 else B3
B2:  k3 = k2 * 2               (back to B1)
B3:  return k2
\end{lstlisting}
Trace: $k_2=1,2,4,8,16$; the test fails for $k_2=16$ and the function returns 16.

\textbf{Q15.} Triples: $0:(+,a,b)$, $1:(*,c,d)$, $2:(-,(0),(1))$, $3:(=,x,(2))$. After evaluating $c*d$ first: (a) with triples the triples swap places, so triple 2 must become $(-,(1),(0))$ and every other reference to either of them must be rewritten; (b) with indirect triples the four triples stay exactly as they are and only the order list changes, from $(0),(1),(2),(3)$ to $(1),(0),(2),(3)$.

\textbf{Q16.} (a) CFG suffices (it is a syntax question). (b) SDD needed: it needs a symbol table and a rule that consults it. (c) SDD needed (an attribute \textit{val}). (d) SDD needed (an attribute \textit{type} and a rule comparing it). (e) CFG suffices: the parse tree is the output of syntax analysis.

\textbf{Q17.} Creation order: $p_1$ = leaf $a$; $p_2$ = leaf $b$; $p_3$ = leaf $3$; $p_4=\mathit{Node}(\tok{'+'},p_2,p_3)$; $p_5=\mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},p_1,p_4)$; $p_6$ = leaf $c$; $p_7=\mathit{Node}(\tok{'-'},p_5,p_6)$. The rules $E\to T$ and $T\to(E)$ create nothing. The root $p_7$ has the children $p_5$ (the node $a-(b+3)$) and $p_6$ ($c$); seven nodes for four operands ($2\cdot4-1=7$).

\textbf{Q18.} The prints occur in the order \texttt{a, b, +, c, *, d, e, /, -}, so the output is \texttt{ab+c*de/-}. The right-recursive scheme prints the same string, because its actions stand right after the operand they follow.

\textbf{Q19.} Grammar: $B\to D\,R$, \ $R\to D\,R\mid\varepsilon$, \ $D\to\mathbf{0}\mid\mathbf{1}$.\\
(a) SDD: $B\to D\,R$: $\at{R}{i}=\at{D}{val}$, $\at{B}{val}=\at{R}{s}$; \ $R\to D\,R_1$: $\at{R_1}{i}=2\cdot\at{R}{i}+\at{D}{val}$, $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$; \ $R\to\varepsilon$: $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$; \ $D$ unchanged.\\
(b) SDT:
\begin{align*}
B &\to D\ \{\ \at{R}{i}=\at{D}{val}\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{B}{val}=\at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to D\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=2\cdot\at{R}{i}+\at{D}{val}\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}\ \}
\end{align*}
For \texttt{101}: $R.i=1$; after the digit 0 the next $R$ gets $2\cdot1+0=2$; after the digit 1 it gets $2\cdot2+1=5$; $R\to\varepsilon$ gives $R.s=5$, so $B.val=5$.

\textbf{Q20.} $B.t=\mathit{float}$ is copied down as $C.b=\mathit{float}$ through three levels; the empty production gives $C.t=\mathit{float}$, and on the way up $C.t$ becomes $\mathit{array}(6,\mathit{float})$, then $\mathit{array}(5,\mathit{array}(6,\mathit{float}))$, and finally $T.t=\mathit{array}(4,\mathit{array}(5,\mathit{array}(6,\mathit{float})))$. The procedure is the one of Example~A1: if the next token is \texttt{[}, read the number $n$, call \texttt{C(b)} recursively and return \texttt{array(n, c1)}; otherwise return \texttt{b}.

\textbf{Q21.}
\begin{lstlisting}
int L()      { match(ID);  return R(1); }            // R.i = 1 ;  L.count = R.s
int R(int i) {
    if (look == ',') { match(',');  match(ID);  return R(i + 1); }
    return i;                                        // R -> epsilon:  R.s = R.i
}
\end{lstlisting}
For \texttt{x, y, z}: \texttt{L()} calls \texttt{R(1)}, then \texttt{R(2)}, then \texttt{R(3)}, which returns 3.

\textbf{Q22.} SDT:
\begin{align*}
S \to \mathbf{if}\ \tok{(}\ &\{\ L_1=\mathit{new}();\ \at{C}{true}=L_1;\ \at{C}{false}=\at{S}{next}\ \}\ C\ \tok{)}\\
&\{\ \mathit{print}(\text{``label''},L_1);\ \at{S_1}{next}=\at{S}{next}\ \}\ S_1
\end{align*}
\begin{lstlisting}
void S(label next) {
    label L1;
    if (look == IF) {
        match(IF);  match('(');
        L1 = new();
        C(next, L1);               // C.false = S.next, C.true = L1 ; C prints its code
        match(')');
        print("label", L1);
        S(next);                   // S1.next = S.next ; S1 prints its code
    }
    else { /* other statement types */ }
}
\end{lstlisting}
On-the-fly generation is possible because $S.code$ is the concatenation of $C.code$, the label, and $S_1.code$, in the same order as $C$, $S_1$ appear in the body. Printing each piece when it becomes available gives the right order.

\textbf{Q23.} Grammar: $T\to F\,R$, \ $R\to *\,F\,R\mid /\,F\,R\mid\varepsilon$.\\
(a) SDD: $T\to F\,R$: $\at{R}{i}=\at{F}{val}$, $\at{T}{val}=\at{R}{s}$; \ $R\to *\,F\,R_1$: $\at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}\cdot\at{F}{val}$, $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$; \ $R\to /\,F\,R_1$: $\at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}\,/\,\at{F}{val}$, $\at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}$; \ $R\to\varepsilon$: $\at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}$; \ $F\to\mathbf{num}$ unchanged.\\
(b) SDT:
\begin{align*}
T &\to F\ \{\ \at{R}{i}=\at{F}{val}\ \}\ R\ \{\ \at{T}{val}=\at{R}{s}\ \}\\
R &\to *\ F\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}\cdot\at{F}{val}\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\\
  &\mid\ /\ F\ \{\ \at{R_1}{i}=\at{R}{i}\,/\,\at{F}{val}\ \}\ R_1\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R_1}{s}\ \}\ \mid\ \varepsilon\ \{\ \at{R}{s}=\at{R}{i}\ \}
\end{align*}
For \texttt{12/3*2}: $R.i=12$; after \texttt{/3} the next $R$ gets $12/3=4$; after \texttt{*2} the next gets $4\cdot2=8$; $R\to\varepsilon$ gives $R.s=8$, so $T.val=8$. The order matters: $R.i\,/\,F.val$ gives $12/3=4$, whereas $F.val\,/\,R.i$ would give $3/12$.

\textbf{Q24.} The inner call \texttt{g(x)} has $A.\mathit{list}=[x]$; the outer call has $A.\mathit{list}=[t_1,t_2]$, where $t_1$ is the value of \texttt{g(x)} and $t_2$ the value of \texttt{y + 1}.
\begin{lstlisting}
param x
t1 = call g, 1
t2 = y + 1
param t1
param t2
t3 = call f, 2
z = t3
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Q25.} Labels: $\mathit{test}=L_2$, case~3 $=L_3$, case~7 $=L_4$, $L_d=L_5$; $\mathit{CL}.\mathit{list}=[(3,L_3),(7,L_4)]$.
\begin{lstlisting}
        goto L2
L3:     s = 1
        goto L1
L4:     s = 2
        goto L1
L5:     s = 0
        goto L1
L2:     if n = 3 goto L3
        if n = 7 goto L4
        goto L5
\end{lstlisting}
The tests must stand \emph{after} all the bodies, and while the cases are parsed the compiler does not yet know how many cases follow (nor, for the last test, the default label). The pairs are therefore collected in the list and printed at the closing brace.
